These results demonstrate that linguistically grounded modifications to transformer self-attention improve the detection of communicative meaning, generalize beyond benchmark datasets to real-world mental-health data, and yield traceable, role-grounded explanations that extend beyond predictive performance to interpretability.

Error Rate is Not Enough: Costs of Augmentation in Low-Resource ASR

Katsumi Ibaraki, David Chiang

cs.CL

Data augmentation is standard practice for low-resource automatic speech recognition (ASR), but its benefits are almost always reported as aggregate error rates, leaving open why and when a method helps. On four low-resource languages and English at five data sizes, we cross two augmentations that modify existing audio (speed perturbation and SpecAugment) with two that modify text transcriptions and synthesize audio from it. We analyze errors at the phoneme level and find that they help for complementary reasons. SpecAugment has the most reliable aggregate improvements, but increases tone and palatalization errors, whereas text-based methods introduce consonant insertions; in combination, each reduces the errors the other introduces. This complementarity is predictable. Tone and palatalization are each signaled by a single acoustic property and occur least frequently in training (lowest training exposure), and text-based methods that flatten word frequency raise their frequency by 37--38%, reversing those errors while further improving aggregate error. The improvement from combining the methods exceeds the sum of their individual improvements; the superadditivity vanishes by 100 hours, limiting the complementarity to the low-resource regime. Where specific error types matter, we recommend selecting augmentation by training exposure and acoustic properties, both measurable before any training.

Can Language Models Learn When to Abstain? Policy Learning and Calibrated Answer Selection

Juan Terven, Diana-Margarita C\'ordova-Esparza, Ruben Gomez, Carlos Miranda Rocha, Julio-Alejandro Romero-Gonz\'alez, Edgar Chavez-Urbiola

cs.CL

A useful language model should know when to answer and when to say "I don't know." Can we improve that decision by training the model itself or by training a separate component to check its answers? We study both approaches on Qwen3-14B for factual questions, using a score that rewards correct answers, penalizes mistakes, and allows abstention. Across three training seeds and two held-out confidence thresholds, a learned answer selector improves threshold-averaged utility over the original model's calibrated confidence filter. On TriviaQA, mean coverage increases from 20.2% to 30.9%, while the pooled error rate among scored answers decreases from 23.0% to 14.5% under exact-match grading. The tested reinforcement-learning changes do not establish consistent gains. On NQ-Open, the selector improves threshold-averaged utility over the filter but still performs worse than always abstaining. A partial human-reviewed audit preserves the TriviaQA finding. Our results support learning to select answers in this setting while showing that reliable abstention across datasets remains unresolved.

Task Context Shapes Component Roles in Language Models

Sovesh Mohapatra, Dani S Bassett

cs.CL, cs.AI

Large language models reuse the same attention heads across tasks, but identifying these shared heads does not explain what they do differently in each task. A natural source of inspiration is task-dependent interactions among shared brain regions, which motivate a network view of language-model computation. Here, we compare fixed model components across tasks using identical graph facts, statements specifying which directed connections are present. We measure how interventions affect attention heads and the activation state in later layers (influence), as well as the answer (importance). Task changes redistribute the influence in five open-weight models spanning four families and 1.5-14 billion parameters. In Gemma-2-9B and Qwen3-14B, an activation edit changes an early internal activation vector and switches which fact the model reads while the written instruction stays fixed. The same heads then respond to different graph facts and influence later components differently. For each prompt, we construct two linear response maps, one before the edit and one after it. Each map predicts a later head's response from a measured change in an earlier head. For a separate factual intervention, the map for the corresponding condition reduces mean squared prediction error by 53.6% in Gemma and 5.0% in Qwen compared with the average of the two maps. These findings show that task context changes both which graph facts the shared components respond to and how their outputs affect later computation. A network account may help explain how models use shared parameters to perform different computations. Measurements of task-dependent influence could also guide interventions intended to alter one behavior while preserving others.

Structural Automata for Neural Semantic Parsing

Xiao Zhang

cs.CL

Neural semantic parsers generate meaning representations token by token, yet well-formedness depends on references, scope, and graph relations that unfold during generation. Constrained decoding prevents locally invalid tokens but does not guarantee that a valid prefix can be completed within a fixed output-length limit. We investigate structural automata at three stages of DRS parsing (decoding, post hoc processing, and training) and additionally evaluate decoding on AMR. For decoding, we introduce accepting-path decoding (APD), which maintains a verified completion within the remaining length limit and follows the model's preferred token whenever that token can also be completed. APD guarantees an accepted output without retracting tokens. Under tight length limits, it improves Smatch++ over local masking by 5.1 and 2.4 points on DRS and by 6.4 and 3.2 points on AMR, comparable to rollback under the same limit, which retracts tokens. For post hoc processing, invalid outputs concentrate on the same inputs: eight unconstrained samples yield no valid output for 10.2% of inputs with limited training data, compared with 0.4% with full data, where filtering comes within 0.2 Smatch++ points of APD. For training, penalizing automaton-rejected tokens improves development Smatch++ by 3.6-11.0 points after 10% of training, but this advantage diminishes as cross-entropy alone reduces the probability assigned to rejected tokens from 14% to below 2% within the first sixth of training with limited data.

When VLMs Destroy What They Know: Spatial Representations Fall Below Lexical Priors

Shubh Garg, Chen Shani

cs.CL

Vision-Language Models (VLMs) increasingly analyze and reason about images, yet it remains unclear whether their internal representations faithfully encode the structured relations required for such reasoning. We introduce EICS (Evaluation-Invariant Concept Stability), a metric for testing whether representations consistently encode concepts under controlled one-concept interventions, and CASP-VL (Causally controlled Spatial Perturbations for Vision-Language models), a benchmark of causally controlled spatial perturbations derived from Visual Genome scene graphs. Across multiple VLM architectures, we find that spatial relations are represented substantially less faithfully than lexical information, even when the same models score well above chance on compositional benchmarks. Further analysis shows that the relation is identifiable at the predicate's own tokens but is lost when the sentence is aggregated into a global representation, whether through the (EOS) token or by mean pooling. These findings reveal a systematic failure invisible to output-based evaluation: VLMs do not preserve, in the representation used downstream, relational information that is present in their own input.

RSI-Anything: Unlocking Recursive Self-Improvement with World Models

Taoyong Cui, Pinxian Zeng, Wanghan Xu, Hao Xiao, Shuhan Xue, Qiang Gao, Wanli Ouyang, Pheng Ann Heng, Zhenfei Yin, Yingcheng Wu, Ling Yang

cs.CL

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) aims to transform interaction experience into persistent improvements, yet external feedback is costly and world-model predictions may become unreliable as policies evolve. We introduce RSI-Anything, a domain-general framework built on JEPA-Anything that uses predictive world models to guide action and learning-data selection. Observed feedback drives policy or workflow improvement and conditional world-model adaptation, while component and compatibility checks govern the retention of updated policy-model pairs. Across diverse computational and scientific domains, RSI-Anything demonstrates substantial gains. In code repair, with the coder fixed, success increases from 26.3% with a static verifier to 29.2% through predictive selection and 31.2% after world-model adaptation, at comparable test usage. Graphical user interface (GUI) evaluations showed higher task completion, while average actions decrease by 39.4%. Across five successive policy versions, laboratory-planning scores more than double from 21.80% to 46.01%. In reaction optimization, RSI-Anything achieves 96.15% mean best observed yield versus 82.16% for Bayesian optimization under the same 40-experiment budget. An end-to-end immune-science application further connects workflow evolution and predictive modeling with therapeutic hypothesis generation. These results demonstrate how predictive world models and retained system updates enable recursive improvement across diverse environments.

CORAL-KV: Compact Cross-Layer Prediction and Residual Coding for Autoregressive Video KV Caches

Albert Ge, Akash Haridas, Utkarsh Saxena, Ruijing Yang, Ian Colbert, Frederic Sala, Vikram Appia, Emad Barsoum

cs.CV, cs.LG

Autoregressive video diffusion models maintain a growing attention key-value (KV) cache, making memory a key bottleneck for long video generation. Recently, clustering-based quantization has emerged as an effective method to compress video KV caches by grouping similar key and value vectors then quantizing residual errors. However, when later frames attend to the compressed history, small reconstruction errors can compound as the video grows. Beyond the within-layer similarity exploited by clustering, we find strong dependencies across cache layers in video models. This motivates Coral-KV - Calibrated Offline, Residual-coded, and Adapted Locally - a KV-cache compression framework that uses cache states in each layer to predict keys and values in subsequent layers, then quantizes the remaining prediction residuals. We construct compact block-diagonal-plus-low-rank (block-DPLR) predictors: block-diagonal components model channel relationships within each attention head, while a global low-rank term models dependencies between heads. Coral-KV exposes a flexible fidelity-memory trade-off: it can reuse its predictors across videos or refit lightweight block-diagonal predictors to the current video for higher fidelity. Across extensive evaluations on both HY-WorldPlay and MAGI-1, our reusable configuration improves PSNR over QVG-4 by 0.32 dB and 4.95 dB, respectively, while using 48% and 37% less cache memory. Against QVG-Pro, our video-adapted configuration improves MAGI-1 PSNR by 3.77 dB while using 33.7% less cache memory. Coral-KV also maintains higher reconstruction fidelity over extended generation and reduces temporal discontinuities while retaining comparable generation latency.

TraceICL: Thread-Resolved Demonstration Curation with Consistency Calibration for Robust Multimodal In-Context Learning

Yigit Yilmaz, Elena Petrova, Mehmet Kaya, Lucia Rossi, Amir Rahman

cs.CV

Interleaved multimodal in-context learning (ICL) lets frozen large vision-language models (LVLMs) acquire new tasks from a handful of image-text-answer demonstrations, yet it remains fragile: demonstrations bind to the wrong images, redundant exemplars crowd out informative ones, and noisy answers are copied verbatim into predictions. We present AVA, a training-free framework that organizes the demonstration pipeline of multimodal ICL into three composable stages. A Thread-Resolved Decomposer (TRD) segments the interleaved context into per-sample threads and re-stitches them with explicit boundaries, restoring image-text binding. An Influence-scored Demonstration Selector (IDS) estimates each candidate's marginal influence with one extra forward pass, removes redundancy, and orders the survivors by descending value. A Weak-to-Strong Consistency Calibration (WSC) module scores each demonstration by a weak sibling model's reconstruction confidence and softly reweights its contribution during decoding. On five vision-language benchmarks with interleaved 4-shot prompts, AVA improves the strongest same-setting baseline by +1.7 points on average, with gains of up to +2.4 points on VizWiz, shrinks the require-visual performance gap on ScienceQA-IMG, and retains most of its gain under 30% noisy demonstrations, at a 26% latency overhead that an offline cache amortizes. No gradients are ever computed; the LVLM stays frozen.

TerraAgent: Demonstration-Grounded Multi-Agent Reasoning for Long-Context Remote Sensing Visual Question Answering

Jingwen Huang, Yiming Fang, Hanxiao Wu, Boyuan He, Keyi Luo

cs.CV

Remote sensing visual question answering (RS-VQA) requires answers that truly depend on image evidence, yet current remote sensing multimodal large language models (RS MLLMs) are still evaluated mainly by accuracy, which cannot reveal whether a correct answer was produced without using the image. This setting introduces three challenges: demonstrations retrieved across different ground sample distances (GSD), sensors, and city textures may be poorly matched; real queries often involve long visual contexts from multi-scale tiles, multi-temporal frames, and retrieved knowledge, where evidence can be diluted and errors can propagate through multi-step reasoning; and prior-only answering remains invisible to standard metrics. We present AVA, a training-free framework that wraps a frozen RS MLLM with three modules. GDR performs GSD-bucketed, scene-graph-based demonstration retrieval and filters demonstrations whose answers are unsupported by their images. MACE decomposes each query into an evidence-typed sub-question DAG, routes sub-questions to suitable perception tools, and records verified provenance in a shared evidence ledger with bounded re-perception. CVA audits the final answer through counterfactual perturbations of cited evidence, including ablation and swap, and computes a visual-dependency score for confidence calibration and evidence-forcing re-query. On RSVQA-LR/HR, VRSBench, and LHRS-Bench, AVA improves average accuracy by up to 2.40 points over the strongest same-setting baselines, raises the visual-dependency score from 0.28 to 0.49, reduces the prior-only answer rate from 46.2 percent to 21.7 percent, and lowers the hallucination rate from 17.1 percent to 9.8 percent, with 2.6x the cost of a single backbone forward pass and no fine-tuning.

MIRAGE: Metric Inference and Reliability-Aware Gaussian Estimation from Feed-Forward Vision

Xinzhe Wang, Changjian Jiang, Kaiwen Song, Xudong Li, Kerui Ren, Ran Yi, Lizhuang Ma, Chunhua Shen, Linning Xu, Tao Lu, Mulin Yu

cs.CV

Feed-forward 3D priors enable high-quality reconstruction from image streams but often lack reliable metric scale and suffer from long-term trajectory drift. In contrast, LiDAR--inertial estimation provides metrically accurate motion but lacks the image-space consistency needed for high-fidelity reconstruction. We propose $MIRAGE$, which anchors feed-forward visual geometry to LiDAR--IMU metric motion and regulates online pose corrections according to correspondence support and cross-modal agreement, jointly enabling accurate metric pose estimation and high-fidelity Gaussian reconstruction. Experiments on Oxford Spires, M2DGR and INS show that MIRAGE achieves accurate metric trajectory estimation, outperforming COLMAP, a widely used but computationally expensive reference for vision-only benchmarks. Motivated by the scarcity of long-horizon monocular benchmarks for large-scale indoor reconstruction, we further build a multimodal data-collection platform and use MIRAGE to establish accurate 6-DoF reference trajectories for a new vision-only benchmark. We benchmark recent monocular large-scale reconstruction methods on this dataset, providing a systematic evaluation of their performance in long-horizon indoor scenes.

Scaling Multi-Teacher Distillation for Digital Pathology

Sofi\`ene Boutaj, Pierre Marza, Varun Belagali, Dimitris Samaras, Maria Vakalopoulou, Stergios Christodoulidis

cs.CV, https://sofiene-boutaj.github.io/omni-projectpage/

Multi-teacher distillation allows transferring knowledge from multiple "teacher" networks to a single "student" network. This method is promising in fields such as digital pathology, where many powerful foundation models were proposed recently. However, as we show in this paper, the standard multi-teacher distillation approach reacts poorly to an increase in the number of teachers used to train a student encoder. We demonstrate that learning teacher-specific representations is a key to scaling in MTD. Importantly, different design choices, such as learnable teacher tokens, a tailored attention scheme, additional mixture-of-experts layers and a contrastive loss, are proposed to better learn such teacher-specific representations. We show that our method better scales with respect to the number of considered teachers, allowing us to train compact student encoders, up to 10x more efficient than larger teacher foundation models, while matching their performance and even outperforming them on a large set of tile-level and slide-level tasks. We conduct a thorough empirical validation, evaluating more than 10 foundation models on 39 tasks spanning tile and slide levels. As a result, we release a new collection of strong and efficient foundation models, named OMNI, trained from the knowledge of 10 state-of-the-art foundation models. We finally conduct an analysis of the learned teacher-specific representations, highlighting their complementarity and explaining why they can be easily aggregated at downstream time.

Mind the Temporal Gap: Evaluating Video Models for Temporal Understanding in Driving

Shiva Sreeram, Erfan Aasi, Tsun-Hsuan Wang, Alaa Maalouf, and Daniela Rus

cs.CV, cs.RO

Modern video-language models are increasingly treated as general-purpose video reasoners, yet it remains unclear whether they robustly capture temporal dynamics in safety-critical settings such as driving. We study this question through controlled ego-motion tasks that require identifying actions from driving videos. Across video-language models, we find that standard prompting, visual prompting, few-shot prompting, and chain-of-thought prompting remain unreliable and highly task- and model-dependent, with persistent class biases, although newer models show substantial task-specific few-shot gains. In contrast, lightweight LoRA finetuning and simple probes trained on intermediate visual features recover strong motion-discriminative performance, indicating that motion-relevant cues are often present inside the models but are not reliably used by the final video-to-language pathway. These results suggest that, in driving, current video-language models remain unreliable temporal reasoners and often behave more like frame-level, bias-driven recognizers than dependable temporal world models.

MemReg: Streaming Outdoor LiDAR Point Cloud Registration with Hybrid Memory Buffers

Jiuming Liu, Mengmeng Liu, Guangming Wang, Lihao Liu, Michael Ying Yang, Yunpeng Zhang, Francesco Nex, Hao Cheng, Hesheng Wang

cs.CV

Prior outdoor LiDAR registration works are commonly limited by the pair-wise input paradigm, which neglects temporal correlations inherent within streaming sequences. In this paper, we propose a novel multi-frame outdoor point cloud registration network for streaming LiDAR scans enhanced with pose-related memory buffers. The key observation is that long-term temporal LiDAR sequences can provide rich global contextual information to complete sparse measurements, filter outliers, and address low-overlap problems, thereby boosting registration performance. Specifically, two hybrid memory buffers are designed, including an implicit memory feature buffer and an explicit memory pose buffer, to store and dynamically update pose-related temporal features. Moreover, a novel dynamic history weighting module is developed to adaptively fuse current and history pose-related features. Extensive experiments on three outdoor datasets, including KITTI, nuScenes, and Apollo-Southbay, demonstrate state-of-the-art performance of MemReg, surpassing all previous pair-wise methods and also multi-frame SLAM systems. Our method also generalizes surprisingly well to multiview indoor registration scenarios with rather competitive performance on 3DMatch, 3DLoMatch, and ScanNet. Code will be released at https://github.com/IRMVLab/MemReg.

ForcingBooster: Adaptive Patch-wise Denoising for Fast Autoregressive Video Diffusion

Zekai Gu, Peng Wang, Jiahao Lu, Yongsen Mao, Cheng Lin, Yuan Liu

cs.CV

Autoregressive video diffusion models have become a leading approach to real-time interactive video generation and video world modeling. However, existing methods typically allocate computation uniformly across space when generating the next video chunk, updating all patches with the same denoising schedule and number of function evaluations. This ignores the spatial heterogeneity of video content: static, simple, or recurring regions are often easier to denoise, while other regions require further refinement or additional context. A uniform budget thus leads to redundant computation on easy patches, whereas reducing the fixed step count can leave difficult patches insufficiently refined. We introduce \methodname{}, a general framework that dynamically allocates denoising steps to individual patches: easy patches stop early to improve efficiency, while difficult patches receive more refinement steps. This design accelerates generation with negligible quality degradation. Our method is compatible with most existing few-step autoregressive diffusion models, requiring only a lightweight routing module with modest training. Experiments demonstrate that \methodname{} reduces the total number of denoising steps applied across video patches by about 50\% while maintaining visual quality comparable to the four-step baseline.

Feature Competition and Its Representational Origins in Vision Language Models: Evidence from the Stroop Paradigm

Nurb\"uke Teker, Rui Xiao, Zeynep Akata, Shuchen Wu

cs.CV

When you ask a vision-language model (VLM) a question with an image, it answers your question based on the image. But to act means to choose: saying one thing means choosing to respond not say another. This becomes an issue when the image contains multiple features that equally relate to the question at hand: how would VLMs choose to respond? This problem is precisely studied by the classical Stroop task in psychology: among a set of deliberately designed images whose text and color conflicts (e.g., \textcolor{blue}{RED}), participants are instructed to report the color. We adapt this psychological paradigm to a benchmark and prompted a range of contrastive and generative VLMs on their behavior. Across models, we observe a persistent bias toward the feature of text in Stroop images: when text and color disagree, models preferentially generate responses centered to the text rather than color. We then opened the black box and examined the source of this bias and why certain features influences the model's behavior more than others. In the embedding space, we found that textual cues are represented more strongly and separably than color cues. This representational asymmetry predicts intervention success: steering models toward text-related directions is substantially easier than steering them toward color-related directions. Using a classical psychological paradigm, we demonstrate, at both behavioral and representational levels, that distinct features within the same image can exert unequal influence on VLM behavior. Our findings suggest that VLMs assign different degrees of saliency to different features within the same image, highlighting the need to understand how cue-specific saliency shapes their performance in widely used downstream applications.

WoodBR-Cross: A public wood-image dataset and individual-tree-disjoint benchmark for species identification

Daniel Casanova, Alexandre Bahia Gontijo, Arnaldo Candido Junior, Silvana Nisgoski, and Pedro Luiz de Paula Filho

cs.CV

Wood-image benchmarks can yield optimistic estimates when images from the same tree occur in both training and test sets. WoodBR-Cross contains 4,875 transverse-surface images in 38 operational taxonomic classes, linked to 303 distinct trees identified by numeric catalogue witness codes (codigos-testemunho) in the Brazilian Forest Service collection. A species-level subset comprises 3,406 images, 20 classes and 214 trees. Three tasks compare duplicate-aware image partitions for all classes (A), image partitions for the species subset (B1), and individual-tree-disjoint partitions for that subset (B2). In B2, all images from each tree are kept together, so no tree is shared across training, validation or test within a fold. ResNet-50, ConvNeXt-Tiny and ViT-B/16 were evaluated with three outer folds, internal validation and five training seeds. Handcrafted and frozen-feature baselines complement border-only and randomized-label controls. Validation-selected ConvNeXt-Tiny achieved B2 tree-level macro-F1 of 0.8621 +/- 0.0317; frozen DINOv2 features yielded 0.9000 +/- 0.0722. Values are means and standard deviations across three seed-averaged fold means. Results estimate performance on previously unseen trees from this collection.

Bridging Human and Model Attention: Explainability Analysis of CNN, Mamba, and ViT Architectures with Gaze-Based Validation

Marco Stefani, Edoardo Parpaiola, Matteo Bino, Gianluca Caregnato, Francesco Roncolato, Daniel Fusaro, Loris Nanni, Alberto Pretto

cs.CV, cs.HC, cs.LG

High-risk applications of deep learning require precise characterization of model decision-making. We conduct a systematic explainability evaluation across Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), Vision Transformers (ViTs), and Vision Mamba (ViM). Unlike predominantly qualitative studies, we quantify the alignment between model attention and human visual perception using eye-tracking ground truth. We formalize this comparison via a standardized normalization pipeline mapping post-hoc attribution maps and gaze heatmaps onto spatial probability distributions. Alignment is evaluated across fine-grained classification, medical imaging, and emotion recognition using architecture-specific XAI methods. We measure fidelity using Jensen-Shannon similarity, Chi-square similarity, and Pearson correlation, three complementary estimators capturing support overlap, mass discrepancy, and structural co-variation. Our results reveal distinct attention dynamics: CNNs tend to exhibit a more global focus, with spatially more diffuse saliency maps that correlate well with human perception in general contexts, while ViTs demonstrate fine-grained, patch-level attention, often focused on discriminative microfeatures. The Mamba architecture shows biased behavior: it achieves strong explainability in medical tasks but exhibits divergent intensity distributions in natural images; our analytical characterization of the metrics makes this dissociation explicit, since near-unit support overlap is attained simultaneously with a vanishing linear correlation. We further analyze the effect of backbone freezing and ensemble strategies on model interpretability. The results highlight how architectural and training choices influence the alignment between model saliency and human visual attention.

Zebra3D: Efficient Hybrid Foundation Models For Scalable 3D Reconstruction

Utkarsh Saxena, Ruijing Yang, Mehdi Rezagholizadeh, Akash Haridas, Vikram Appia, Emad Barsoum

cs.CV, cs.LG

Feed-forward 3D foundation models reconstruct scenes from unposed images, but the quadratic cost of global attention limits their scalability to large view sets. We present Zebra3D, a layerwise sparse-linear hybrid attention framework that improves the efficiency of pretrained 3D foundation models without training from scratch. Across VGGT, HunyuanWorld-Mirror, and $\pi^3$, we observe a broadly consistent depth-wise pattern: early stages are relatively tolerant to linear replacement, while sensitive middle-to-late stages tend to exhibit more concentrated attention. Zebra3D exploits this structure by assigning linear-centered attention to tolerant stages and tile-sparse attention to sensitive stages, using landmark summaries to preserve unselected context. Closed-form affine fitting enables training-free conversion, while optional post-conversion training combines task supervision with output and relational feature distillation. Across all three backbones, Zebra3D achieves strong reconstruction and camera-pose accuracy at high attention sparsity, with training-free variants remaining close to their dense counterparts. End-to-end speedups increase with input view count, reaching up to $7.1\times$ at 1,024 views. After post-conversion training, Zebra3D-VGGT reaches 80.5\% RealEstate10K AUC@30 at 94\% mean attention sparsity, within 1.1 percentage points of dense VGGT fine-tuned on the same data and schedule.

Reference-Free Food Volume Estimation from Egocentric Stereo Video

Vivan Kumar, Gautham Vinod, Fengqing Zhu

cs.CV

Current stereo vision-based approaches for food portion estimation are hindered by the user burden of using a mobile phone to capture multiple images or placing a reference object in the scene for scale calibration. As a step towards passive dietary intake monitoring, we introduce a method that uses stereo video from a wearable egocentric device to reconstruct food items in true physical scale to accurately estimate volume. By utilizing FoundationStereo for zero-shot stereo matching, SAM 2 for food segmentation, and TSDF fusion over a set of keyframes, our method yields a visually and metrically accurate 3D mesh of any plated food item from a single stereo recording. We leverage the unique offerings of wearables, such as a factory-calibrated stereo baseline and camera poses estimated on-device via visual-inertial odometry (VIO), to perform fully automatic reconstruction without a scale reference object in the scene. We show strong initial volume estimation results across five recorded food items, achieving a mean absolute percentage error of 9.1%.

Shift-Aware Identity-Guided Latent Refinement for Referring Audio--Visual Segmentation

Kun Li, Sami Sebastian Brandt, Michael Ying Yang

cs.CV

Referring Audio--Visual Segmentation (Ref-AVS) is an emergent multimodal task requiring the precise identification and segmentation of a specific object based on a natural language expression grounded in both visual and auditory cues. Unlike traditional AVS, which segments generic sound sources, or referring video object segmentation (Ref-VOS), which relies solely on visual--textual grounding, Ref-AVS necessitates fine-grained tri-modal interaction across vision, audio, and language. However, existing methods often fail to maintain consistency when subjected to target shift, a phenomenon where the model erroneously transfers its focus between objects due to transient occlusions, acoustic fading, or identity ambiguities. To address this, we propose a novel Shift-Aware Identity-guided Latent (SAIL) refinement framework. It introduces a dual-token strategy that decouples frame-level dynamics from a global semantic anchor to explicitly model target-shift cues. We design a shift-aware refinement module that rectifies drift by aligning tokens with domain-specific evidence. Finally, we leverage a decoder that integrates the refined embeddings with a continuous memory update to maintain spatio-temporal coherence and identity consistency throughout the video sequence. Extensive experiments conducted on two Ref-AVS benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed method, significantly outperforming existing AVS, Ref-VOS, and Ref-AVS approaches. The code will be released after publication.

Domain Adaptation of SAM 2.1 for Building-Component Segmentation in Image-Based 3D Reconstruction

Ehsan Agha Ebrahimi, Yong-Cheol Lee, Keith G. Mills

cs.CV

3D reconstruction of building components using image-based scanning requires appropriate segmentation of the objects in the 2D building images. In order to benefit from consistent grouping of the 2D masks in 3D environments, vision foundation models such as the Segment Anything Model have been utilized by Group Anything with Radiance Fields (GARField) to perform 3D segmentation. This study fine-tuned SAM 2.1 on the HBD dataset, consisting of 2,679 annotated images from 42 buildings, to enhance multi-class segmentation of 2D building images, covering 11 classes (Wall, Window, Ceiling, Door, Column, Beam, Floor, Opening, Curtain Wall, Roof, and Lift). The model was evaluated using per-class IoU, Dice, and mIoU metrics, and the results show an improvement in mIoU from 49.9% to 69.6%, indicating a meaningful improvement in class segmentation and providing a more accurate model for distilling the masks in 3D. SegFormer was then used as a baseline, generating per-class masks for comparison with SAM 2.1, which indicates improvement in some object classes with larger surface area. The masks in the building images were grouped in the neural radiance field, and the pretrained SAM 2.1 was compared with the fine-tuned SAM 2.1 in the newly rendered images. This comparison shows that fine-tuning improves the grouping of building components at a user-selected grouping scale. This advances the application of Group Anything with Radiance Fields (GARField) in 3D, enabling better segmentation of building structures for 3D auto-reconstruction.

Spend Where It Matters: Particle Search with MH Refinement for Budget-Constrained Audio-Visual Alignment

Shivanshu Shekhar, Raghavendra Addanki, Tong Zhang, Uttaran Bhattacharya

cs.CV, cs.SD, eess.AS

Reward-guided inference-time alignment can improve joint audio-visual generation, but optimizing multiple alignment objectives requires many expensive model evaluations, making existing methods costly under limited compute. Particle-based search enables global exploration but incurs estimation error with a finite population, while Metropolis-Hastings (MH) can refine candidates locally but benefits from a strong initialization. We introduce relative gains across seven metrics of $30.7\%$ over BoN and $14.1\%$ over EvoSearch with JavisDiT++, and $9.2\%$ over BoN and $17.0\%$ over EvoSearch with LTX-2. Beyond joint audio-visual generation, we also demonstrate the benefits of adaptive particle allocation on SD1.5 image generation, improving AestheticScore by $2.2\%$ and ImageReward by $6.0\%$ over uniform allocation.

Structure-Aware Matching for Robust UAV Thermal-to-Satellite Geo-Localization

Chun Liu, Yuxin Wang, Zhenxiang Huo, Linlin Zhu, Tao Xu, Henggang Han

cs.CV

UAV thermal-to-satellite geo-localization localizes UAVs by matching real-time thermal images against georeferenced satellite images, enabling autonomous navigation in GNSS-denied and low-visibility conditions. However, substantial cross-modal disparities in color, brightness and viewpoint hinder conventional appearance-based methods from establishing reliable correspondences. While existing approaches predominantly address global modality shifts, they overlook the invariant scene structures inherently shared between thermal and satellite views. To bridge this gap, we propose SAMF, a Structure-Aware Matching Framework that explicitly exploits stable cross-modal structural cues to improve UAV thermal geolocalization. Specifically, the Directional Structure Enhancement Module (DSEM) strengthens robust structural representations by capturing directional and low-frequency spatial patterns, and the Modality-Aware Affine Remapping Module (MARM) subsequently applies affine correction to these enhanced features, further promoting cross-modal alignment. Additionally, we introduce the Thermal-UAV-Paired benchmark derived from the Thermal-UAV dataset. Extensive experiments on both the Boson-nighttime and Thermal-UAV-Paired datasets show that SAMF achieves state-of-the-art performance. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness and synergistic complementarity of DSEM and MARM. The code and dataset are released at: https://github.com/YuxinWang879/SAMF.git.

APEX: Asynchronous Prefix Denoising for Extreme Compression

David Dinkevich, Nisan Chiprut, Yoav HaCohen, Dani Lischinski

cs.CV, cs.LG

Fewer latent tokens promise cheaper diffusion. Yet aggressive latent compression creates a reconstruction-generation dilemma: adding channels improves reconstruction but makes generation harder. We introduce APEX, short for Asynchronous Prefix denoising for EXtreme compression, which pairs a hierarchical latent space with asynchronous denoising over channel groups. Our autoencoder orders channel prefixes from structure to detail, while one shared denoiser generates them at staggered local times without adding tokens. On ImageNet-512, APEX outperforms existing methods at every evaluated channel count under 64x and 128x compression. At matched training and guided sampling compute, it also outperforms a 256-token model at every evaluated budget, with larger gains at lower compute. Code is available at https://daviddinkevich.github.io/APEX.

CaptchaAgent: Verifiable Visual Reasoning for CAPTCHA Solving

Shangquan Sun, Ruoyu Chen, Zhirui Liu, Xiang Liu, Chenghao Qian, Wenqi Ren, Xiaochun Cao

cs.CV, material, included

Modern reasoning CAPTCHAs require fine-grained perception, compositional relation reasoning, and grounded browser interaction. End-to-end vision-language model agents remain brittle in this setting because a missed object, incorrect count, or confused relation can propagate directly to the final action. To address this problem, we present CaptchaAgent, a structured visual reasoning framework for interactive CAPTCHA solving. Instead of predicting an action directly from a screenshot, CaptchaAgent first constructs a visual intermediate representation (V-IR) that explicitly records entities, attributes, and spatial or topological relations. A symbol-only ASCII protocol further requires the parser to commit to a concrete object set and count. Building on this global representation, localized evidence refinement revisits ambiguous regions at higher effective resolution, while counterfactual consistency verification tests whether the resulting action remains stable under task-preserving transformations. Across three proprietary backbones on Open CaptchaWorld, CaptchaAgent improves Pass@1 by 21.3-25.8 percentage points over a commercially available browser agent baseline evaluated with the same backbones. On the separate offline Oedipus-data benchmark, it further improves average Pass@1 by 9.2-12.3 percentage points over the Oedipus solver across three matched backbones. The intermediate traces show that exact counting, continuous geometry, and ordered execution remain major sources of automation difficulty for the evaluated agents, providing practical guidance for designing more resilient compositional CAPTCHAs.

Selective Spatio-Temporal Fruit Inspector (S$^2$FI): A Mamba-based Framework for Rotational Fruit Quality Assessment

Hao Tian, Jie Hu, Guoqi Shan, Fan Wang, Wenzhuo Zhang, Youshan Zhang

cs.CV

The global fruit and vegetable industry confronts severe post-harvest sorting challenges for small and medium-scale growers. Large intelligent sorting systems are too costly, while manual sorting suffers from low efficiency, inconsistent standards, and high labor demand. To bridge this gap between expensive equipment and inefficient manual work, this study develops a low-cost, high-efficiency, lightweight fruit sorting system driven by deep learning, suitable for small and medium growers. We propose the Selective Spatio-Temporal Fruit Inspector (S$^2$FI) architecture, which combines a 3D convolutional encoder with a Mamba-based backbone.It efficiently extracts spatiotemporal features, adaptively weighs informative frames during fruit rotation, and models long-range dependencies with linear complexity, reducing motion blur and occlusion. The hardware uses a dual-conveyor and curved rail design, supporting household power, fast deployment, and lightweight embedded deployment to improve affordability. We also built a conveyor-based fruit quality video dataset with 6,460 samples. Experiments on the held-out test set show that our method achieves the best trade-off among accuracy, efficiency, and practicality, outperforming the compared baselines on the test set, achieving an excellent balance of accuracy, speed, and practicality for real-world sorting.

ROSETTA: Rotation Subspace Estimation for Training-free Transmodal Alignment

Alex Junho Lee

cs.CV, Transactions, on, Image, Processing, on, March, 1,, 2026

When a query must be matched against a database built with a different sensor or processed by a different encoder, the independently trained encoders impose distinct coordinate systems on the feature space. No canonical reference frame exists to which all encoders can be aligned, and no single training procedure has fully closed this gap. We show that the relative transformation between any two such systems is a spatially preserving rotation in whitened feature space, whose angular velocity exhibits white-noise characteristics: large in norm, temporally uncorrelated, and unpredictable from the features themselves. However, the cross-covariance between encoder outputs has low effective rank, constraining only a small fraction of the rotation parameters, therefore local alignment is tractable. ROSETTA is a training-free calibration layer that estimates the local rotation via an expectation-maximization procedure, requiring no coobservation at query time. We validate ROSETTA on three public driving datasets spanning LiDAR, camera, thermal, and event modalities in cross-modal, cross-device, cross-representation, and cross-backbone settings, with milliseconds overhead per query.

Win-Win Fusion: Multi-Modal Image Fusion with Visual-Semantic Collaboration

Hao Zhang, Xuhui Zuo, Jiayi Ma

cs.CV

Existing multi-modal image fusion (MMIF) methods generally face an optimization gap between the visual and semantic domains, limiting the enhancement of perception capabilities. To overcome this long-standing barrier, this work proposes W2F, a new visual-semantic mutual reinforcing paradigm for MMIF. First, we reinforce the foundation for visual-semantic collaboration by amplifying feature complementarity through two targeted designs. Specifically, the segmentation-guided fusion loss introduces object-aware structural guidance into visual fusion, while the embedded information restoration prior strengthens the ability of visual fusion to provide reliable perceptual evidence for the semantic branch under degraded conditions. Then, we introduce a strong multi-modal information interaction mechanism composed of interactive gated mixed attention (IGM-Att) and progressive coupling attention (PC-Att). These modules ensure comprehensive scene representation from both local and global perspectives, providing informational support for visual-semantic collaboration. Finally, we construct a collaborative mechanism to couple visual fusion and semantic fusion. It employs a feature search module to extract complementary key features from both tasks, enriching the representational potential of visual and semantic information. Meanwhile, a gradient correction strategy ensures collaboration from the optimization perspective, enhancing performance for both visual and semantic fusion through consistency learning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that W2F surpasses state-of-the-art methods in both visual appeal and decision accuracy. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/HaoZhang1018/W2F.

MeshBind: Point-to-Mesh Binding for Non-Rigid Scene Flow Automatic Annotation

Yinqi Chen, E Li, Shuaijun Wang, Weiyuan Peng, Meiying Zhang, and Qi Hao

cs.CV

Scene flow estimation is essential for many autonomous driving (AD) and robotic applications, but obtaining ground-truth scene flow for non-rigid objects such as pedestrians is highly difficult. We present MeshBind, an automatic annotation method that uses mesh models to generate per-point scene flow for non-rigid objects. The novelty of this work is threefold: 1) developing a multi-view module that reconstructs an articulated mesh model from six surround cameras and registers it to the LiDAR frame; 2) developing a local-frame binding technique that attaches each point to the mesh via a per-triangle tangent-normal frame, invariant under rigid motion yet adaptive to articulated deformation; 3) developing a three-layer flow registration pipeline that combines ego-vehicle motion compensation, box binding, and mesh binding to produce full-scene per-point flow. We implement MeshBind in SF-Studio, an interactive annotation tool that visualises flow and allows human mesh corrections with automatic recomputation. We also release SFB (SUScape-Flow Benchmark), a large-scale benchmark built on the public SUScape dataset, covering 41k frames with per-point non-rigid pedestrian annotations and a Rigid/Semi-Rigid/Non-Rigid evaluation protocol. Experiments show that fine-tuning on SFB reduces three-way endpoint error (EPE) by 2-3x, and brings non-rigid EPE to the same level as rigid EPE.

AD-SRF: Attack-Defense Semantic Reinforcement for Multi-Modal Image Fusion

Hao Zhang, Zhiqian Zheng, Linfeng Tang, Xinyu Xiang, Jiayi Ma

cs.CV

Existing image fusion methods struggle to balance visual quality and semantic attributes due to the inherent conflict between their optimization objectives. To address this challenge, we propose an attack-defense semantic reinforcement framework for multi-modal image fusion, termed AD-SRF. By decoupling the optimization of these two aspects and then losslessly reintegrating them, this framework injects rich semantic attributes into the fused image without compromising its visual quality. First, we develop a transformer-based restorative visual fusion network as a strong visual baseline. Equipped with the cross-modal retrieval module and the information restoration-fusion collaborative loss, it produces highly visible fused images even under degraded conditions. Second, we introduce an attack-defense game mechanism to strengthen the semantic attributes of the fused images. The attacker learns perturbation patterns that impair downstream segmentation, whereas the defender restores missing semantics from the original inputs and reintegrates them into the fused image through an imperceptible semantic map. Through iterative adversarial interaction, this mechanism drives the defender to continually strengthen the fused image's semantic attributes, boosting downstream segmentation performance without compromising visual quality. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AD-SRF achieves a win-win performance in both visual quality and semantic attributes. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/HaoZhang1018/AD-SRF.

A Lightweight Velocity Correction Framework for Improving Instruction Following in Few-Step Text-to-Image Generation

Yating Gao, Zhenlong Zhu, Wen Zihao, Lenan Zhang, Jiexiong Wang, Yang Yang, Guanjun Jiang, Yixiong Zou, Takuya Maekawa

cs.CV

Few-step text-to-image generators produce high-quality images with few network forward passes, yet struggle with complex prompts involving multiple subjects, interactions, spatial relations, counting, and attribute binding. Can these errors be reduced without changing the generator's parameters or increasing its forward-pass count? We propose a lightweight correction framework for a frozen 6B-parameter generator. The generator predicts velocities that determine how its intermediate image representation is updated; a corrector with fewer than 20M parameters learns residual adjustments at selected sampling steps. Because earlier corrections change the states encountered at later steps, our On-Trajectory Flow-Bridge Supervision derives residual targets at the states actually reached during corrected generation. A single corrector is shared across selected steps and runs in parallel with the generator. Only the corrector is trained, requiring neither reward optimization nor re-distillation. To our knowledge, this is the first framework for lightweight velocity-residual adaptation of fully frozen few-step text-to-image generators that preserves their original sampling schedule and forward-pass count. On our target complex-scene distribution, the framework achieves a +17.60-percentage-point NetWin over frozen Z-Image-Turbo in pairwise instruction-following evaluation, with only 26.9 ms (0.84%) additional end-to-end latency per image and no increase in backbone evaluations.

JMT-YOLO: Multi-task Domain Adaptation via Segmentation-Guided Consistency Learning

Zhenhua Yu, Jiahao Zhang, Wenchao Zhang, Ou Ye, Hengxiang Zhao

cs.CV

Domain adaptive object detection (DAOD) aims to reduce performance degradation due to distribution shifts between training (source) and testing (target) datasets. However, owing to insufficient modeling of fine-grained features and limited instance-level alignment, existing approaches become less effective under complex environmental conditions, such as extreme weather conditions and object occlusion. We overcome these challenges by proposing JMT-YOLO, a novel joint multi-task learning framework for DAOD. JMT-YOLO is based on the Mean Teacher architecture. It introduces an auxiliary semantic segmentation task and a masking mechanism to enhance the modeling of foreground-background semantic boundaries in the target domain. Specifically, fine-grained feature degradation is reduced by introducing a multiscale context-aware segmentation head equipped with a dense connection structure to effectively capture local and global contextual features. A mask-consistency loss is formulated by applying random masking operations to target images, thereby guiding the network to learn structural context and improving instance-level feature alignment. Extensive experiments on three benchmark domain adaptation tasks (Pascal VOC to Clipart1k, Cityscapes to Foggy Cityscapes, and KITTI to Cityscapes) demonstrate that JMT-YOLO achieves detection performances of 46.9%, 57.3%, and 61.0%, respectively. These results demonstrate superior performance over existing state-of-the-art and baseline methods, thereby validating the effectiveness and generalization of JMT-YOLO.

BLEND: Balancing Personalization vs. Generalization in Federated Vision-Language Models

Payam Abdisarabshali, Fardis Nadimi, Kasra Borazjani, Naji Khosravan, Richeng Jin, Seyyedali Hosseinalipour

cs.CV, cs.LG, (NeurIPS),, 2026

Federated vision-language models (FedVLMs) represent one of the emerging frontiers of machine learning, aiming to integrate federated learning within the fine-tuning pipelines of vision-language models (VLMs). In this work, we introduce BLEND, a new framework designed to jointly enhance the personalization and generalization capabilities of FedVLMs. BLEND proposes a selective VLM parameter fine-tuning and aggregation strategy that consists of (i) global vision and text adapters shared across clients, and (ii) a personalized vision projection adapter tailored to each client. BLEND further introduces a novel loss function that blends the outputs of the global and personalized adapters to achieve personalization, while simultaneously promoting generalization through a principled anchor design implemented via the projection heads of the zero-shot VLM. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BLEND consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, highlighting its ability to effectively balance personalization and generalization across diverse system settings. (Code is available at: https://github.com/payamsiabd/BLEND.git)

What Egocentric Video Embeddings Encode and How to Combine Them with Inertial Sensors for Human Activity Recognition

Kansuke Habano

cs.CV, Challenge, @, HASCA, 2026

Egocentric video and body-worn inertial sensors are increasingly recorded together, and the video is usually encoded by pretrained models. What these embeddings represent when the camera sits on the head, and how they should be combined with inertial sensing, has received little attention. Across VideoMAEv2, CLIP, DINOv2 and the egocentric VC-1, the leading principal directions of the embedding are dominated by where the camera looks. Measured head orientation explains them on Ego-Exo4D for egocentric (R^2 0.49-0.70) but not exocentric cameras, and the scene layout explains them on WEAR. The part that the limbs' inertial signals predict separates activities across people. In a set-level probe, the rest separates exercise sets within a participant but not activities across people. This is consistent with separate roles: the video finds the clips of the same set, and the inertial classifier labels them. On WEAR, with temporal order removed and one random limb per one-second clip, we propagate inertial predictions over a within-participant video graph whose leading components are removed. This raises subject-wise cross-validated macro-F1 from 0.648 to 0.852. In our comparisons, propagation helps only over the video graph, and fusing the modalities in one classifier, or adding inertial edges to the graph, performs worse.

UniDeblur: Unified Motion Deblurring via Fourier Convolution Learning

Xintian Mao, Jiansheng Wang, Huaiyuan Wang, Qingli Li, Yan Wang

cs.CV

Image deblurring is a fundamental task in computer vision, yet existing methods mainly specialize in isolated scenarios. Consequently, the deblurring performance of these models drops significantly across scenarios with different cameras and image contents. This motivates the development of a unified motion deblurring framework that can handle diverse blur patterns. To this end, we construct a multi-source dataset and develop a data pruning and quality control pipeline. Then, to restore blur images with various motion blur kernels across different scenarios, we propose a unified motion deblurring framework via Fourier Convolution Learning. Existing deblurring networks usually rely on pixel-level mappings and overlook the convolutional nature of blur. Thus, the network mostly fits the image content itself rather than learning the convolution process, making it difficult to handle multi-source blur images. Instead of relying on spatial mapping, we perform blur-adaptive global filter learning in Fourier space, which avoids the limited spatial support of kernel estimation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our unified model effectively handles motion blurs across diverse sources and scenes, achieving competitive performance across both in-domain and OOD benchmarks. Code will be released at github.com/DeepMed-Lab-ECNU/Single-Image-Deblur.

In-Context Learning Can Help Vision-Language Models Overcome Training Priors

Kun Wang, Xindi Wu, Sanghyuk Chun, Olga Russakovsky, Esin Tureci

cs.CV

Vision-language models (VLMs) learn strong statistical regularities during training, which can make them fail to properly respond to visual evidence when input images violate those regularities. It is challenging to disambiguate these prior-induced errors from errors caused by the model missing the underlying visual capabilities. In this work, we use controlled visual in-context learning to encourage the model to focus on the visual evidence, and demonstrate that this alone is frequently sufficient to correct the errors -- suggesting that the models in fact do possess the relevant visual capabilities. Specifically, we show that across five vision-centric tasks, demonstrations yield large gains on prior-conflicting counterfactual examples ($+27.2\%$ for Qwen3-VL 235B and $+15.1\%$ for Qwen3-VL 32B) while leaving accuracy on prior-aligned examples nearly unchanged. These findings generalize across five VLM families with gains from $+9.7\%$ to $+30.3\%$. We analyze visual representations and find that demonstrations indeed redirect attention toward grounding cues while reducing reliance on cues that support the learned prior. Finally, we find that in-context learning is limited when the underlying visual capability is weak or absent. Together, these results show that current VLMs possess visual grounding capabilities that standard evaluations may overlook, underscoring the need for controlled evaluation design in diagnosing and improving model capability.

Video Codec-Guided Training-Free Token Compression for Video Large Language Models

Xihua Sheng, Ruijie Liu, Chang Wen Chen

cs.CV

Video large language models (VLLMs) rely on long visual token sequences to understand complex temporal content, which makes visual token compression important for efficient inference. Existing training-free compression methods mainly reduce the number of tokens sent to the language model, but the compression process itself can still require many visual feature comparisons, especially for intra-frame token protection and inter-frame temporal token merging. This paper proposes a video codec-guided training-free token compression method for VLLMs that uses coding information already available in compressed video as a low-cost search prior. The method aligns reference-frame dependencies, motion vectors, and residuals with the visual token grid. Residual information forms compact candidate pools for token protection, while motion information is propagated along reference-frame chains to construct localized candidates for temporal token merging between sparsely sampled frames. Local visual feature similarity comparison is then used within these candidates to make the final compression decisions, without modifying the base VLLM. Experiments on three representative VLLMs and four video-understanding benchmarks show that the proposed method achieves performance comparable to the state-of-the-art training-free baseline FlashVID, and in some settings surpasses it, while substantially reducing the feature comparisons required by token protection and temporal matching. These results demonstrate that codec information can effectively improve the accuracy-complexity tradeoff of video token compression.

MotionCoach: Constructing Multimodal Guidance for Video Professional Motion Editing

Siyi Qian, Hanqing Yang, Weihuang Lin, Zihao Yang, Yichen Lu, Yuanhong Zheng, Sheng Li

cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.MM

Video editing models achieve strong performance in modifying appearance, backgrounds, styles, and motions, empowered by large-scale training data. Beyond general scenarios, professional motions are significant in performance, sports, and martial arts. This paper focuses on professional motion editing, which involves fine-grained motion technique and temporal structure. Given professional motion names but lacking detailed descriptions, existing models often fail to generate professional motions, such as incorrectly lifting the front wheel under a Stoppie instruction. To address this challenge, we introduce MotionCoach, a trained multimodal agent that uses multi-turn tools and performs self-verification, producing a grounded prompt and a reference clip as reliable and sufficient multimodal guidance to inject external professional motion knowledge into video editing models. To supply training data, we build a data pipeline to construct trajectories. We cold-start the agent on MotionCoach-SFT-10k and apply Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on MotionCoach-RL-2k with rewards on the motion guidance prompt, the motion outcome video, and the format. For evaluation, we introduce VPMEBench, comprising 314 professional motion editing tasks with fine-grained references and annotations. Extensive experiments show that MotionCoach improves the overall score from 55.1 to 65.6 on Seedance 2.0 and from 51.2 to 64.5 on MiniMax-H3. Learning to construct multimodal guidance enables precise professional motion editing without retraining video editing models.

Learning Representations to Guide Pixel Diffusion Transformers

Zihao Pan, Mengting Chen, Cheng Shi, Taihang Hu, Zhengrong Yue, Zikang Wang, Yuan Wang, Wei Li, Sibei Yang

cs.CV, Project, page:, https://pan-zihao.github.io/Representation-Guidance

Visual representations are largely developed for understanding, yet what makes them useful for generation remains unclear. We study this question in pixel diffusion transformers and uncover two key insights. First, better representation quality does not necessarily translate into greater generative utility, as externally aligned semantics can be progressively lost during pixel synthesis. Second, architectural separation with specialized pixel decoders mainly redistributes semantic and spatial modeling, without necessarily yielding representations better suited to generation. These insights suggest that a useful generative representation must be both properly formed and actively exploited. Motivated by these insights, we introduce Representation Guidance (RG), which directly forms and exploits internal representations while retaining a plain Transformer architecture. A low-dimensional bottleneck forces generation-relevant information into a compact representation, which is then explicitly used to guide decoder generation by modifying the flow-matching target according to its induced prediction offset. RG learns clearer semantic and spatial structure while scaling efficiently in both parameters and computation. On ImageNet 256x256, RG-L/16 with only 459M parameters outperforms substantially larger pixel diffusion transformers, while RG-XL/16 achieves an FID of 1.55. RG further reaches an FID of 1.74 at 512x512 and extends to text-to-image generation, achieving 0.89 on GenEval and 87.1 on DPG-Bench.
