Learning Features, Not Layouts: Feature-Axis Attention as a Transfer Prior for Medical Imaging

Aon Safdar, Mohamed Saadeldin

cs.CV

Natural-image-pretrained foundation models are increasingly adapted to medical imaging, yet transfer remains unreliable under domain shift, modality heterogeneity, and label scarcity. We trace part of this fragility to an overlooked design choice. Standard vision transformers place all global modeling on self-attention over spatial tokens, an object-layout prior well suited to natural scenes but not to medical images, where the discriminative signal lies in feature correlations and texture that recur across scanners while layout does not. We ask whether the axis of global attention, spatial versus feature, is itself a lever for transferable medical representations. Existing channel-aware hybrids confound this question, adding channel attention to a spatially-mixed backbone and leaving the axes coupled and the feature axis unmeasurable in isolation. We design FAD-ViT, a backbone that strictly decouples the two axes, making feature-axis attention the primary global operator and the attention axis a controlled variable. Evaluating comprehensively across 23 classification, segmentation, and low-data tasks under matched tokenization, capacity, and protocol, we find that shifting global attention from the spatial to the feature axis leaves from-scratch performance essentially unchanged, yet markedly strengthens transfer. Identical ImageNet pretraining yields up to roughly three times the transfer gain, with consistent improvements on every held-out segmentation task at up to a quarter the parameter count and far lower compute than standard and channel-attention baselines. The advantage is largest where texture rather than geometry is discriminative, tracking greater reuse of pretrained features. The axis of global attention is thus a design lever for medical transfer, and making it channel-primary is a simple, cheap, and principled choice.

Unified Generative Video Compression with Autoregressive and Bidirectional Video Diffusion

Tianyu Zhang, Shuai Huo, Dengchao Jin, Hewei Liu, Meng Lei, Tianwen Wu, Jiawen Gu, Chao Zhou, Li Li, Dong Liu

cs.CV

Video diffusion models can reconstruct visually compelling content from severely compressed representations at ultra-low bitrates, but they impose a rigid deployment trade-off. Bidirectional (BI) models achieve high quality through dense full-sequence attention, yet require the entire sequence before reconstruction and incur substantial latency. Autoregressive (AR) models enable low-delay streaming, but their restricted temporal context reduces compression efficiency. This tension is particularly relevant when a video stream must be decoded in real time yet is also stored for later, quality-oriented playback, making AR and BI models natural choices for the live and replay stages, respectively. To support both stages, we present UniGVC, a Unified Generative Video Codec that supports two decoding modes from a single bitstream. UniGVC-BI employs a BI-DiT with dense attention for quality-oriented random-access decoding, whereas UniGVC-AR uses windowed AR-DiT for low-delay streaming. Both paths share a hierarchical latent codec that recurrently produces the same conditions with ultra-low bitrates, allowing UniGVC-BI to serve as a conditional teacher for single-step distillation of UniGVC-AR. A tiny conditional video decoder further reduces streaming latency while preserving most of the reconstruction quality. On standard 1080p benchmarks, UniGVC-BI achieves the best overall rate-distortion-perception trade-off among the evaluated methods and produces temporally stable video at rates as low as 0.0005 bits per pixel. Meanwhile, UniGVC-AR remains competitive while decoding 1080p video at 36.85 FPS, over $250\times$ faster than UniGVC-BI. Code and models will be released later.

What Visual Representations Do World Action Models Need?

Kai Liu, Jiangran Lyu, Tingyu Deng, Yixin Zheng, Jingtian Wan, Wenbo Cui, Mi Yan, Yizhou Zhou, Haoran Li, Dongbin Zhao, Zhizheng Zhang, He Wang

cs.CV, cs.RO

World Action Models (WAMs) inherit powerful priors from pretrained video generation models, but their variational autoencoder (VAE) representations are optimized for visual reconstruction rather than manipulation. External visual representations can improve manipulation, but existing methods couple representation choices with different integration mechanisms, obscuring their individual effects. We systematically compare visual representations, five integration mechanisms, and feature combinations under controlled settings. Our study yields three main findings. First, strong general-purpose visual representations provide broader robustness gains and can match or outperform representations pretrained for specific visual capabilities. Second, channel-wise joint prediction achieves the highest average success rate; further analyses suggest that future-feature prediction and the action expert's effective use of added visual information contribute to its gains. Third, additional sources should be selected for the task-relevant information they contribute beyond existing representations, as the same source can help one combination but harm another. However, even the best evaluated composition provides a smaller additional gain than the initial improvement from adding a strong general-purpose representation to the VAE-only baseline. We instantiate the resulting Representation Recipe as R$^2$-WAM. It achieves state-of-the-art performance on RoboCasa GR1 Tabletop with an 84.7% success rate and reaches 92.8% on RoboTwin 2.0, improving over the VAE-only baselines by 5.9 and 2.2 percentage points, respectively. Taken together, these findings suggest that future work should prioritize stronger general-purpose visual representations and more effective mechanisms for integrating them into WAMs, with feature composition serving as a supplementary route to improvement.

Dataset-Specific Encoding of Image Content Underlies Cross-Dataset Failure in Brain MRI Foundation Models

Minh Sao Khue Luu, Bair N. Tuchinov

cs.CV

Frozen features of brain MRI foundation models lose accuracy on new datasets, a failure usually blamed on the features encoding where each scan came from. We show that the failure lies elsewhere. Across 17 public datasets and eight frozen representations, every representation recognizes the MRI sequence within each dataset, yet most fail on an unseen one. The cause is neither dataset encoding nor a shift of each dataset: the content itself is encoded along different directions in each dataset. This explains which datasets fail, for the sequence and for sex, and why removing the dataset signal with INLP, LEACE, CORAL or adversarial training never improves sequence transfer. Only the broadly pretrained 3DINO and SAM-Med3D encode the sequence consistently enough to transfer; the brain-specific BrainIAC and BrainFM do no better than an untrained network. Code is available at https://github.com/BrainFM/brainfm-profiler/tree/main/representation_utility.

GeoGR: Spatial-Semantic Token Compression and Group Routing for Efficient 3D VLMs

Yongxin Zheng, Hao Wu, Fang Li, Hu Fu, Peiran Yin, Xudong Wang, Haozhe Hu, Xinghao Chen, Yunpu Ma, Wei Zhang, Xiaoyu Shen

cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CL

Multi-view representations enable pretrained image and video vision-language models to understand 3D scenes, but their long visual token sequences make inference costly. Redundancy arises both from repeated observations of the same scene regions across views and from processing all visual tokens at every LLM layer, regardless of their relevance to the current computation. We propose GeoGR, a two-stage framework that jointly compresses scene representations and visual computation. At the projector level, GeoSemZip consolidates cross-view observations into 3D voxel tokens and combines semantic saliency with spatial coverage to construct a compact scene representation. Unselected voxel features are aggregated into contextual anchors to retain scene information that would otherwise be discarded. At the LLM level, we identify an effective visual computation window and observe that query-conditioned token relevance remains locally stable but changes over longer layer intervals. Guided by these findings, GroupRoute combines late entry and early exit with recoverable group-wise skipping. Low-relevance tokens bypass complete LLM layers while retaining their hidden states and can rejoin the active group at subsequent anchor layers selected under a computation budget. For compression-aware post-training, we construct a compact dataset of 67K samples by retaining 30% of the original training pool. With this protocol, GeoGR retains 95.2% and 96.8% of the dense post-trained models' performance on LLaVA-OneVision-7B and Video-3D LLM-7B, respectively, while accelerating LLM prefill by $6.8\times$ and $6.1\times$. These results demonstrate the consistent effectiveness of GeoGR across 3D VLMs with and without explicit 3D spatial encoding, highlighting a favorable performance-efficiency trade-off. The code is available at https://github.com/EIT-NLP/GeoGR.

Redirecting Teacher Supervision: Temporal-Response-Guided Latent Distillation for Video-Language Models

Xinkai Lu, Junli Zha, Haolan Wei, Jiahui Wang, Chao Zhou, Xiang Song

cs.CV

On-policy distillation (OPD) transfers capabilities through teacher predictions on student-generated sequences, as studied by GKD and MiniLLM for language models and Video-OPD for video tasks. We ask how internal supervision can further support temporal learning. Direct hidden-state alignment does not explicitly use the student's temporal responses to choose a supervision direction. We propose Temporal-Response Guidance (TRG), which measures representation responses to local temporal perturbations, adds the teacher correction's projection onto their span back to the original correction, and restores its original norm. The projection is not normalized separately, and correction components outside the response space are retained. With an orthogonal tangent projector and a detached target, TRG changes the supervision direction while preserving the cosine-loss value and aligned-representation gradient norm at target construction. On TempCompass, Video-MME, MVBench, and Video-MMMU, TRG improves the 4B student's macro accuracy by 2.86 percentage points over the reported output-only OPD baseline.

NOFACE3D: NOise-scheduled Focused Anchoring and Controlled Enhancement for 3D Generation

James Rose, Jingwen Liang, Wenchao Ma, Oren Jacob, Haomiao Jiang

cs.CV

Recent image-to-3D models generate complete, high-quality assets from a single image, with global shape and appearance that are already convincing; what remains is the fine scale, where small, thin, elongated, or detail-rich regions are reconstructed softly and under-resolved. This gap can arise in generators with fixed-resolution spatial grids: the same spatial sampling density is used for a large, smooth, low-curvature surface and for a thin, high-curvature structure that carries most of the perceived detail. To this end, we introduce NOFACE3D, a training-free framework that refines these detail-critical regions while preserving the rest of the asset. Given a generated mesh, its input image, and a refinement box, NOFACE3D extracts the selected submesh, scales it up to fill the generator's input volume, and re-encodes it into the full-resolution latent grid. Conditioned on the image crop, it refines the region through flow-based sampling with a spatially varying noise schedule that anchors boundary latents. The refined region is then integrated back into the original asset, preserving the surrounding geometry and appearance. We implement NOFACE3D on TRELLIS.2 and its sparse voxel representation; all of it operates purely at inference time on the frozen pretrained generator. We evaluate NOFACE3D on PAniC-3D, TexVerse and BigDetail, a new benchmark of 339 assets with annotated detail-critical regions. On all three, it generates sharper geometry and finer texture detail than the baselines. Project page: https://idkcouscous.github.io/NoFACE3D/

UniMergeTrack: Hierarchical Model Merging for General RGB-X Visual Object Tracking

Shiao Wang, Xiao Wang, Yuhe Ding, Jiarui Xu, Zikang Yan, Bo Jiang, Yonghong Tian, Jin Tang

cs.CV

RGB-X tracking leverages complementary modalities, such as thermal infrared, event, or depth, to improve robustness under challenging conditions. Existing general-purpose RGB-X trackers typically follow two paradigms: independent task-specific training and joint training across modality combinations. However, the former suffers from knowledge isolation and model redundancy, while the latter introduces optimization interference across heterogeneous tasks. In this work, we present UniMergeTrack, a new model merging paradigm that preserves task-specific optimization while enabling effective cross-task knowledge integration. UniMergeTrack first trains modality-specific trackers independently and then performs hierarchical model merging. Specifically, transferable backbones are merged across RGB-X tasks via high-pass-filtered merging, and complemented by lightweight task-specific experts that preserve modality-specific cues. Tracking heads are merged within each task using optimal transport-guided channel alignment to mitigate hidden channel mismatch. A subsequent distribution alignment fine-tuning stage further improves compatibility between the unified backbone and task-specific heads. Extensive experiments on the LasHeR, RGBT234, VisEvent, VOT-RGBD22, COESOT, and CDTB benchmarks demonstrate that UniMergeTrack achieves state-of-the-art or competitive performance across RGB-Thermal, RGB-Event, and RGB-Depth tracking tasks, while exhibiting strong generalization to unseen datasets. The source code will be released upon acceptance. The source code of this paper will be released on https://github.com/Event-AHU/OpenEvTracking.

A4-MR: Abnormality-aware Anatomical Alignment for MR

Noel D'souza, Zhijian Yang, Nishank Singla, Pranay Doshi, Krisztian Koos, Fei Tan, Bruno Nunes, Laszlo Rusko, Bernadett Kolozsvari, Eszter Sabo, Emanuele Valeriano, Vikash Gupta, Seyed Iman Zare Estakhraji, Marc Lebel, Farzin Haddadpour, Taha Kass-Hout, Erhan Bas

cs.CV

Recent advances in large-scale foundation models have enabled strong generalization across a wide range of clinical and research tasks in magnetic resonance imaging (MRI). However, existing vision-language MRI models remain limited in anatomy-specific localization and pathology-aware representation, particularly in real-world settings. We introduce A4-MR, a 3D vision-language foundation model trained on approximately 50,000 MRI studies comprising 491,000 series, roughly half of which are paired with radiology reports. A4-MR is designed to: (1) learn anatomy localization through large-scale pretraining with noisy labels; (2) align image and text representations at the anatomical level; and (3) learn distinct representations of healthy and abnormal anatomy. Across a diverse set of downstream tasks, A4-MR outperforms existing foundation models, including a +1.7 AUROC improvement over MedSigLIP for abnormality detection. Using a frozen encoder, A4-MR also surpasses an end-to-end task-specific MedNeXt model for knee segmentation by +1.4 Dice and performs comparably to the best task-specific model for abdominal organ segmentation on AMOS, within 0.4 Dice. Finally, without anatomical prompts, A4-MR localizes 3D anatomy comparably to MedSAM2 provided with ground-truth bounding boxes. Together, these results demonstrate that anatomy-level multimodal alignment and abnormality-aware representation learning can enable a single MRI foundation model to support localization, segmentation, and pathology-related tasks.

Evolving Creatives with Clicks: CTR-Driven Advertising image Editing via Attribute Disentanglement and Preference Alignment

Xu Zhang, Haohan Wang, Zheng Zhang, Yingjie Xia, Wei Feng, Honghe Zhu, Jingjing Lv, Changping Peng, Xuheng Qin, Zehua Zhang, Zheng Luo, Qicheng Li

cs.CV, cs.AI

The optimization objective for e-commerce advertising creatives has shifted from aesthetic quality to online business metrics like click-through rate (CTR). Recent methods generate appealing ad images from product images by constructing CTR-based reward signals and refining prompt generation via Reinforcement Learning. Such paradigms equally penalize all attributes within an underperforming image, even though some are actually beneficial to clicks. In this work, we propose Evolving Creatives with Clicks (ECC), which reformulates creative optimization as fine-grained editing rather than generation from scratch. ECC selectively refines attributes that hurt CTR while preserving those that contribute positively. Specifically, faced with a rich attribute space across massive images, ECC first identifies 28 attributes significantly correlated with CTR via ANOVA and Random Forest regression. Based on attribute contrasts between images with disparate CTRs, we instruction-tune a prompt model to generate editing suggestions, then refine it with real click preferences since reference-derived suggestions are not uniformly beneficial. Furthermore, we introduce Consistent Preference Optimization (CPO) as ECC's preference optimization strategy, which enforces consistency across semantically equivalent shuffled suggestions to mitigate DPO overfitting and improve preference generalization. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ECC significantly improves the CTR of ad images, outperforming state-of-the-art models by a substantial margin. The complete data pipeline, including attribute extraction and analysis, the resulting dataset, and source code will be released upon acceptance.

Volume Rendering with Metric--Phase Fields for Thin Structures and Sign-Ambiguous Geometry

Junkai Deng, Jiayi Kong, Xuhui Chen, Guanli Hou, Fei Hou, Junhui Hou, Wenping Wang, Ying He

cs.CV

Neural implicit surface methods that couple volume rendering with signed distance functions (SDFs), such as VolSDF and NeuS, achieve high-quality multi-view reconstruction for watertight objects. However, these approaches rely on a globally consistent inside-outside partition of space. This assumption becomes fragile for thin structures (e.g., sheets, tubes, and thin walls) and can break down for sign-ambiguous geometry (e.g., surfaces with boundary, non-manifold junctions, and closely spaced surface components), often resulting in spurious closures or loss of fine structures. Unsigned distance field (UDF) formulations relax the global sign requirement and better support non-watertight geometry, but are substantially harder to optimize. We propose PhaseSurf, a neural volume rendering framework built on Metric-Phase Fields (MPFs). MPFs decompose geometry into a nonnegative metric field capturing unsigned proximity and a continuous phase field providing a soft sign cue. By coupling the two through residual phase injection, MPFs yield a signed composite implicit function with stable near-surface gradients while retaining UDF-like robustness in sign-ambiguous regions. This design enables direct iso-surface extraction using standard Marching Cubes and avoids the failure modes of both pure SDF- and UDF-based formulations. We further introduce MPF-specific training losses that enforce metric consistency, stabilize phase transitions near the interface, and suppress spurious geometry in free space. Experiments on multi-view datasets with thin structures and sign-ambiguous geometry show that PhaseSurf produces more stable reconstructions and higher-quality geometry than UDF-based methods and recent 3DGS pipelines, while avoiding artifacts typical of SDF-based approaches.

Robust Vision-Language Navigation with Awareness of Instruction-Execution Progress

Jiagan Huang, Zikun Zhou, Hongpeng Wang, Guangming Lu, Jun Yu, and Wenjie Pei

cs.CV

In Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) tasks involving multi-step instructions as guidance, an agent must associate its observations with the relevant instruction content at each decision point. Most graph-based methods commonly match candidate trajectories with the entire instruction, allowing already executed content to interfere with subsequent decisions. Although instruction-execution progress provides a direct cue for separating executed and pending content, existing progress estimation approaches often rely on explicit progress supervision, sophisticated progress-learning stages, or costly online reasoning with LLMs. In this paper, we propose a robust VLN framework with Awareness of Instruction-Execution Progress (VLN-AIEP), which couples progress estimation with navigation decision-making. VLN-AIEP associates the visited trajectory with the instruction to suppress embeddings corresponding to executed content, and then matches candidate locations with the pending content. This coupling makes correct navigation decisions depend on accurate progress estimation, allowing the progress estimator to learn directly from navigation decision labels without explicit progress annotations. To support progress estimation, we construct a landmark-anchored trajectory representation that encodes the semantic labels and viewpoint-relative geometry of instruction-referred landmarks. These landmarks serve as spatial references for associating the visited trajectory with executed instruction content. Extensive experiments on R2R, RxR, CVDN, and R2R-CE demonstrate that the proposed method achieves favorable performance across multiple metrics.

What Do Visual-Head Discovery Scores Actually Find? Attention Source, Necessity, and Random-Control Pitfalls in Vision-Language Models

Md Abdul Kadir, Daniel Sonntag

cs.CV

Attention heads that attend to image regions relevant to a question are increasingly used to steer, ground and interpret vision-language models (VLMs) and to reduce their hallucinations. They are mostly found by ranking heads with an attention- pattern score and validated by steering or masking them. We ask how the choices inside such a discovery score shape the heads it returns, and which of the two validations identifies them. On Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct we vary the query type, the discovery corpus and the token positions whose attention is read (the attention source) in a full 2 x 3 x 2 design, and test all 12 head-sets for steerability (CERS: Contrastive Evidence-Region Steering score) and necessity (NCS: Necessity Con- trast Score) against layer-matched random head-sets. (1) The attention source and the query type reshape the selected heads far more than the corpus: changing the source keeps a median of 49 of the top-100 heads, the query 55 and the cor- pus 84, while two halves of the same data share 96-99. (2) Necessity follows the attention source: head-sets discovered from query-span attention are more nec- essary than last-token ones (difference 0.083, p=6 x 10^-10), and the two re- ferring + query-span sets lie 17-19 standard deviations above 250 layer-matched random sets (Holm-adjusted p=0.048). The referring + query-span effect re- plicates on several recent VLMs. (3) Steerability is not specific to the discovered heads: no discovered head-set steers better than its layer-matched random sets (rank p >= 0.14), and random sets drawn from the same or from any layer span the discovered sets' scores. Necessity therefore separates the discovery settings, while steering at this strength does not. We turn these findings into a protocol for reporting visual-head discoveries.

Replaying the Movie in Your Mind Decoding Dynamic Visual Perception and Recall from EEG

Zikai Fang, Tian-Yi Zhou, Xuan-Hao Liu, Yixi Li, Xiaoyu Xing, Yutong Pei, Bao-Liang Lu, Wei-Long Zheng

cs.CV

Decoding dynamic visual content from EEG has advanced rapidly for visual perception, yet it remains unclear which aspects of neural representations are preserved when people internally replay dynamic scenes. We introduce PAIR, an EEG benchmark collected from 20 participants using an interleaved, clip-wise paradigm that pairs naturalistic video viewing with immediate visual recall. This design enables a comparison between externally driven perception and internally generated recall for the same stimulus content within a fixed trial sequence. We benchmark multiple decoding models on hierarchical semantic classification at both fine-grained and coarse-grained levels, meta-feature decoding tasks covering motion, color, object number, face, and human presence, and a state-decoding task that discriminates perception from recall. Within-subject validation results show numerically similar performance for several model--task combinations, alongside higher perception accuracy in two coarse-grained comparisons after multiple-comparison correction. In contrast, perception and recall are highly separable at the state level under the present validation protocol. State accuracy remains high after alpha-band ablation, although this does not exclude ocular or other task-related contributions.

GuidedNav: Shaping Vision-Language Navigation Representations through Pre-action Attention and Spatial Guidance

Jingyu Guo, Jiaxin Huang, Chengxing Lin, Xiaoyu Luo, Cheng Wen, Shanshan Ye, Tingjin Chu, Shaoli Huang, and Mingming Gong

cs.CV

Vision-and-language navigation (VLN) requires an agent to track progress through an extended instruction, identify landmarks relevant to its current objective, and understand the surrounding spatial structure to act. End-to-end navigation policies typically rely on action supervision to implicitly learn these capabilities. Without explicit guidance on the evidence underlying each decision, however, policies can overfit to correlations predictive of training actions rather than learn the task-relevant grounding and geometry needed for generalization. Our insight is to guide the representations that connect instructions to actions: identifying what remains to be done determines which visual evidence matters, while understanding the geometry around that evidence informs how to move. We introduce GuidedNav, a unified framework that explicitly shapes these representations through training-time guidance. Subtask and landmark guidance steer native pre-action attention toward the active instruction segment and relevant visual regions, grounding action prediction in the current objective. Spatial guidance aligns the policy's internal representations with geometric features that capture long-horizon context and local spatial structure. Together, these objectives strengthen navigation capabilities while avoiding the inference overhead of intermediate textual reasoning and online geometric encoding. We also introduce Guided-R2R and Guided-RxR, two annotation-enriched navigation datasets that support more interpretable VLN research. GuidedNav establishes state-of-the-art performance among the compared monocular RGB policies, improving success rates by 8.1% and 13.1% relative to the strongest prior results on R2R-CE and RxR-CE val-unseen, respectively, while achieving faster inference. Extensive experiments further demonstrate the effectiveness and real-world transferability of our approach.

Measurable Semantic 3D Gaussian Splatting with Joint Region and Depth Supervision for Interactive Building Inspection

Yi Zhang, Tian Tan, and Ray Kai Leung Su

cs.CV

Image-based 3D reconstruction provides a scalable representation for building inspection and maintenance. However, photorealistic reconstruction alone does not fully provide the geometric measurability, semantic structure, and interactive information access required for engineering applications. This study proposes a measurable semantic 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) framework with region and depth supervision for interactive building inspection from multi-view imagery. Mask-based region supervision suppresses irrelevant backgrounds and focuses optimization on target regions. Point-wise depth supervision guides Gaussian centers toward physical surfaces using reference geometry derived from multi-view stereo (MVS) point clouds, providing geometric anchors for measuring physical dimensions and areas. A semantic Gaussian field associates learnable class features with individual Gaussian primitives, enabling semantic rendering and component-level inspection. These capabilities are operationalized through AEC-SplatTwin, an interactive building inspection platform supporting real-time visualization, semantic analysis, quantitative measurement, and multimodal large language model (MLLM)-assisted interaction. On the synthetic UAV-inspection QuakeCity and smartphone-captured HKUPB datasets, the framework reduces Gaussian-center depth MAE by 58.1% and 86.8%, while its semantic field achieves mIoU values of 0.8529 and 0.8642. On HKUPB, the mean relative errors for height, width, and area measurements are 0.33%, 1.07%, and 0.60%. These results show that the proposed framework extends 3DGS beyond visual reconstruction into a measurable, semantically structured, and interactive engineering representation for building inspection.

From Glance to Focus: Mitigating Recognition Laziness in MLLMs for Fine-Grained Recognition

Yutong Yang, Zhuqi Liu, Mouxing Yang, Xi Peng

cs.CV

Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) generally fall short of expectations in Fine-Grained Visual Recognition (FGVR), although they exhibit promising FGVR foundations, i.e., visual perception and broad category knowledge. Most existing studies seek to remedy this deficiency by adopting domain-specific post-training, at the risk of compromising generalization to unseen domains. Different from these efforts, in this paper, we revisit the FGVR mechanism underlying MLLMs from both the empirical and theoretical perspectives and uncover an inference-time phenomenon termed FGVR Laziness. In brief, FGVR Laziness refers to the insufficient association between visual cues and category concepts under certain candidate category lists and recognition guidance, leaving the FGVR foundations of MLLMs largely under-utilized. To mitigate FGVR Laziness, we propose Laziness-resIstant Visual rEcognition (LIVE), a training-free framework which jointly refines candidate lists and recognition guidance to elicit the latent FGVR capability of MLLMs. Specifically, LIVE first performs query-adaptive candidate inference to derive a compact yet reliable candidate list. Meanwhile, LIVE constructs category-specific FGVR skills by mining the intra-category characteristics and inter-category differences. Finally, LIVE selectively invokes detailed skill guidance according to recognition uncertainty and category confusion, shifting MLLM-based FGVR from a cursory glance to a focus on associating discriminative visual cues with category concepts. Extensive experiments on 13 benchmarks against 24 state-of-the-art methods spanning fine-grained category recognition, action recognition, and general category recognition, verify the effectiveness and generalizability of LIVE across different MLLM backbones. The code will be released upon acceptance.

PreCoG: Preview-Edit for Efficient Video Correction in Generation-Time

Yilai Liu, Wenqi Pei, Zhifeng Lu, Shiyuan Zhang, Zongxi Liu, Hongyang Du

cs.CV

Iterative text-to-video generation relies on feedback to correct compositional and temporal failures. However, existing methods typically require another full generation run for each correction, repeating computation and potentially disrupting already correct content. We introduce PreCoG, an iterative framework for correcting semantic failures during video generation. PreQA diagnoses failures from low-cost previews and constructs corrective conditions, while PreEdit edits cached generation trajectories under these conditions, attenuating orthogonal changes to limit disruption of already correct content. PreCoG completes generation only for the selected candidate, reusing its cached trajectory to reduce redundant computation and help preserve semantic consistency between the preview and the final video. Experiments show that PreCoG achieves the highest VBench and T2V-CompBench scores and approaches VSR's leading VS2 score at $0.56\times$ its inference time. It also outperforms GENMAC on all three quality and alignment metrics at $0.86\times$ its inference time.

Fix the Match, Not the Solver: STITCH -- a Self-Supervised Compatibility Measure for Eroded Visual Puzzles

Nadav Alali, Ohad Ben-Shahar

cs.CV

Unlike the clean and pixel-aligned complete pieces of standard jigsaw benchmarks, real-world puzzle fragments lose their boundary regions to erosion, destroying the signal that classical compatibility measures (CMs) rely on. We first re-establish, via oracle experiments, that the CM, not the solver, is the performance bottleneck: with a perfect CM, even a simple greedy solver achieves perfect reconstruction. This motivates STITCH, a self-supervised CM that sidesteps missing boundaries by comparing frozen ViT patch tokens from piece interiors via a lightweight cross-attention head (~249K trainable parameters), trained without manual annotation. Plugged into unchanged compatibility-based solvers, STITCH reaches .992/.954 Abs/Perf on JPwLEG-5 (+.629 Perf over the best prior method), nearly saturating the benchmark. On a new ImageNet 7x7 benchmark, it outperforms all alternatives, including inpaint-then-match pipelines built on modern generative models. Importantly, performance remains robust across erosion levels and generalizes to puzzles with over 40% missing pieces without retraining. Trained on the recent GAP datasets of irregular, heavily eroded fragments, the same CM recipe enables compatibility-based solvers to surpass recent learning-based solvers on GAP-3 and GAP-5. Since this leap in performance is achieved by a pluggable CM module, the takeaway is clear: fix the match, not the solver.

CompactSplat: Spatially Adaptive Gaussian Distribution for Feedforward Scene Reconstruction

Bing He, Jingnan Gao, Yunuo Chen, Zhengxue Cheng, Rong Xie, Li Song, Wenjun Zhang

cs.CV

Reconstructing 3D scenes from sparse views without per-scene optimization remains highly challenging, especially for recovering accurate geometry and fine textures. While recent feedforward approaches leverage generalizable 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) for scene generation, they typically assign one or multiple Gaussians to each pixel. Such uniform allocation not only produces highly redundant representations by wasting primitives in homogeneous areas, but also fails to exploit the inherent geometric priors of planar surfaces, often resulting in sub-optimal structural modeling. To address these limitations, we present CompactSplat, a novel feedforward framework that enables a content-aware allocation of 3D Gaussians. By integrating texture-guided spatial partitioning with hierarchical sampling, CompactSplat adaptively distributes primitives - concentrating dense Gaussians in complex regions while tiling flat areas with expanded, scale-modulated primitives. Moreover, our framework inherently supports dynamic sparsity control at inference time, enabling seamless trade-offs between rendering fidelity and memory footprint without requiring network retraining. Extensive experiments on RealEstate10K, DL3DV, and ScanNet demonstrate that CompactSplat consistently outperforms prior methods on both standard metrics and high-resolution rendering consistency, achieving high-fidelity reconstructions with significantly fewer primitives.

High-Throughput Generative Video Super-Resolution on a Single Consumer GPU

Georgios Panaretou, Sotirios Chatzis

cs.CV

Generative video super-resolution (VSR) can recover visually compelling high-frequency detail by leveraging diffusion priors, but iterative sampling and heavy spatio-temporal coupling make practical deployments difficult. We study a different operating point: practical HD generative VSR on a single high-end consumer GPU without end-to-end retraining. To this end, we present a zero-shot framework built entirely from pretrained components. The proposed system decouples spatial enhancement from temporal stabilization. A time-distilled one-step diffusion backbone handles spatial upscaling, while temporal coherence is promoted by a lightweight pretrained flow-based interpolator that synthesizes intermediate frames between super-resolved keyframes. To further improve scalability, we introduce a resolution-aware hybrid attention scheme that, in a zero-shot manner, replaces selected high-resolution global-attention layers with neighborhood attention while preserving global attention in semantically critical low-resolution layers, while also improving temporal consistency. Experiments show that the proposed framework reaches up to ~32 FPS at 720p resolution under interleaved inference on a single NVIDIA RTX 4090. At 4K, the proposed attention substitution can yield up to a 44% speedup over the original attention implementation. Across standard VSR benchmarks, the method consistently shifts the speed-quality trade-off decisively toward runtime efficiency, trading reconstruction fidelity for strong no-reference perceptual quality, while remaining reasonably competitive against generative approaches in temporal consistency. It also exposes clear limitations in handling more challenging motion, which a simple motion gate partially mitigates. Code will be released upon acceptance.

MixTok: Adaptive Visual Tokenization with Global 1D Tokens and Sparse 2D Refinement

Chenge Li, Yefei He, Xinyu Liu, Feng Chen, Hong Zhou, Bohan Zhuang

cs.CV

Efficient visual tokenization requires balancing representation compactness with the preservation of fine-grained image content. Conventional grid-based tokenizers allocate tokens uniformly across spatial locations despite non-uniform visual complexity, whereas compact 1D tokenizers aggregate global information but can struggle to preserve local details. We introduce MixTok, a hybrid visual tokenizer that combines a compact 1D global representation with sparse 2D tokens for local refinement. A content-adaptive router ranks candidate locations by refinement priority and determines the number of 2D tokens for each image under a target average token budget. At the highest-scoring locations, 2D token features replace the spatial features recovered from the 1D representation, and a shared decoder reconstructs the image from the resulting mixed features. On ImageNet 256 x 256, MixTok achieves highly competitive reconstruction and generation performance, with an rFID of 1.195 and a gFID of 1.95 at respective average budgets of approximately 128 and 131 content tokens per image.

Destination-Conditioned Adaptation with Semantic-Geometric Alignment for Generative Inbetweening

Zhiyu Xu (1), Wei Shang (2), Yushen Xu (1), Wenwen Li (3), Zhiyan Xu (4), Pengfei Zhu (1), Dongwei Ren (1) ((1) Tianjin University, (2) Singapore Management University, (3) Tianjin Medical University, (4) Southeast University)

cs.CV

Generative inbetweening aims to synthesize temporally coherent intermediate frames between a start frame and a target frame. Although pretrained image-to-video diffusion models provide strong priors for large-motion inbetweening, they are not explicitly designed to reach a specified target frame. They may therefore generate realistic individual frames while drifting from the target or producing unstable motion under large displacements and non-rigid dynamics. We propose DSG-GI, a framework that adapts pretrained latent video diffusion models through \textbf{D}estination-Conditioned Adaptation and \textbf{S}emantic-\textbf{G}eometric Alignment. Destination-Conditioned Adaptation treats the target frame as the destination of the sequence and provides temporally progressive guidance along the generated trajectory. Semantic-Geometric Alignment aligns the token-wise spatio-temporal relational geometry of backbone features with that of a frozen video foundation encoder and applies a flow-matching-aware latent flow constraint to samples near the data endpoint of the probability path. Together, these components strengthen destination guidance and preserve coherent semantic-geometric motion structure. Extensive experiments on standard and large-motion inbetweening benchmarks demonstrate improvements in temporal stability, target-frame faithfulness, and perceptual quality. Our code will be made publicly available.

Golden Anchor for Video Generation

Jinyuan Xu, Shitong Shao, Lichen Bai, Zikai Zhou, Guoxi Huang, Nantheera Anantrasirichai, Zeke Xie

cs.CV, cs.LG

Video generation supports digital content creation, with applications in virtual avatars, AI-driven filmmaking, interactive game engines, and world models. State-of-the-art systems achieve controllability primarily through image-to-video (I2V) generation, in which an injected anchor image determines the identity, composition, and style of the resulting video. In modern pipelines, this anchor is synthesized by a text-to-image (T2I) generator. This raises a largely overlooked question: Are images obtained directly from the T2I model truly suitable for current video models? Our analysis shows that anchor aesthetic and quality scores are weakly correlated with downstream video quality. The structure-and-texture score is negatively correlated with I2V video quality in both the visual and motion dimensions. Fine-tuning the T2I model on an image reward accordingly yields anchors with visibly denser structural and character detail: they score higher on image metrics, yet lower the video reward on every I2V model we test and change downstream motion unpredictably. We therefore introduce Golden Anchor Learning (GAL), to our knowledge the first framework for learning golden anchors by optimizing a T2I generator with downstream video feedback. GAL evaluates candidate anchors through the videos they induce under a frozen I2V model and converts video rewards into image-level preferences for DPO fine-tuning of the T2I generator. On all four I2V models, GAL raises the VBench average by $2.97\%$--$8.46\%$ over the untuned generator. The rendered clips also score higher under VideoReward-Overall, and their extracted frames under HPSv3 and UniPercept. GAL yields consistent motion gains across all four I2V models, as measured by a $5.62\%$--$38.24\%$ increase in VBench dynamic degree. Although GAL is trained only on HunyuanVideo-1.5 renders, these gains extend to the other three I2V models.

Complexity-Calibrated Reconstruction Discrepancy for No-Reference Point Cloud Quality Assessment

Haocheng Wu, Xiangjie Sui, Xin Sun

cs.CV

The discrepancy induced by surface reconstruction in a distorted point cloud is jointly influenced by its intrinsic structural complexity and signal degradation. This observation motivates a referencefree point cloud quality assessment (PCQA) strategy that estimates and compensates for the complexity-induced component. We implement this strategy through successive reconstruction. Specifically, reconstructing the distorted point cloud produces an input-toreconstruction discrepancy that combines the effects of signal degradation and content complexity. Reapplying the reconstruction operator with stronger smoothing produces a discrepancy between successive reconstructions, which serves as a proxy for the complexityinduced component. This proxy is then used to calibrate the inputto-reconstruction discrepancy. We apply the same strategy to 3D geometry patches and their aligned 2D regions, enabling local comparison of the resulting discrepancies. The calibrated geometric and projected-view discrepancies are then fused into a final degradation score. For both branches, the discrepancies are measured using frozen pretrained perceptual representations. Our method does not require PCQA-specific training or fine-tuning. Experiments on three public databases show that the proposed method consistently outperforms most generic baselines and achieves performance comparable to several full-reference metrics.The code is available at https://github.com/HC-W-whc/CCRD-NR-PCQA.git.

Multi-Modal Graph Neural Network-Inspired Vessel Identification and Tracking for UAV-Based Maritime Surveillance

Chenjie Zhao, Jiangtian Nie, Jiawen Kang, Yuxu Lu, Ryan Wen Liu

cs.CV

The unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV)-based maritime surveillance is becoming an important part of maritime Internet of Things (IoT) and intelligent transportation systems. It provides a flexible and cost-effective way to large-scale maritime traffic monitoring. However, achieving robust situational awareness remains challenging in practical maritime environments. The main challenges arise from heterogeneous sensing modalities, adverse imaging conditions, and spatial misalignment caused by UAV motion. To address these problems, this paper proposes an airborne multi-modal graph learning-driven framework, termed AMGT, for vessel identification and tracking in UAV-assisted maritime IoT. Specifically, visible and infrared images are first aligned and fused through a multi-parameter perspective transformation model. This improves visual perception under low visibility and complex illumination conditions. Automatic identification system (AIS) messages are then temporally synchronized using interpolation and linear prediction. To detect small and oriented vessels, we develop a mixed-modality MAir-YOLOv8-OBB detector, followed by the MAir-SORT algorithm for stable vessel tracking. We further design a graph learning-driven association network to model temporal dynamics and spatial dependencies among vessels and between vessels and UAVs. A motion-aware coordinate unification (MCU) strategy is also introduced to reduce nonlinear misalignment caused by UAV motion. Experiments on the MAP-M2UAV50 dataset show that AMGT achieves high accuracy and efficiency. The results demonstrate its potential for UAV-assisted maritime IoT applications, including vessel monitoring, traffic situational awareness, and intelligent maritime transportation services. The dataset and code will be available at https://github.com/MAPGroup/AMGT.

Automated Detection and Encoding of Cartographic Signs in Historical Maps

Remi Petitpierre

cs.CV

Symbols and icons in archival maps represent an expansive source of evidence for historical geography. In this paper, we present a two-stage framework for (1) detecting cartographic signs in historical maps and (2) generating interpretable sign embeddings that capture both semantics and stylistic variations. We also introduce CaSiDD, an open benchmark dataset spanning multiple sign categories and diverse cartographic styles, designed for generic sign detection. Using YOLOv10-X, we demonstrate the practicality of automated sign detection on historical maps. We then present strategies for creating contrasted, informative embeddings of cartographic signs. We approach embedding design systematically, maximizing expressiveness while suppressing digitization or background material confounders. Our final method, based on an adapted RADIOv4 backbone, effectively encodes both sign form and semantics, while producing interpretable representations. This work lays a foundation for scalable evidence extraction for the comparative study of map signs within cartographic culture and geohistorical research.

FPD-Drive: Future Policy Distillation for End-to-End Autonomous Driving

Li-Heng Chen, Qifeng Chen, Chengwei Wu, Yingjie Zhang, Sheng Yang, Hongbo Fu, Shaoqing Ren

cs.CV

End-to-end autonomous driving models are typically trained on real-world driving logs, which record only the trajectory actually executed in each scene. This provides limited supervision for learning alternative valid behaviors. Multi-target supervision can mitigate this limitation, but broader trajectory coverage makes safe candidate selection more challenging. We propose FPD-Drive, an end-to-end planner that distills privileged future knowledge into a visual driving policy to improve both multi-trajectory supervision and safety selection. Specifically, we introduce Future-Privileged Multi-Target Distillation to transfer diverse, high-quality trajectory targets from a teacher with access to recorded future observations to the student. These targets extend supervision beyond the single recorded human trajectory. For candidate selection, we further introduce Safety-Aware Score Correction to correct ranking errors between nearby safe and unsafe candidates and raise underestimated safety scores of safe candidates. At deployment, the student requires neither future observations nor teacher inference. Our base model achieves 94.9 PDMS on NAVSIM v1 navtest and 55.9 EPDMS on NAVSIM v2 navhard. The scaled FPD-Drive-Pro improves these scores to 95.5 PDMS and 58.1 EPDMS, respectively, achieving SOTA performance among the compared methods on both benchmarks. Zero-shot closed-loop evaluation on HUGSIM further yields an HD-Score of 45.1 and RC of 55.3.

Jag: Direct Box Prediction for Efficient Visual Grounding

Xiuyuan Zhu, Ke Lu, Hao Wu, Zijin Du, Dongming Zhang, Jian Xue

cs.CV

Visual grounding combines image--language understanding with geometric prediction, yet autoregressive multimodal models often express a box as a coordinate sequence. Sequential decoding adds computation to the multimodal processing needed for localization. Adapting these representations to box geometry offers a route to direct prediction. We present Jag, which adapts a compact pretrained multimodal model for direct continuous box prediction. A lightweight regression head reads the last valid input-token state, conditioned on the image and referring expression, and predicts all four box coordinates together. Geometric supervision jointly adapts the language backbone, visual merger, and regression head, allowing localization losses to shape both the multimodal representations and the coordinate mapping. Inference retains image--language processing and returns the box in one forward pass, without coordinate generation or text parsing. Across benchmarks, Jag consistently improves grounding accuracy over its base model across the observed target sizes and expression groups. Component ablation show that jointly adapting the representations and head substantially improves accuracy over training the head alone. Against existing specialized grounding models, it achieves competitive accuracy with lower single-request latency and GPU memory use. These results show how supervision tailored to the geometric answer can specialize a compact pretrained multimodal model for accurate grounding and efficient direct prediction. Project page: https://xyzzzh.github.io/Jag/

Deep Learning Approach for RealTime Contraction Detection in Laboring Dairy Cattle

Sebastian Puchala, Dominik Gronkiewicz, Marcin Go{\l}\k{e}biewski, Grzegorz Grodkowski, Grzegorz Gwardys

cs.CV

Currently, the animal production sector is undergoing rapid development of techniques that allow automatic monitoring of animal behaviour and health. This paper presents a computer vision-based approach for the automatic recognition of labor contractions in cows. During the course of the study, videos depicting 100 cow births were collected. From the obtained videos, 8500 cow contraction actions and about 25 000 non-contraction sequences were extracted which constituted the dataset. Vision Transformers Learn Word Meaning from Images Alone

Anders Gj{\o}lbye, Sebastian Ray Mason, Stella Frank, Lars Kai Hansen

cs.CV, cs.CL

Can a vision model learn what words mean from pixels alone? We show that the two largest DINOv3 vision transformers organize words rendered in images by word meaning, despite being pretrained using self-supervision on images only, without paired captions, class labels, or other language supervision. We derive word representations from embedding differences between word images and matched controls, then test whether semantically related words are neighbors within each model's embedding space, while controlling for spelling similarity. We find that brand names in the same industry group together, as do nouns from the same semantic domain. Country-capital pairs exhibit consistent vector offsets, similarly to word embeddings trained on text. These word representations also align with the representations of the visual object categories they name. DINOv3-H and DINOv3-7B are, to our knowledge, the first self-supervised vision models to show substantial semantic structure for rendered words. Comparisons with text and speech encoders reveal shared structure among representations of the same words. Contrastive alignment with either encoder further strengthened DINOv3-H's word-level semantic structure, while leaving the other finetuned vision models largely unchanged. Image-only training can therefore teach vision models not just what words look like, but also how their meanings relate.

Beyond a Single Crop: Contextualized Embeddings for Player Re-Identification

Ge Luo, Changhuo Yang, Weisi Fan, Rui Yang, Shaokai Wang

cs.CV

Person re-identification is typically addressed by learning identity-discriminative embeddings from individual person crops. However, player re-identification in broadcast sports videos presents a unique challenge: players from the same team often share similar appearances, while video sequences provide repeated observations of the same players under different viewpoints and surrounding contexts. Existing crop-based approaches largely ignore this contextual information, which can be critical for resolving visually similar identities. Here, we propose ContextReID, a contextualized embedding framework that represents a player by jointly considering other players in the same frame and observations from different frames of the broadcast sequence. By incorporating both co-occurring players and cross-frame observations, the model learns identity features relative to the surrounding visual context rather than relying only on isolated appearance cues. We implement this framework with a vision-language model that takes sets of player crops as input and generates context-aware embeddings for individual players. A combined contextual learning and retrieval objective encourages the model to capture cross-frame identity relationships while maintaining discriminative embeddings for player matching. Experiments across three multi-object tracking benchmarks show that ContextReID improves player retrieval over conventional single-crop embedding approaches in both Rank-1 and mAP. These results highlight the utility of spatial and temporal context for reliable player identification in broadcast sports videos.

SheafPrune: Sheaf-Guided Graph Propagation for Visual Token Pruning

Xiaoyang Ma, Chi Wang, Hong Sun

cs.CV

Multimodal large language models process the complete visual token sequence, increasing prefill cost even when many tokens are irrelevant to the instruction. Token-wise saliency captures limited inter-token dependence; graph-based selection instead depends on how relations govern importance propagation and ranking. We study support-constrained propagation with node-degree correction. Inspired by sheaf geometry, SheafPrune uses modality-specific maps and a sparse text--visual graph to transfer relevance, then propagates scores on a separate visual graph and corrects them for degree. It retains a hard top-$k$ subset once before the frozen language model. The selector has about $4.5$M parameters and is trained end-to-end through the frozen LLaVA-NeXT-8B backbone using only the language-modeling objective. Explicit retention-ratio conditioning lets one checkpoint serve every budget. Across four backbones, ten benchmarks and seven pruning ratios, with all baselines re-implemented under one protocol, SheafPrune achieves the best retention at $22$ of $28$ backbone--budget settings, with monotonically growing margins in the moderate-to-aggressive regime. On LLaVA-NeXT, retaining $10\%$ of visual tokens yields a $5.15\times$ net prefill speedup and an $84.0\%$ end-to-end FLOPs reduction. Together with component ablations, these results support graph-constrained importance propagation and degree correction as a design for visual token selection across the evaluated backbones and tokenization schemes, improving the trade-off between accuracy retention and inference cost. code:https://github.com/Bob0817912/SheafPrune_ICLR_2027

CV-OPD: Contrastive Vision On-policy Distillation

Haowei Wang, Hao Lin, Qi Zhu, Jiayi Ji, Deyi Ji, Liujuan Cao, Rongrong Ji

cs.CV, cs.LG, cs.MM, of, VLMs., Computer, Vision., Post-training., VLM., LLM

On-policy distillation (OPD) reveals versatility and convenience during VLM post-training, providing dense supervision for student from teacher. However, we observed an anomaly that the distilled VLM could still answer the questions accurately even when the related visual regions were masked or replaced with noise. Building on this observation, our quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate that OPD, which uses the answer as a hint, has failed to teach students to genuinely seek out and rely on visual evidence. Our theoretical analysis suggests that this failure arises from a shortcut enabled by the lack of visual constraints during distillation. To address the issue, we introduce Contrastive Vision On-policy Distillation (CV-OPD), which compares teacher distributions induced by positive and negative crops while conditioning on the same student prefix and augments optimization with a margin-based contrastive constraint, which is modulated with discrepancy gating across token and sample level. Specifically, CV-OPD first introduces a negative teacher unrelated to the answer and designs a Contrastive Divergence Loss (CDL) that constrains the feature distribution of student by contrasting the positive and negative teachers, correcting how the student exploits visual regions. The CV-OPD introduces a Discrepancy Aware Gate (DAG) that uses token-level and sample-level discrepancies to select visually relevant tokens for optimization, directing the student's attention toward visual content. Extensive experiments across a broad range of fine-grained visual benchmarks demonstrate that CV-OPD substantially alleviates shortcut and consistently improves the visual reasoning capabilities of existing VLMs.

ConClick: Element-Wise Contrastive Learning Improves GUI Grounding

Ruotian Zhang, Yuhui Zhang, Junzhe Huang, Yu Xin, Hehe Fan, Xiaoxiao Sun

cs.CV, data:, https://github.com/zhangruotian/ConClick

GUI grounding, the task of locating the interface element specified by a user instruction, is a fundamental capability of GUI agents. However, existing grounding models remain error-prone, often causing downstream task failures. We systematically evaluate 10 grounding models across five datasets and identify a major error pattern: most incorrect clicks hit non-target interface elements rather than the background, with different models repeatedly selecting the same distractors. Motivated by these findings, we propose ConClick, a contrastive learning framework that teaches models to distinguish the target element from plausible distractors. We construct ConClick-Data, a dataset containing 4,292 challenging target-distractor pairs, and train the model using complementary listwise ranking and pairwise margin objectives. ConClick improves the grounding accuracy of Qwen3-VL-8B from 54.71% to 66.03% on ScreenSpot-Pro, from 83.72% to 86.28% on MMBench-GUI L2, and from 63.33% to 70.20% on OSWorld-G, outperforming prior methods built on the same or similarly sized backbones. We further introduce ReClick, an inference-time strategy that revisits promising regions and compares predictions across competing candidates, yielding an additional 2.96 percentage-point improvement in average accuracy across the three benchmarks. Together, our analysis, dataset, and methods provide both empirical insights and practical tools for building more reliable GUI agents. Data and code are available at https://github.com/zhangruotian/ConClick.

OWLET: On-Policy Weighted Learning via Evidence Transfer for Video Large Language Model Post-Training

Qitan Lv, Ke Fan, Hong Wang, Wen Wu, Yilin Wang, Bowen Zhou, Feng Wu, Chao Zhang

cs.CV

Post-training has become a key stage for eliciting reasoning capabilities in Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs). Existing post-training methods mainly follow two paradigms: reinforcement learning (RL) and on-policy distillation (OPD). However, they do not fully exploit visual evidence for effective training. RL supervises the policy with a single trajectory-level reward, which is too coarse-grained for video reasoning: the same response-level advantage is applied to every generated token, disregarding that the tokens of a video response are grounded in the visual stream to very different degrees. OPD provides token-level supervision through output-distribution matching, but lacks direct supervision of the spatiotemporal representations that produce those outputs. We observe across extensive experiments that the same model reasons more accurately when its video view combines global temporal context with question-relevant details. Comparing the same student-generated response under this view and uniform sampling reveals changes in token likelihoods and internal representations, providing a training signal for evidence use. Building on this observation, we propose OWLET, an evidence-privileged RL method that derives training signals from a richer teacher view. This view couples uniform global context with question-conditioned local windows, providing visual evidence without requiring a larger model. To better exploit this privileged visual evidence, OWLET introduces (i) \textbf{evidence-aware credit redistribution}, which compares token likelihoods across the two views and redistributes positive response-level advantages according to their relative evidence support; and (ii) \textbf{spatiotemporal state alignment}, which aligns the two views' hidden states on verifier-accepted responses.

QCLR: A Plug-in Robustification Module via Quantization for Contrastive Learning in Self-Supervised Skeleton-Based Human Action Recognition

Nanzhu Chen, Feixiang He, Yunfeng Diao, Duygu Sarikaya, He Wang

cs.CV

Skeleton-based human action recognition has gained increasing attention due to the stability of skeleton data across diverse environments and advances in motion capture technologies. Following the success of supervised learning approaches, self-supervised learning approaches, particularly contrastive learning ones, have achieved impressive performance. However, they remain vulnerable to adversarial attacks. In this work, we propose a plug-in robustification module via quantization for contrastive learning in self-supervised skeleton-based human action recognition (QCLR), which enhances the robustness of representations against adversarial attacks while preserving their representational capability. Specifically, we employ a dual-level quantization scheme across the encoding pipeline, which breaks the gradient propagation and absorbs small adversarial perturbations within the quantization bins. To mitigate the impact of inevitable quantization error, we further design an attention-guided multi-faceted quantization strategy to maintain the representation quality. Experiments on several benchmark datasets demonstrate that integrating QCLR into a progressive lineage of mainstream contrastive learning models substantially improves robustness against both gradient-based white-box and gradient-free black-box adversarial attacks, while preserving highly competitive classification accuracy.

Decoding Register Tokens Preserves Global Scene Details

David Jacob Drexlin, Julius Hense, Jonas Dippel

cs.CV, cs.LG

Pretrained vision transformers learn structured image representations spanning spatial patch tokens and non-spatial tokens. Recent analyses suggest that non-spatial register tokens encode global scene properties such as illumination, blur, and style. Yet patch-based image decoders discard these outputs, leaving the decoder to recover image appearance from patches alone. We investigate whether retaining register features improves decoding when the complete spatial patch representation is already available. We study this question using representation autoencoders (RAEs) with frozen register-equipped encoders. Our decoder processes four register tokens together with all patch tokens through joint self-attention. Appearance probes, paired reconstruction-error analysis, and fixed-decoder register substitutions examine which global image properties are accessible from registers and how their values affect reconstructed pixels. For generation, we train a multi-head attention pooling (MHAP) predictor to recover register features from sampled patches, keeping the pretrained diffusion model and its samples unchanged. On ImageNet-1K, our ViT-B decoders achieve mean reconstruction FIDs of 0.472 with DINOv2 and 0.442 with DINOv3, reductions of 9.5% and 5.2% relative to their respective patch-only baselines. The corresponding PSNRs are 19.73 and 20.54 dB. For generation, our DINOv2 models achieve a mean FID of 1.641, 9.8% below the patch-only baseline on identical sampled patches.

Sonic-AMG: Streaming Human Motion Generation from Spatial Audio

Jingcheng Ni, Zekun Li, Zhiyang Dou, Xiaoyan Cong, Ziyao Zeng, Hongyang Du, Randall Balestriero, Taku Komura, Srinath Sridhar

cs.CV, cs.GR, cs.LG, cs.RO, cs.SD

Modeling human reactions to spatial audio requires understanding both what is heard and where it comes from. Prior work generates motion offline from complete acoustic and spatial input sequences. Humans, however, react to sounds without knowing what they will hear next. We present Sonic-AMG, to our knowledge the first framework for streaming human motion generation from spatial audio. The model encodes acoustic content and body-relative source geometry as separate tokens. Conditioned on these tokens, a causal transformer with a flow head generates continuous motion latents, from which a causal decoder reconstructs motion segments. Standard teacher-forced training derives motion histories and body-relative source locations from ground-truth motion, whereas streaming inference relies on generated motion for both, resulting in a training-inference mismatch. To reduce this history discrepancy, we introduce rehearsal training, which combines full-sequence teacher-forced supervision with supervised prediction along short autoregressive self-rollouts. To keep spatial conditioning consistent with generated motion, we propose spatial feedback, which recomputes body-relative source locations from generated root poses during both rehearsal training and inference. On SAM, Sonic-AMG achieves state-of-the-art FID and R-precision. It further supports real-time online generation of coherent reactions across sound events and source locations. Project page: https://jingchengni.com/sonic-amg/.

PAGE: Parts-Aware Guidance for Co-Speech Gesture Portrait Video Generation

Yabiao Wang, Jiafu Wu, Yicheng Xu, Teng Hu, Shengzhe Zhou, Yanxiao Sun, Jiangning Zhang, Ying Tai, Yong Liu, Ming-Hsuan Yang

cs.CV, Technology, (TCSVT), 2026., Project, page:, https://aigc-explorer.github.io/PAGE/

Co-speech gesture video generation aims to synthesize realistic, audio-driven portrait videos from a single reference image and a speech signal. Despite advances in visual quality, diffusion-based approaches still suffer from degraded local details and imperfect audio-visual alignment. We present PAGE (Parts-Aware GuidancE), a general, backbone-agnostic optimization framework for diffusion-based generation that enhances local spatial fidelity and global audio-visual consistency at both the training-objective and denoising-trajectory levels. During training, we propose Parts-Specific Reweighting (PSR), which adaptively increases supervision in semantically important regions such as lips, eyes, and hands, improving visual fidelity in perceptually critical areas. During inference, we introduce Consistency Classifier Guidance (CCG), an audio-conditioned latent classifier that identifies and corrects audio-visual inconsistencies in the generated video. We further design Differential Guidance (DG), an efficient inference strategy that refines facial and body motion separately while avoiding cross-region interference. To enable large-scale evaluation, we construct the Chinese Announcer Speech (CAS) dataset, the first comprehensive Chinese benchmark for co-speech gesture portrait video generation. Compared with the widely used PATS dataset, CAS includes substantially more identities, video clips, and scene diversity. Extensive experiments on both PATS and CAS demonstrate that PAGE achieves state-of-the-art performance in perceptual quality, gesture diversity, and audio-visual alignment. Project page: https://aigc-explorer.github.io/PAGE/.

NovaSplat: Model-Free 6D Object Pose Estimation via 3D Gaussian Splatting Onboarding and Refinement

Georgios Floros, Evangelos Sartinas, Orestis Vangelis, Maria Pateraki

cs.CV, cs.RO

6D pose estimation of novel objects without CAD models remains an open challenge: model-free methods must build the object representation from scratch during onboarding and have long trailed their model-based counterparts. We present NovaSplat, a model-free pipeline that closes this gap. From a short smartphone video captured at onboarding, we train a 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) model that serves both as a template source for detection and coarse pose estimation and as a differentiable backbone for pose refinement. Our refiner optimizes the object-to-camera pose with combined photometric and geometric losses under a continuous rotation parametrization free of gradient singularities. On a household-object benchmark, NovaSplat surpasses all model-based methods. On a challenging industrial benchmark with textureless and specular surfaces, it outperforms all model-free competitors while substantially narrowing the gap to model-based ones. We further introduce NovaSplat-Lite, an inference variant that is $3.1\times$ faster at negligible accuracy cost. Code will be made available at ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

ThinkDrag: Semantic Drag-Based Image Editing with Visual Reasoning

Armando Fortes, Tianyi Wei, Chindanai Trakantannarong, Shangchen Zhou, Xingang Pan

cs.CV

Drag-based image editing provides an intuitive interface for spatially controlled image manipulation by allowing users to specify handle and target points. Existing methods have made substantial progress through optimization-based, one-step, and feed-forward formulations, but they often interpret drag instructions as geometric displacements. This limits their effectiveness when the desired edit depends on interpreting image content and inferring a plausible semantic transformation from sparse point constraints. We introduce ThinkDrag, a unified multimodal framework for drag-based image editing with visual reasoning. ThinkDrag is trained to associate point constraints with meaningful object- and scene-level transformations, and can optionally produce an explicit reasoning trace that interprets the intended edit before image generation. This reasoning trace improves interpretability, particularly for ambiguous drags. Drag Endpoint Markers, motivated by an analysis of cross-image attention, provide an explicit correspondence signal that grounds each drag in image space. To support this framework, we construct a supervised dataset of semantic drag transformations paired with reasoning traces and introduce DragBench++, a benchmark targeting challenging drag-based editing scenarios with reference edit solutions. Experiments show that ThinkDrag achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving generation quality and plausibility while maintaining competitive point-following precision.

CoVA: Composing VAE Representations for Generative Learning

Zhaowei Chen, Yikai Ge, Linze Li, Yao Tang, Xiang Li

cs.CV

Representation alignment with pretrained visual encoders improves diffusion transformer training but requires auxiliary encoders and extra training overhead. Recent approaches instead exploit representations from the generative model's own variational autoencoder (VAE). However, reconstruction-oriented VAE features are not optimized for the semantic discrimination and spatial structure needed for generative learning. We introduce \textbf{CoVA} (\textbf{Co}mposed \textbf{VA}E Representations), a lightweight framework that composes VAE features online into stage-wise guidance for flow-matching training. A small latent composer is jointly trained with class supervision, masked latent reconstruction, and horizontal-flip consistency, yielding semantically discriminative, spatially structured dense features and sample-dependent class posteriors. CoVA aligns intermediate SiT features with the composed representations early in training, then removes the increasingly detrimental alignment and reuses detached composer posteriors for sample-dependent soft label conditioning. We further uncover a hidden confound in soft label conditioning: averaging class embeddings attenuates their magnitude, coupling semantic interpolation with conditioning strength. This attenuation alone can substantially improve FID while degrading Recall, revealing a non-semantic source of the apparent gain. CoVA corrects this confound by restoring the hard-label magnitude, isolating posterior-dependent directional semantics. CoVA adds only 8.5M training-only auxiliary parameters (1.3\% of SiT-XL/2) and introduces no inference-time overhead. On ImageNet 256$\times$256, CoVA reduces SiT-XL/2 FID from 17.2 to 7.4 (\textbf{57\%}) in 400K steps. With classifier-free guidance, it achieves an FID of \textbf{2.03} in only 400 epochs, versus 2.06 after 1,400 epochs for vanilla SiT-XL/2.

LETHE: Defending against Unauthorized Image-to-Video Generation via Value Diversity Collapse

Boheng Li, Yueming Xing, Yiming Li, Jie Zhang, Tianwei Zhang

cs.CV, cs.CR

Image-to-video (I2V) diffusion models can animate a single reference image into high-quality, subject-consistent videos, posing threats of misuse as anyone can turn personal photos into fabricated videos without consent. A promising solution is to proactively add an adversarial perturbation to the image before release, such that videos generated from it would be unfaithful or visually corrupted. However, due to an insufficient understanding of what governs how subsequent frames read the reference image in modern I2V models, existing protections struggle to offer strong protection under common perturbation budgets. In this paper, we approach the problem by exploring this underlying reading mechanism. Through a convex geometric analysis, we theoretically identify the spread of the reference value vectors as a key factor governing this reading process, and empirically find that even a mild contraction of this spread can severely degrade the generated video, making it an efficient lever for protection. Guided by this insight, we propose LETHE, a novel protection method that directly disrupts this reading process. LETHE pairs two complementary objectives, where a value collapse objective drives the reference value vectors toward uniformity, while an attention attraction objective draws subsequent frames toward these collapsed tokens to amplify corruption. We further construct two human-centric I2V protection benchmarks and conduct extensive experiments with multiple modern I2V models, which demonstrate the state-of-the-art protection performance and versatility of LETHE.

Exploring Attention Sinks in Text-to-Image Transformers from Diffusion and Autoregressive Models

Binglei Li, Mengping Yang, Zhiyu Tan, Wenxu Qian, Junping Zhang, Hao Li

cs.CV, cs.CL

Attention sinks are tokens or positions that consistently receive disproportionate attention mass, often beyond what their semantic content would suggest. Despite extensive study in large language models, their behavior and functional relevance in text-to-image (T2I) Transformers remain largely unexplored. To fill this gap and better understand how text-to-image Transformers allocate and route attention, we explore this phenomenon of text-to-image transformers across bidirectional diffusion models and causal autoregressive models. Specifically, we explore whether attention sinks exist, where they emerge, and how they functionally influence generation. Through systematic attention analysis and targeted interventions, we identify that attention sinks emerge in both bidirectional diffusion and autoregressive models but exhibit distinct patterns. The autoregressive model concentrates attention on the first and global-scale tokens, whereas the bidirectional models develop sinks near modality boundaries, within text regions, and at spatial corners. Moreover, these patterns vary across generation stages and are localized to a sparse subset of layers and heads. To assess their functional relevance, we perturb sink-related heads and token positions. It turns out that perturbing sink-ranked heads produces larger average changes than non-sink comparisons, while some sink-position perturbations have limited effects on bidirectional model scores. We further introduce register tokens to redistribute attention sinks and find that several configurations preserve or modestly improve generation performance. Overall, attention sinks are architecture-, inference-stage-, and component-dependent phenomena, providing a basis for mechanistic analysis and principled attention design in text-to-image transformers.

VideoAWR: Reinforcing Video Flow Models with Decoupled-Timestep Advantage-Weighted Rectification

Zheming Fu, Wenbin Ning, Ruizhe He, Wei Shang, Xiaoxiao Ma, Lianrui Mu, Chang Liu, Lei Wang, Siming Fu

cs.CV

Regression-based RL for flow models mandates sampling \emph{without} classifier-free guidance (CFG) to align the rectification target with the reference marginal velocity. Extending Advantage-Weighted Rectification (AWR), which dynamically scales this regression target based on reward, to video generation encounters a cold-start pathology: unguided rollouts collapse into low-fidelity regions where intra-group reward gaps remain wide but no longer track perceptual quality, rendering the optimization target arbitrary. Conversely, utilizing guided rollouts to restore fidelity, while keeping the regression target tied to the guided sampling distribution, provably triggers a geometric guidance blow-up under exponential-moving-average (EMA) updates. We propose \textbf{VideoAWR}, which resolves this tension by untying sampling from the optimization anchor. An EMA reference policy generates \emph{guided} rollouts to restore reward separability, while the trainable policy is rectified against the \emph{unguided} conditional anchor. We prove this decoupled objective is mathematically exact: the centered advantage cancels the anchor in expectation, filtering out the reward-neutral guidance offset and retaining only the reward-correlated covariance increment. Consequently, inference runs single-branch and CFG-free, yet inference-time CFG preserves its original semantics. Furthermore, we introduce \emph{Decoupled Timestep Rectification}, supervising each rollout at an independently sampled timestep without bias to improve sample efficiency. Experiments on Wan2.1-1.3B and 14B demonstrate stable optimization and superior visual and motion quality in (https://github.com/Fuzm7/FlowAWR) >>}% ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
