Can machines reason non-verbally?

Shaheer U. Saeed, Zion Tse, Gregory Slabaugh, Daniel C. Alexander, Yipeng Hu

cs.CV

Modern machines can solve equations, follow logic and reason through language, yet they still struggle with problems that require visual non-verbal reasoning. This is evident even in advanced systems such as DeepSeek, ChatGPT and Gemini, which remain limited on visual tasks such as completing abstract patterns or delineating ambiguous regions-of-interest. Unlike explicit intermediate steps in verbal reasoning which can be verified through logic or automated tests, visual reasoning involves implicit intermediate steps with no clear methods for verification. Non-verbal reasoning is therefore challenging to model and, as a result, remains a fundamental limitation of current machine intelligence. Here we introduce GenVerOpt, a general framework that unifies generation, verification and optimisation into a reasoning pipeline for machines. By generating candidate solutions, verifying their correctness, and iteratively refining them, GenVerOpt enables per-sample optimisation rather than relying solely on dataset-level trends captured during model training. This process mirrors human problem-solving where multiple possibilities are imagined, evaluated, and refined until a correct solution emerges. Across diverse domains, GenVerOpt demonstrates capabilities beyond existing systems: it surpasses humans on abstract IQ puzzles, matches clinicians in prostate cancer segmentation, exceeds experts in liver vessel reconstruction and outperforms state-of-the-art approaches in tumour boundary alignment. These results show that machine reasoning can extend beyond language, establishing visual non-verbal reasoning as a foundation for more flexible and generalisable intelligence.

Hide and Seek: HIDE Concepts to SEEK explanations for black box models

Bhanu Tokas, Rahul Nair, Rockwell Jackson, Hannah Kerner

cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG

Interpreting machine learning model predictions is a central challenge, particularly for black-box vision models. Concept-based attribution methods interpret models using human-understandable concepts. Existing concept-based methods can only interpret classification and regression models. They require access to model weights or large concept-annotated datasets, and most methods cannot provide image-level attribution. We propose Hide and Seek (HnS), a new method for concept-level attribution that does not require access to model weights or labeled concept datasets. HnS can interpret a wide range of vision models. Given a concept and image set, HnS retrieves concept-aligned images using a vision-language model, localizes the concept within each image, masks the localized region, and measures attribution from the change in the black-box model's output between the original and masked images. This enables faithful attribution by directly measuring the model's behavior and supporting local attributions. We validated HnS on CelebA and COCO-Stuff. Compared to existing methods, HnS showed better agreement with ground-truth attributions. We demonstrated the applicability of HnS on diverse tasks, including image captioning and satellite image segmentation, where no existing concept attribution method can be used. We provided our anonymous code in the supplementary material.

Images Talk Back: Guiding MMDiTs by Suppressing Image-to-Text Feedback

Yucheng Yang, Xiaoxiao Sun, Shusheng Yang, Jiakang Chen, Serena Yeung-Levy

cs.CV

Multimodal diffusion transformers (MMDiTs) allow the evolving image to update internal text representations through joint attention. These representations then guide subsequent image updates, raising a question: does this feedback help correct deviations from the prompt, or preserve them? We find that image-to-text (I2T) feedback can preserve the objects and attributes already represented in the evolving image, including errors. Transplanting I2T messages can rescue failures or disrupt successes. At matched intervention strengths, suppressing the influence of I2T feedback improves generation, whereas opposite controls degrade it. These findings motivate Semantic Feedback Guidance (SFG), which suppresses the influence of I2T feedback by guiding away from an auxiliary branch that strengthens this feedback and weakens text-to-image conditioning. The in-place variant, iSFG, directly suppresses I2T feedback and strengthens text-to-image conditioning, requiring no extra model evaluations. Temporal interventions motivate running the SFG branch during the first 20% of image sampling steps, requiring about $1.2\times$ the base NFE without classifier-free guidance (CFG). Experiments across three image-generation backbones show gains in alignment and preference-model scores. On FLUX, adding SFG to CFG raises GenEval from $0.6661$ to $0.7479$; on SD3.5M, iSFG adds $+1.33$ HPSv2.1 over CFG at zero extra NFE. The control also transfers to text-to-video and image-to-video generation, improving VBench scores. Code is available at https://github.com/yyc1215lll-creator/SFG.

InstantFusion: A Shared Latent Interface for Heterogeneous Image Generators

Xuanlang Dai, Jiazi Bu, Yujie Zhou, Yuhong Liu, Beichen Zhang, Penghui Yang, Ziyu Liu, Yuhang Zang

cs.CV

Diffusion/flow models have emerged as the de facto paradigms for image generation, giving rise to a diverse family of modern generators with increasingly heterogeneous architectures and representations. Despite their remarkable generation capabilities, the cross-model alignment of latent representations and the consistency of their underlying denoising dynamics remain underexplored. In this work, we uncover an intriguing cross-model compatibility: independently trained generators exhibit similar latent spatial organization, while directly handing off intermediate states between unaligned models can still preserve coarse visual content. Motivated by these observations, we propose InstantFusion, a lightweight shared latent interface that connects the denoising trajectories of heterogeneous image generators. Specifically, to enable flexible cross-model state translation, model-specific Latent AutoEncoders (LAEs) are introduced to project intermediate states from different generators into a shared latent space and decode them into the latent space of a target generator. This shared space is constrained by two complementary objectives: aligning intermediate states derived from the same image at matched noise levels and synchronizing their corresponding local denoising velocities. Once trained, InstantFusion allows denoising to seamlessly transition across frozen generators without requiring a dedicated mapping for every model pair. The versatility of InstantFusion is demonstrated across acceleration, multi-preference composition, cross-architecture on-policy distillation, and cross-model composition, achieving competitive performance across these diverse settings.

Towards Physically Grounded Articulated 3D Assets: A Multi-Agent Pipeline and Benchmark

Xudong Cai, Ruijie Zhu, Haoliang Shang, Ruining Li, Marc Pollefeys, Shangzhe Wu, Andrea Vedaldi, Chuanxia Zheng

cs.CV

In this paper, we study physically grounded articulated 3D asset generation---moving the goal from reproducing how objects look to capturing how they move and function. Inspired by the literature on procedural and agentic generation, we present Artisan, a system that extends the latest method Articraft by introducing a multi-agent pipeline with physical simulation feedback, enabling comprehensive attributes beyond geometry, appearance, and physically plausible articulation, such as collision-free motion, stability under gravity, and accurate mass and material properties. Furthermore, we construct a unified benchmark spanning four key aspects: spatial fidelity, physical plausibility, downstream task performance, and overall quality. The benchmark encompasses 100 reference assets from ArtVIP and LightWheel, 50 stability prompts, and 50 ABO objects with reference masses, with protocols evaluating various metrics. Through extensive evaluation of more than 10 representative models, Artisan achieves competitive spatial fidelity while attaining the lowest collision rates and highest stability rates among the evaluated methods. Together, our system and benchmark establish insights for advancing 3D generation towards physically plausible, functionally usable assets.

Algebraic Integer Quantization for Machine Learning Applications

Anthony Kostal-Vazquez, Vassil Dimitrov, Branden Butler

cs.LG

In this article, we propose algebraic integer number formats (AINF) as a framework for highly configurable and accurate compressed computation. A key property of AINF is its straightforward reduction to purely integer arithmetic, enabling error-free multiply-accumulate operations. We develop a matrix multiplication engine that exploits this property, obtaining performance within a predictable factor of the most efficient numerical counterparts on modern hardware accelerators. Another key advantage of AIQ is the freedom to select algebraic integers tailored to the numerical characteristics of a given use case, offering optimization opportunities that are fundamentally unavailable in floating-point or fixed-point formats. Motivated by these properties, we introduce algebraic integer quantization (AIQ), a lossy compression format for large language model (LLM) inference, and provide results demonstrating that AIQ achieves competitive accuracy compared to the industry-standard MX format across a diverse set of models and benchmarks at equivalent bit-per-parameter budgets. The experiments presented in this article employ algebraic integers of degree two (quadratic integers); generalizations to higher-order and, in particular, multidimensional algebraic integers promise even greater computational advantages and remain a promising direction for future work.

Kairos: Toward a Byte-Level Foundation Model for Every Modality

Fabien Furfaro

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL

Small language models trained on a single local machine leave little room for architectural trial and error. Kairos combines bidirectional gated-DeltaNet linear attention and sliding-window attention over shared projections (LiZAttention2), block-wise Attention-Residual aggregation, a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) feed-forward layer and a multi-scale byte-level codec, trained with a uniform-noise denoising objective over one byte space shared by text, image, audio, video, lidar and control streams; this report asks whether the combination optimizes at all before asking whether it is useful. We give code-derived algorithms for each component, including a cross-session recurrent-state memory gate, then run convergence sanity checks: overfittinga single 440-byte sentence at 1--15M parameters, text only, with three seeds for codec and attention choices and single-run sweeps for seven further factors. The two attention variants tested at 3\,000 steps (LiZAttention2 and window attention alone) reach a minimum loss near $10^{-3}$. After 600 steps, the mean minimum loss ranges from 0.018 (patch codec) to 0.354 (depthwise convolutional codec) with an identical ranking on three seeds. An apparent advantage of shared over separate attention projections closes with more steps or more diverse data and reverses when the projections are frozen: it is a convergence-speed effect, not a capacity effect. These checks suggest that the implementation optimizes; they say nothing about generalization, multimodal training or comparison with existing models, which a planned pretraining run is meant to address. The Discussion sizes a 200M-total/25M-active target analytically; none of its throughput figures is measured. Code is available at https://github.com/fabienfrfr/Kairos . Python package at https://pypi.org/project/kairos-fm/ .

An Input-Dependent Readout Phase for Resonant Sequence Models

Shivesh Rane

cs.LG, cs.CL

Sequence models with a fixed-size state avoid the quadratic cost of attention, but face a design choice. An input-dependent state transition gives content-dependent memory at the cost of FFT-based training, while a fixed transition keeps the O(T log T) convolutional path but reads memory the same way for every input. ResonatorLM, a recent model of the second kind, reads a bank of damped complex oscillators through a fixed learned phase per head. We make that readout phase a function of the current token, which adds input dependence to the read only, keeps the convolution exact, and costs 0.2 percent more parameters. An analysis of the untrained bank shows that phase does encode elapsed time, but an ordinary token falls below the background within 2-8 steps and no choice of damping extends linear reconstruction beyond about 16 tokens. In 6M-parameter character-level models on WikiText-2, the input-dependent phase matches the original at the training length and removes its failure beyond it: trained on 256 tokens and tested on 1024, perplexity falls from 12.81 to 3.76. We trace the failure to long-memory heads that accumulate more history than training exposed them to. The learned phase tracks the current character rather than distance and does not improve retrieval.

CircuitTree: Composing Circuits into A Syllable Transcriber

Jiankun Wei, Ewan Dunbar, Gerald Penn

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.SD

The goal of circuit discovery is to decompose a monolithic neural network into tiny modular subnetworks which are interpretable in the sense that each does an identifiable subtask. We develop a practical method for composing arbitrarily many circuits into a single model. We propose a greedy, density-scaled composition algorithm that overcomes limitations of previous attempts to compose circuits by naive union, then generalize it to $N$ circuits via CircuitTree, a new data structure built on a $\Theta(N\log N)$ tree-compose schedule. We compose circuits pruned from HuBERT for classifying individual phonemes into a syllable classifier, a capability none of the 39 phoneme circuits has individually. Chaining CircuitTrees into a hierarchy-of-circuits pipeline, we transcribe open syllables at 64.8\% accuracy, within 1.2 points of a 66.0\% ceiling that shares no circuitry at all, with 99.5\% of circuit components shared.

Behind-the-Meter Photovoltaic Visibility from Net Smart Meters: Deployment Regimes and Operational Insights

Jack Zheng, Hao Wang

cs.LG, Energy, Internet, and, Energy, System, Integration, (IEEE, EI2, 2026)., IEEE, Xplore, publication, pending

Distribution operators need visibility of behind-the-meter photovoltaic (PV) generation, yet most residential customers are observed only through net smart meters. Deep-learning-based PV disaggregation can restore this visibility, but its operational value depends on whether the model serves known customers, new customers, future periods, or new regions. We quantify this dependence using four deployment regimes that separate household transfer from temporal extrapolation, applied to Ausgrid smart-meter data with cross-dataset adaptation to the Pecan Street data. Using a fixed training protocol, we benchmark representative deep sequence architectures for PV disaggregation under regime-matched evaluation. At a fixed 48-hour context length, daylight mean absolute error (MAE) of the best model rises from 0.104~kW on seen households in the future test period to 0.185~kW on unseen households over the same period, and under a fixed training contract the deployment regime explains far more of the error variation than model choice. Under target-domain normalization, fine-tuning on five target-region households reduces source-model transfer error by 28\%--36\%. Long input windows help known-customer extrapolation, while their new-customer benefits vary by model. These findings suggest regime-matched error budgets, limited target supervision for model adaptation in new regions, and use-case-dependent context selection for energy data analytics.

GlucoPRISM: Protocol-Supervised Pretraining for Cross-Cohort Glucose Transfer

Adinath Madhavrao Dukre, Abdullah Mohammad, Yifan Lu, Eran Segal, Ahmed Metwally, Imran Razzak

cs.LG

Continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) foundation models encode each day as one vector that mixes who the person is, what happened that day and how the sensor measured it, so measurement differences travel with the representation into new cohorts. We ask whether pretraining can place the measurement component that harms transfer in known coordinates, so that deployment drops them with a fixed projection and no target data. GlucoPRISM supervises a GlucoFM backbone with response contracts, rules for how each part of the representation should change when only the day or only the sensor changes: a 64-dimensional Trait band should change with neither, a 48-dimensional State band only across days, and a 16-dimensional Sensor band should track the device. Synthetic sensor views calibrated on paired Dexcom and Libre recordings, different-day pairs and glucose-summary targets supply this supervision without clinical labels. Across twelve cross-cohort directions, dropping the Sensor band from the same frozen encoder raises mean transfer AUROC from 68.75 to 73.57, against 69.3 for GlucoFM. The gain has two parts. Into CGMacros, the one target cohort recorded with two sensor types, filtering adds 11 to 19 points, and this holds when the generator is recalibrated on half of the participants and scored on the other half. On the other eight directions filtering is neutral and the encoder itself exceeds GlucoFM by 3.5 points. The device remains linearly decodable after filtering, so the filter removes a device-related component that harms transfer rather than all measurement information. The retained bands improve window AUROC on thirteen of fourteen public task--cohort cells (Holm-adjusted p=0.031) and correlation on fourteen of fifteen clinical endpoints in an external cohort of 9,303 participants.

CacheScope: Exact-Compute Allocation for Training-Free Diffusion Acceleration

Qinhan Lyu, Jusheng Zhang, Zhixing Zhang, Hao Liu, Zijie Liang, Jin Jiang, Keze Wang

cs.LG

Training-free feature reuse accelerates diffusion transformer inference by replacing full denoising computation at selected steps with cached approximations. Existing methods mainly improve the reconstruction of skipped features, but we find that once a stable first-order reconstructor is used, increasing reconstruction complexity brings limited gains, whereas the placement of exact-computation steps has a much larger effect on fidelity. More importantly, absolute shallow-probe variation is jointly affected by input-trajectory displacement and network response strength, and therefore does not directly reflect cache risk. We address this confounding by normalizing probe-output variation with the corresponding input displacement, yielding a directional gain per unit trajectory movement. Based on this signal, we propose CacheScope, which identifies high-risk reuse steps online and selectively falls back to full denoising computation while leaving the underlying cache reconstructor unchanged. CacheScope requires no additional training, gradients, auxiliary networks, or prompt-specific offline schedule optimization. Across FLUX.1-dev, Wan2.1-T2V-1.3B, and Open-Sora 1.2, CacheScope achieves up to 3.57x acceleration and reduces median LPIPS by up to 70.1% over the strongest probe-based baseline.

Market Impact Estimation via Causal Foundation Models

Dennis Thumm, Ying Chen

cs.LG, cs.CE, q-fin.TR, November, 14-17,, 2026,, Milan,, Italy

When a trader executes a large order, the relevant question is not \emph{what will the price be?} but \emph{what would the price have been had I not traded?} Market impact is thus a causal quantity, yet classical models (Almgren--Chriss, propagators) regress price on flow, which is biased whenever informed flow moves prices through information as well as trading. We present a proof-of-concept \emph{Causal Market Simulator}: a continuous-time causal prior-data fitted network (PFN) trained on a synthetic market prior in which a hidden informational flow confounds the flow--price relation by construction, with confounding strength an exact per-episode knob. On this prior, the naive estimator's bias follows a closed form we confirm empirically (up to 68\% overestimation), while the PFN tracks the unbiased true-parameter oracle at high confounding. Its \emph{do-ablated twin}, the same model with the intervention inputs removed, collapses onto the pre-window mean. Zero-shot, the model responds at the ground-truth scale to metaorders in an ABIDES limit-order book ($+0.38$ pre-onset standard deviations against seed-replay counterfactuals), and its intervention response replicates on about 2,300 detected flow bursts in BTC/ETH/SOL perpetuals and 3,800 on Nasdaq equities, evidence consistent with, though not proof of, causal transfer. Off-prior the response degrades gracefully, and split-conformal recalibration repairs interval calibration. Transfer hinges on encoding the metaorder as added flow rather than a clamped price level, not on more training. A recurrent state-space variant closes the remaining shape gap: its impact accrues over execution duration where the transformer's stays flat, raising the correlation with measured metaorder impact from $+0.68$ to between $+0.92$ and $+0.96$, at the cost of weaker on-prior deconfounding that traces to the training recipe rather than to recurrence.

Efficient Learning of Survival Analysis Models Under Outcome-Dependent Truncation and Censoring

Itai Zilberstein, Ioannis Anagnostides, Arman Kilic, Tuomas Sandholm

cs.LG

Modern machine learning models are typically trained on data assumed to be representative of the predictive tasks the model will face in the future, but practical datasets violate this assumption. We focus on outcome-dependent truncation in which an observation appears in the training data depending on its label. Our work connects two largely distinct literatures: efficient learning under truncation and survival analysis. Existing methods for truncated survival analysis do not characterize the optimization landscape and instead rely on iterative procedures such as expectation maximization. We address this gap and prove that Cox regression can be efficiently learned under truncated and right-censored data using first-order optimization when the baseline hazard is known (despite nonconvexity). In contrast, when the baseline hazard is learned jointly, we construct identifiable and correctly specified models whose negative log-likelihood contains strict suboptimal local minima. On experiments with four survival datasets, we show that accounting for truncation reduces the prediction bias induced by naive Cox regression and extends to neural networks. We also show orders of magnitude speedups compared to standard expectation maximization.

Enriched Holomorphic Neural Networks for 2D and 3D Laplace Problems

Kalp Shah, Andrei Vlad Nicula, Mihnea Andrei B\^arsan, Robert-Stefan Sofroni, Agneya Bhardwaj, Jan Jel\'inek, Alejandro Valverde Mahou

cs.LG

Boundary-value problems for the Laplace equation develop singular behaviour near corners, edges and junctions that smooth approximators resolve poorly. We combine holomorphic neural networks with singular functions derived from the local geometry and boundary conditions. The representation is harmonic by construction, so no interior residual is needed: the network fits the smooth remainder from boundary data alone, and the enrichment terms capture the singular behaviour. Singular exponents are trainable, so the construction also applies when the local expansion is not known in advance. We extend the approach to three dimensions using independent networks evaluated along complex null directions. The enriched model is the most accurate tested method on every problem. Its margin is several orders of magnitude where the enrichment captures the singularity exactly, and a factor of about 1.7-2.1 over the next-best method on a CdZnTe radiation detector with many singular sites, in both two and three dimensions.

Transformer-Based Masked Diffusion Models Provably Learn Distributions with Latent Structure

Jingda Wu, Changxiao Cai

cs.LG, cs.IT, math.IT, math.ST, stat.ML, stat.TH

Masked diffusion models (MDMs) generate discrete data by iteratively unmasking multiple tokens in parallel using a learned mask predictor. Their output carries two errors: parallelization error due to parallel sampling, and estimation error from the mask predictor learned with finite samples. Existing theory treats these errors separately, and the available statistical bounds typically scale with the number $V^L$ of length-$L$ sequences over a vocabulary of size $V$, suffering from the curse of dimensionality. In this work, we establish end-to-end statistical guarantees showing that transformer-based MDMs learn structured distributions with a sample complexity governed by their latent structure. Specifically, we model this structure through mixtures of hidden Markov trees, which capture latent dependencies within components and heterogeneity across components. When the data distribution is well approximated by such a mixture, a transformer-based MDM with parallel unmasking schedules achieves $\varepsilon$-accurate sampling in KL divergence with $\widetilde{O}(LK_{\mathrm{par}}/\varepsilon)$ training samples, where $K_{\mathrm{par}}$ is polynomial in $L$, $V$, the number of hidden states, and the number of mixture components. The transformer network and sampling procedure require no knowledge of the tree topologies or component parameters, thereby adapting to the unknown latent structure automatically. Overall, our results provide theoretical justification for the empirical effectiveness of MDMs on real-world data.

Sample-Adaptive Zeroth-Order Post-Training

Haiyuan Wan, Yanyi Li, Weicheng Lin, Cong Fang

cs.LG

Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization offers a memory-efficient alternative to backpropagation for fine-tuning large language models. However, existing ZO methods typically sample training examples uniformly, overlooking their evolving importance and leaving avoidable estimation variance. To address this limitation, we propose Sample-Adaptive Zeroth-Order Optimization (SAZO), which learns sample importance online and adaptively allocates queries to accelerate convergence. SAZO extracts gradient-norm information from existing directional differences to estimate sample importance without access to true gradients. Furthermore, to track evolving importance from sparse minibatch feedback, SAZO maintains per-example exponential moving averages that aggregate observations across updates. The method requires no additional model evaluations and only $O(N)$ additional scalar state compared with MeZO. We prove that SAZO asymptotically matches the leading convergence term of the minimum-variance sampling oracle, and achieves a strictly faster convergence rate than standard methods. Extensive experiments across four model families demonstrate improved average downstream performance and up to $6\times$ faster convergence to the same training loss in terms of query count.

An all-transistor physical-learning transformer can be trained to perform in-context learning

Michael C. Abbott, Benjamin B. Machta

cs.LG, cond-mat.soft

Most systems trained by equilibrium propagation or coupled learning are in the same class as multi-layer perceptrons: while they may be universal function approximators, they struggle to learn associative tasks which are easy for transformers. Equilibrium propagation relies on a bidirectional flow of information through the physical system between input and output. The reverse flow is broken by more complex nonlinear systems, such as those performing multiplication which is needed to express dot-product attention. Here we explicitly restore this bidirectional flow by introducing extra circuit elements for this purpose into a double or dyadic circuit. We then use these ideas to construct an attention mechanism exploiting the exponential current law of sub-threshold MOSFET devices. We show that in simulations this circuit can be trained to perform a simple in-context learning task, despite using realistic reverse components which transmit only an approximate gradient. For this training to succeed we need cross-entropy loss (not mean squared error). We generate nudges appropriate for this loss using a novel floating circuit element, based on the same MOSFET sub-circuit used for softmax. We train the resistors in this circuit using the Lion optimiser, which can be implemented using one capacitor per parameter to store local memory of past gradient estimates.

DiffusionART: Efficient, Stable and Scalable Reinforcement of Diffusion Models

Zhenglin Wan, Jiayu Chen, Zhengfeiyang Zhang, Pengfei Zhou, Wangbo Zhao, Fangyuan Kong, Pengfei Wan, Kun Gai, Yang You

cs.LG, https://github.com/vanzll/DiffusionART

Online reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tunes diffusion models with black-box rewards, but reverse-process methods such as Flow-GRPO are limited in efficiency by poor credit assignment, while forward-process methods such as DiffusionNFT are efficient yet unstable without carefully tuned exponential-moving-average (EMA) off-policy training. We show that NFT-style objectives contain a hidden forward--reverse consistency force that joint positive--negative training cancels only partially, and that EMA stabilizes training through restoration rather than off-policy rollouts. We introduce DiffusionART (Diffusion Reinforcement with Aggregation-First Reward Tilting), which aggregates each prompt's rewards into a shared reward-improving direction for reverse-process states, removing the consistency force by construction, and adds a trust-region-masked second update for drift-adaptive restoration without EMA tuning. It requires no differentiable reward model, remains effective with fewer samples per prompt, and supports pairwise reward models (DiffusionART-Pairwise). Across image and video generation, DiffusionART trains efficiently and stably without per-task tuning. In particular, on HunyuanVideo-13B with equally weighted visual and motion quality rewards, DiffusionART reaches the late-stage mean reward levels of DiffusionNFT and Flow-GRPO-Fast with 12.02 and 11.72 times fewer GPU hours, respectively, while achieving 28.8% and 53.0% higher mean training rewards.

Realism VS Accuracy: Event Sequence Forecasting from a Generative Modeling Perspective

Dmitry Osin, Egor Surkov, Petr Mokrov, Igor Udovichenko, Ivan Karpukhin, Evgeny Burnaev, Egor Shvetsov, Alexander Korotin

cs.LG, Code:, https://github.com/xaosina/event-sequence-forecasting

Event sequences are chains of random events, each described by heterogeneous (numerical and categorical) features. The problem of event sequence forecasting occurs in finance, healthcare, business process intelligence, and other fields. In this work, we study multi-horizon forecasting of event sequences as a conditional generative modeling problem. We evaluate forecasts not only for prediction accuracy, but also for distributional realism. To this end, we present a benchmark comprising metrics for both quality aspects, strong statistical baselines, recent models adapted from adjacent fields, and curated real-world datasets. Careful evaluation reveals that no method excels at both accuracy and realism, highlighting a fundamental trade-off between the two. Finally, we introduce Wasserstein BaryBooster, an algorithm that aggregates samples from a realism-strong diffusion model into a state-of-the-art predictor, demonstrating that distributional realism can be converted into prediction accuracy. Code is available at https://github.com/xaosina/event-sequence-forecasting.

STAPLE: Static Sparse Upcycling of Pretrained Checkpoints via Embedding Modules

Ranajoy Sadhukhan, Euijin Hong, Sheng Cao, Zechun Liu, Shuowei Jin, Sha Li, Changlong Yu, Xin Liu, Qin Lu, Zhenyu Shi, Priyanka Nigam, Beidi Chen

cs.LG

Sparse computation can scale the parametric capacity of LLMs without proportionally increasing active computation, but pretraining sparse architectures remains expensive and data-hungry. Existing sparse upcycling methods mainly target Mixture-of-Experts models, where expert homogeneity, sample inefficiency, and system overheads can limit practical scalability. Static sparse memory offers predictable access and token-specific updates that can help address these challenges, but naively integrating it into pretrained computation yields poor returns. In this work, we introduce STAPLE, a recipe built upon STEM for integrating static sparse memory into pretrained models. Whereas STEM replaces FFN up-projections with token-indexed embeddings, STAPLE retains them and adds the embeddings as a residual pathway, preserving pretrained computation while learning complementary token-specific information in a scalable manner. On OLMo-2 1B, STAPLE improves over continued dense training by 4.27 points on HumanEval, 2.80 on MBPP, 2.27 on GSM8K, and 1.65 on DROP. Average performance increases with static capacity, and improvements persist on the larger OLMo-2 7B backbone. STAPLE also outperforms static sparse baselines such as STEM and an Engram-style model, and achieves a higher average score than an MoE upcycling baseline while decoding 2x faster with less than half its GPU model memory and measured GPU energy per token in our throughput-oriented decoding evaluation. Importantly, despite introducing billions of parameters late in training, our host-offloaded implementation keeps generation throughput within 1.5% and training throughput within 3% of the dense baseline, while caching only the ~5% most frequent embeddings on GPU. These results show that static sparse memory can provide a sample-efficient, scalable, and sustainable path for expanding pretrained models without sparse pretraining from scratch.

Machine Learning and Time-Series Foundation Models for Day-Ahead Electricity Forecasting in a University Building

Amey Vasulkar, Samuel Kernan Freire, Arie-Willem de Leeuw, Tadeo-Baldiri Salcedo-Rahola

cs.LG, cs.AI

Day-ahead electricity forecasts can help building operators schedule heating, ventilation, and other services around anticipated demand. Choosing a forecasting model also requires balancing accuracy with its forecast-time inputs, local training, and computational requirements. We benchmark statistical, machine-learning, recurrent neural-network, and pretrained TimesFM models for a university building. We use chronological validation and evaluate all configurations on the same 57 test days, comparing accuracy across workdays, weekends, and holidays alongside local fitting and forecasting time. Weather covariates use retrospective observations. TimesFM 3.0 with covariates achieves the lowest overall errors, reducing mean absolute error by approximately 43\% relative to weekly seasonal-naive forecasting. XGBoost remains competitive, although paired daily comparisons do not establish a clear mean-absolute-error advantage for TimesFM. TimesFM also performs strongly using only past electricity consumption. Adding covariates improves its holiday forecasts but slightly worsens workday and weekend forecasts, leaving overall mean absolute error almost unchanged. TimesFM requires no building-specific weight training, while XGBoost offers rapid local fitting and inference. The evaluated LSTM takes longer to fit without outperforming either approach. An exploratory historical evaluation reveals seasonal variation, while a separate weather-source comparison produces only small changes in the selected TimesFM configuration's accuracy. These results support pretrained TimesFM as a strong initial forecasting baseline for this building, with XGBoost as a competitive locally fitted alternative. Covariates are particularly useful when improved holiday forecasts justify the additional input preparation.

SynAct: Synergistic Active Feedback Learning for LLM Agents

Ning Liu, Ian Li, Kristina Klinkner

cs.LG

LLM agents executing multi-step workflows fail in two fundamentally different ways: recoverable slips, which further attempts can repair, and knowledge gaps, which retrying cannot resolve. Expert feedback can address the latter, but expert attention is scarce. The key challenge is therefore not whether to ask for feedback, but where feedback can change the outcome. Existing approaches either avoid expert feedback, allowing agents to reinforce their own errors, or allocate feedback at the task level without identifying consequential decisions. We present SynAct, an active learning framework that treats expert attention as the binding constraint and allocates feedback at the level of individual decisions. SynAct ranks decisions by the mutual information between the selected action and the terminal outcome across repeated rollouts, queries an expert at the highest-value decisions, and distills the resulting corrections into transferable rules that the agent can reuse without expert access at deployment. We theoretically characterize what this signal identifies under finite repeated sampling: with k rollouts and at least m observations per action, outcome-constant decisions have zero mutual information, while outcome-separating decisions have mutual information at least H2(m/k) bits, yielding exact separation for any threshold t in (0,H2(m/k)). This also explains why success-based acquisition can be blind when all rollouts fail: success is constant even when failed runs reach different outcomes. Across four agent benchmarks and four models spanning three families, SynAct improves held-out accuracy in all 16 evaluated settings, by +11.7pp on average. The same signal transfers to inference-time routing, where SynAct outperforms budget-matched cascade, uncertainty, and expert-judge routers in 15 of 16 settings, while a single targeted correction outperforms seven additional unaimed retries.

Kronecker Product Attention: Memory-Efficient KV Compression via Structured Factorization

Bahareh Nikpour, Hossein Rajabzadeh, Maryam Dialameh, Vahid Partovi Nia, Parsa Omidi

cs.LG, cs.AI

Efficient autoregressive generation is increasingly limited by the memory footprint of the key-value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length, batch size, and model depth. Existing approaches such as Multi-Query Attention (MQA), Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), Multi-Head Latent Attention (MLA), and Tensor Product Attention (TPA) reduce this cost through different trade-offs, but often sacrifice expressivity, require additional positional-encoding machinery, or impose restrictive low-rank structure. We propose Kronecker Product Attention (KPA), which represents key and value activations as sums of Kronecker products of compact, token-dependent matrix factors. KPA strictly generalizes TPA: rank-one vector-outer-product factorization arises as a degenerate Kronecker split, while non-degenerate matrix factors can represent richer block-structured patterns. We further show that a structured rotary embedding operator matched to the Kronecker representation preserves the relative-position property of RoPE while rotating only the compact factors, removing the need for a decoupled RoPE branch as in MLA. Empirically, KPA matches or exceeds TPA, MHA, MQA, GQA, and MLA across four model scales and nine downstream benchmarks under identical training conditions, while achieving up to 10x KV cache compression. The Kronecker head split is a tuneable hyperparameter that allows practitioners to navigate the compression-accuracy trade-off at design time without architectural changes.

Data-driven Optimal Sensor Placement for Aerosol Monitoring in the UK

Talha Ansar (1), Waqar Muhammad Ashraf (2), Alejandro Coca-Castro (2), Manvendra Janmaijaya (2), Ramit Debnath (3), J. Scott Hosking (2) ((1) University of Engineering, Technology, Lahore, Pakistan, (2) The Alan Turing Institute, London, UK, (3) University of Cambridge, Cambridge, UK)

cs.LG

We develop a data-driven sensor-placement framework and apply it to aerosol monitoring in the United Kingdom (UK). A trained convolutional Gaussian neural process is embedded within the DeepSensor optimisation framework. Genetic optimisation with lazy evaluation and caching reduces mean computational time by 60\% (10$\pm$3 versus 25$\pm$7 minutes) while producing sequential placement performance comparable to the greedy solver. Using the CAMS reference field to construct a retrospective oracle benchmark, Joint NLL improves by about 22\% for Joint-MI-driven genetic placement after five locations for aerosol stations are identified. Seasonal and wind-regime analyses show that agreement between model-based criteria and oracle benchmarks varies with meteorological transport conditions. These results show a computationally efficient approach for data-informed expansion of environmental observing systems.

Recurrent Harness for Agentic Continual Learning

Yuxin Xiao, Zhiyun Lu, Jianyu Wang, Yangtian Zhang, Yang Song, Qifan Wang, Bailin Wang

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL

Language model agents increasingly operate over long interaction streams, requiring online adaptation to distribution shifts through continual learning. However, existing agent harnesses typically either evolve their states only through forward information flow or rely on additional replay or validation data for harness optimization. To address this, we propose ReHarness, a recurrent harness framework for agentic continual learning that complements forward read and write with backward revision under a strictly online streaming protocol. We ask what information could have been captured differently in the harness state to better inform the current instance in the stream. Specifically, after each instance, we leverage the resulting trajectory as feedback to revise the reflections retrieved for that instance, thereby propagating feedback backward through the textual harness state. Across three continual learning benchmarks and three language model backbones, ReHarness consistently outperforms strong raw-context baselines, memory- and reflection-based methods, and prompt optimization approaches. Further analysis shows that ReHarness produces source-grounded and meaningful revisions, corrects overgeneralized or outdated experience under distribution shifts, and improves both stability and plasticity during continual learning.

Amortised Inference through One-Step Implicit Sampling

Vincent Pauline, Kirill Tamogashev, Arran Carter, Sanghyeok Choi, Stefan Bauer, Esmeralda S. Whitammer

cs.LG, stat.ML

Efficient sampling from Bayesian posterior distributions in high dimensions remains a hard problem, despite advances in theory and algorithms. Multiple steps are usually required to draw unbiased samples, for example, iterations of an MCMC algorithm or integration steps in a diffusion-based amortised sampler. However, scalable one-step amortised inference remains a challenging open problem. In this work, we build upon recent progress in data-driven generative modelling to offer a possible solution for one-step amortised sampling. Our one-step implicit sampler (OSIS) uses short-time MCMC dynamics to define a local target for the evolution of an implicitly modelled distribution. In the asymptotic limit, the modelled distribution is a fixed point of the MCMC kernel and thus samples from the target. We analyse the convergence properties of OSIS and show connections with other generative modelling and sampling algorithms. We study various design choices and show that OSIS performs well compared to multi-step amortised samplers and non-amortised MCMC baselines while being far more efficient at sampling time.

Expert-level detection of low-voltage intervals in EEG as a robust biomarker of neonatal encephalopathy

John M. O'Toole, Sean R. Mathieson, Aurel Luca, Soraia Ventura, Sean Griffin, Geraldine B. Boylan, and Robert Hogan

cs.LG, eess.SP, physics.med-ph, review

Background: Discontinuity in neonatal EEG is a critical marker of brain dysfunction but can be difficult to quantify visually. Inter-burst intervals (IBIs) are a key characteristic of discontinuity, yet their definition does not easily translate to AI detection. Objectives: To introduce low-voltage intervals (LVIs), defined exclusively by amplitude and duration, as a context-independent alternative to IBIs, develop an AI detection method for LVIs, and assess their clinical utility as a marker of brain injury. Methods: We developed a ConvNeXt model to detect LVIs and compared its performance with existing methods. We then tested the clinical relevance of detected LVIs across four applications: 1) grading background EEG abnormalities on an open benchmark dataset, 2) classifying clinical hypoxic-ischaemic encephalopathy (HIE) grades and comparing with a normal EEG dataset, 3) seizure prediction, and 4) association with seizure burden. Results: For training and validation, 654 hours of per-channel EEG from 123 neonates were reviewed and 51,162 LVIs were annotated. On held-out validation, ConvNeXt had an AUC of 0.996 and a Matthews correlation coefficient of 0.926. Model-reader agreement exceeded inter-reader agreement ($\Delta \kappa = 0.025$, 95% CI: 0.007 to 0.045, $p<0.001$), attaining expert-level equivalence. On a combined dataset of 444 neonates, percentage LVI showed a strong association with EEG grades (Spearman's $\rho=0.801$), a moderate association with HIE grades ($\rho=0.384$), a weaker association with seizure burden ($\rho=0.244$), and a strong seizure prediction capacity (AUC $=0.872$). Conclusion: AI detection of LVIs achieves expert-level equivalence, whereas quantitative features do not. Automated LVI quantification provides a robust clinical biomarker of neonatal encephalopathy associated with background EEG activity, HIE severity, and seizure risk.

Critical Mass: Structure-Disjoint Splits Drift With the Libraries They Are Built On

Tomo Oga, Devesh Shah, Cailum M. K. Stienstra, Gabriel Asher, Antonio H. de O. Fonseca, Niall O'Connor, Michael Widrich

cs.LG, cs.CE, (NeurIPS, 2026)., Workshop:, AI, Data, Readiness, for, Scientific, Discovery

Molecular machine learning evaluates out-of-distribution performance with structure disjoint splits, a construction rule fixed once at the scale its library happened to have: threshold a pairwise distance, then assign whole connected components to folds. For predicting structures from mass spectra, the field has converged on single-linkage clustering under the myopic Maximum Common Edge Subgraph (MCES) distance at a threshold of 10. This convention was set on 29,000 molecules but public libraries now hold 227,690 molecules. MCES is NP-hard per pair over a quadratically growing set, and so the criterion has never been evaluated at that scale. We propose an optimized version of this criterion, allowing for computationally feasible application on the public libraries: Admissible bounds and a union-find skip reduce $2.6 \times 10^{10}$ molecular pairs to only $1.15 \times 10^{7}$ integer programs, a $2{,}259\times$ saving, while provably producing the lossless partition. We find that at this scale the graph percolates. A single component holds $95.5\%$ of the library, capping any test set at $4.5\%$. What remains is not merely smaller, but systematically different: a held-out compound outweighs a training compound 88.1\% of the time, because a fixed absolute budget is a tighter relative tolerance on a larger molecule. Large library benchmarks using this splitting rule naively therefore conflate structural novelty with extrapolation to larger, more complex chemistry.

Primal-Dual Flow Matching for Sample-Wise Constrained Generation

Zhengyan Huan, Peter Y. Lu, Shuchin Aeron

cs.LG

We consider the problem of learning to generate data while satisfying a prescribed \emph{sample-wise} constraint with a user-specified probability of constraint satisfaction. Starting with a population-level constrained negative log-likelihood minimization and using a flow-matching (FM) parameterization, under regularity assumptions and sufficient expressivity of the velocity field family, we derive guarantees on the probability of constraint satisfaction for the corresponding primal-dual optimization algorithm. We provide a practical implementation of this approach, called Primal-Dual Flow Matching (PDFM), which has direct control over constraint satisfaction rate and requires only binary membership oracle feedback for constraint satisfaction, avoiding the need for differentiable distances, projections, or special structure of the constraint set, such as convexity. We show that PDFM improves constraint satisfaction over vanilla FM and achieves competitive performance against constraint-aware baselines while maintaining distributional fidelity. Our code is available at https://github.com/ZhengyanHuan/PDFM.

Byzantine-Resilient Federated Large Language Model via Hypergraph Message Passing

Kai Li, Hanlin Cai, Zihao Liu, Yichen Li, Jose Alcaraz Calero, Wei Ni, Falko Dressler, \"Ozg\"ur B. Akan

cs.LG, cs.CR

Federated Large Language Models (FedLLMs) are promising to deliver privacy-preserving, collaborative intelligence. The decentralized training process is vulnerable to Byzantine attacks that corrupt semantic consistency and factual resilience through malicious model updates. This paper presents a new resilience strategy that prevents semantically corrupted knowledge from propagating during federated aggregation. A defense problem is formulated to jointly maximize global accuracy and suppress manipulated model updates, enabling trustworthy knowledge aggregation without accessing private local data. To strengthen the resilience of FedLLMs, we develop a novel Hypergraph Message Passing Graph Autoencoder (HMP-GAE) framework, where low-rank model updates are represented as nodes in a hypergraph and high-order semantic relationships among multiple agents are exploited through iterative message passing. By leveraging historical checkpoints and cross-agent relational reasoning, HMP-GAE identifies and suppresses semantically inconsistent updates while preserving knowledge alignment among agents throughout federated training. HMP-GAE is implemented using Qwen-2.5 and Llama-3.2 as pretrained FedLLM backbones and evaluated on AG News and Yahoo! Answers datasets. Compared to existing defenses, HMP-GAE improves global accuracy by $1.21$\%, reduces final-model classification semantic entropy by $44.08$\%, and lowers perplexity by $13.34$\%. HMP-GAE outperforms the GAE-based defense by improving the accuracy $1.39$\%, reducing the semantic entropy by $54.5$\%, and lowering perplexity by $35.5$\%.

Spatial Context or Memory? Sizing the Inputs of Neural Surrogates for Partially Observed Fields

Ruige Kong

cs.LG, https://anonymous.4open.science/r/memory-space-forecasting-5633

Neural surrogates that forecast a physical field from sparse, noisy sensors read a window of nearby sensors and a history of past snapshots. Practitioners choose both by comparing fitted models with each other. Such comparisons cannot tell a model's shortfall from missing information. We measure the trade-off between window and history against the Bayes-optimal error of the same readings. An exact Gaussian analysis shows that noisy or sparse observation of a field with local dynamics makes the optimal forecast read distant sensors only through spatial correlation. On a forced linear field with a known optimum, trained models that read only the window come within 1% of it. We then train such models on viscous Burgers flow and the chaotic Kuramoto-Sivashinsky (KS) equation, with every deciding comparison fixed before any test evaluation. In every field except KS, eight snapshots remove a smaller fraction of the one-snapshot error as the lead time, the number of steps to the forecast target, grows. In KS, for windows up to one correlation length, they remove a larger fraction. This lead-time inversion persists across sensor densities, three architectures and a two-dimensional domain. It shrinks as damping lowers the Lyapunov exponent. Damping also removes the linear instability of KS. We therefore tie the inversion to instability and leave chaos and linear amplification unseparated. For surrogate design the results give two rules. Size the window in correlation lengths of the field. Split a fixed input budget between window and history for the lead time the model will serve, because the best split moves toward wider windows as the lead time grows. In Burgers flow the split chosen at lead time one ends with 13.5 times the best error at lead time eight.

A Categorical Atlas of Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning: Cones, Orbits, and Gauges

Xiaoyu Li, Zhizhou Sha, Chiwun Yang, Dai Shi

cs.LG

Parameter-efficient fine-tuning has hundreds of methods, usually compared by parameter count and benchmark score. We compare them as maps. A pretrained checkpoint is a base point in weight space, and a weight-space method is a smooth map from a small parameter space that starts at that point. These maps form a slice category in which the frozen model is initial and full fine-tuning terminal. Three invariants of the map do most of the work. Its image near the base point governs expressivity, through the first-order image, the dimension, a min-cut bound on rank and whether that point is smooth or an apex. Its fibres, with the optimizer's metric, govern training dynamics. Base change moves the base point and covers splitting initialisations, quantisation, restarts and merging. We prove, for example, that zero-initialised LoRA, and Householder reflection adaptation at its default start under stated rank and parity conditions, begin at the apex of a cone; that no exact initialisation lets LoRA reach an update of rank above twice its own; that exactly orthogonal methods never change a weight's singular values in exact arithmetic, however often they are merged; that under Adam with no epsilon, weight decay or clipping and one shared schedule, LoRA+ from the standard zero initialisation follows exactly the trajectory of plain LoRA with its scale multiplied by the learning-rate ratio; that Adam with shared hyperparameters respects only the signed permutations inside LoRA's continuous symmetry group of full-rank factors; and that the activation function decides when a scaling adapter folds into preceding weights. We classify 382 methods from 138 papers by seven coordinates read off their formulas, with 403 simulation arrows and 485 obstructions. Every numbered result is proved in full, and results due to others are credited where we re-derive them.

Kernelized Parameter Sensitivity Atlases for Amortized Conditional Interpretability

Davide Wiest, Ahanaf Hasan Ariq, Abdul Monaf Chowdhury

cs.LG, cs.CV, stat.ML

Gradient-based attribution provides input-specific information about model parameters, but computing it via backpropagation for many samples is expensive, while static importance scores discard input dependence. We derive KPSA (Kernelized Parameter Sensitivity Atlas) from a block decomposition of the empirical neural tangent kernel. It measures normalized parameter-group sensitivity, embeds those sources in an explicit query space, and uses a lightweight kernel to estimate sensitivities for unseen inputs. We derive bounds that separate finite-source from irreducible representation error and give margin-based top-$k$ recovery. This paper studies two interpretability applications. Firstly, KPSA identifies parameter groups with high susceptibility to perturbations across vision, forecasting, and protein-modeling setups. Across a fivefold range of parameter budgets ($0.02$-$0.1\%$), its selected groups attain $39$-$85\%$ of the perturbation susceptibility of a first-order backpropagation oracle. In vision and forecasting, these parameter groups form sparse sensitivity circuits that extend across depth and vary systematically with the input. Secondly, we use KPSA on an activation-attribution problem for MLP features in transformers. It retains $81\%$ of the gap between a backpropagation oracle and a constant-sensitivity baseline on vision while increasing batched query-time throughput from 551 to 2,041 images/s and reducing peak GPU memory from 4.55 to 0.44GB. On the protein-modeling task, it recovers $68\%$ of this gap. An ablation of KPSA's kernel aggregation shows that nearest-neighbor retrieval in KPSA space can remain competitive. The empirics show that compact kernel atlases can store useful input-dependent parameter sensitivity while amortizing repeated gradient attribution, a step toward scalable conditional interpretability. Code can be found at https://github.com/Axym-Labs/kpsa .

KITE: A Knowledge-Injected Temporal Encoder for Time-Series Event Forecasting

Jonas Petersen, Pratik Jawahar, Riccardo Maggioni, Gian-Alessandro Lombardi, Matteo Cozzi, Marek Gajewski, Federico Martelli, Camilla Mazzoleni, Philipp Christian Petersen

cs.LG, cs.AI

Forecasting rare, high-cost events in multivariate time-series systems is hard because their precursors are seldom observed, so a purely data-driven model has little to learn from. The engineers who run these systems, however, hold knowledge that precedes such events, information absent from the scarce labels. We call this a semantic prior and inject it into a self-supervised event forecaster through two switchable mechanisms, a covariate mechanism and an attention-bias mechanism, each the identity at its null, so one architecture serves every prior type with no per-dataset architecture search. A prior helps when it supplies information the observed window does not already contain: in a short context an informative prior beats no prior in-domain (pooled h-AUROC over the no-prior baseline: a periodicity clock +14%, p<0.001; a conservation residual +47% at a 0.25 label fraction), and a working prior transfers out-of-domain (+53%), substituting for feature transfer, while priors the window already recovers stay inert and the attention-bias mechanism shows no measurable gain. KITE is a switchable, attributable route to contextual conditioning, and a substrate for scaling toward cross-domain, pretrained time-series foundation world models that carry their governing structure explicitly.

Neural expectation-maximization and meta-models

Zhuolin Fu

cs.LG

We interpret Transformer networks as learned approximations to a hybrid probabilistic-inference procedure that combines max-product Loopy Belief Propagation (LBP) with Expectation--Maximization (EM). In this view, a Transformer performs inference in partially observed Markov Random Fields (MRFs) whose structure and parameters are not given a priori but are inferred from data. We show that self-attention implements a differentiable approximation to max-product LBP on an input-conditioned, dynamically structured MRF, with attention weights encoding learned structural affinities. We further establish an EM correspondence at the level of depth: each layer updates latent representations in an E-step-like forward pass, while the transition $\theta^{(l)} \to \theta^{(l+1)}$ across layers acts as an implicit M-step. Building on this interpretation, we introduce \emph{meta-models}, a design paradigm in which inputs generate instance-specific parameters, unifying mechanisms such as self-attention, mixture-of-experts, and gated units within a single framework. Empirical results support the proposed perspective.

Static Gradient Attribution Underperforms a Density-Matched Random Mask on Loss-Based Forgetting Within LoRA B-Matrix

Yu Sun, Junwei Zhou, Zuodong Xiang, Yike Zhang, Pengcheng Xu, Xinyu Guan, Ruoyun Ma, and Hailu Xu

cs.LG

Parameter-efficient continual learning updates a restricted subset of adapter parameters to incorporate sequential tasks while limiting catastrophic forgetting. Gradient attribution is a common rule for selecting that subset, but it requires an additional backward pass over calibration data. We identify a parameter-pool confound in comparisons between initialization-time gradient masks and unrestricted random masks under standard zero-initialized LoRA ($B=0$): the gradient with respect to $A$ is identically zero ($\partial\mathcal{L}/\partial A=0$), so gradient masks select strictly from the $B$ matrix whereas unrestricted random masks draw from both $A$ and $B$. Such a comparison changes both the selection rule and the candidate parameter pool. Isolating the selection criterion by restricting both approaches to the same parameter pool, we find a negative result: a uniform random mask consistently achieves lower loss-based backward transfer than gradient attribution across seven seeds on Qwen2.5-3B. The effect is concentrated on the medical task, which contributes 80\% of the aggregate; code and math move in the same direction, and legal shows no reliable difference. This advantage is consistent with a stability--plasticity trade-off under the tested task sequence, in which random masking slightly reduces single-task fitting. On the two discrete-option tasks (Medical and Legal), the mean accuracy-BWT difference is not statistically distinguishable from zero.

NV-Tesseract-AD: Curriculum-Enhanced Conditional Diffusion for Multivariate Time-Series Anomaly Detection

Aditi Gautam, Manikandan Ravikiran, Saira Qureshi, Sena Fatma Ekiz, Charan Jagwani, Mahmoud Elhadidy, and Sanjeev Roka

cs.LG

We propose NV-Tesseract-AD, a curriculum learning-enhanced conditional diffusion framework for multivariate time-series anomaly detection. Our central hypothesis is that reconstruction difficulty can be explicitly structured along two complementary axes, masking severity and diffusion noise level, and that training from easier to harder tasks along both axes simultaneously stabilizes optimization and improves residual discriminability. We integrate a Transformer-based diffusion backbone with a three-stage dual curriculum over masking ratios and diffusion timesteps, retaining anomalous samples throughout training and relying on structured corruption to drive self-supervised representation learning. We evaluate NV-Tesseract-AD under two regimes: in-distribution training on the TSB-UAD-M corpus and strict zero-shot transfer to ten EasyTSAD benchmark families without per-dataset tuning. The dual-axis curriculum produces masking-invariant reconstruction, confirmed by a near-zero loss gap between complementary masking strategies within the first training stage, and yields an approximately 3.7-fold improvement in F1 and a 56 percent reduction in MSE relative to a non-curriculum baseline. Zero-shot performance reaches F1 = 0.984 on NAB and F1 = 0.948 on NormA. Weaker results on heterogeneous families are traced to unsupervised threshold calibration rather than reconstruction quality, identifying calibration as the dominant remaining detection bottleneck. Accelerated inference via DPM-Solver++ reduces sampling cost by a factor of 3.5 to 5.7 at the dataset level but couples to thresholding behavior, establishing that the sampler and threshold must be validated jointly. Together, these findings demonstrate that aligning diffusion denoising difficulty with curriculum learning principles provides a principled and practical approach for robust anomaly detection in complex multivariate time series.

Provenance-grouped protocol for evaluating machine learning in perovskite photovoltaics

Qiwei Han, Chi Zhou

cs.LG, benchmark

Machine-learning models for perovskite solar cells are typically evaluated using random data splits, which we show systematically leak information through massive internal duplication. Because nearly two-thirds of curated devices share their exact recipe with another, random splits primarily reward the mere recall of historical data rather than testing true physical generalisation. To solve this, we mapped the institutional provenance of the Perovskite Database and established a rigorous hierarchy of grouped splits that progressively isolate duplicate devices, publications, and institutions. Benchmarking thirteen models establishes new state-of-the-art performance across all splits and reveals a critical regime shift: while tree ensembles dominate random splits by recognising familiar recipes, their ranking collapses on genuinely unseen institutions and future discoveries, where composition-aware neural architectures excel. Furthermore, feature importance demonstrates that the device architecture and fabrication methodology dictate final efficiency just as strongly as the absorber chemistry. This standardised protocol empowers the community to separate memorisation from true physical discovery.

REDaCT: Collapse-Free Unlearning in Large Reasoning Models

Joshua Maccoby, Wendy Hui Wang

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, Copyright, 2026, Association, for, Computational, Linguistics, (ACL)., Licensed, under, CC, BY, 4.0

Unlearning in large reasoning models (LRMs) requires removing forget-set information from both the reasoning trace and the final answer. Existing approaches achieve this objective at the cost of reasoning collapse, where the model fails to produce a coherent chain-of-thought (CoT) and does not properly terminate its reasoning process. To address this challenge, we propose REDaCT (REgenerate via Detection and Corrective Tuning), an inference-time unlearning framework for LRMs. REDaCT consists of two key components: (1) a binary leak detector that identifies sentences in the reasoning trace that leak forget-set information, and (2) an embedding optimization module that learns a corrective embedding offline, such that when injected at the first detected leak during inference, it guides subsequent reasoning towards sentences that do not leak forget-set information. Experimental results show that REDaCT completely avoids reasoning collapse, substantially improving reasoning preservation compared to state-of-the-art unlearning methods while maintaining perfect answer forgetting and near-perfect CoT forgetting. Our code is available at: https://github.com/jmaccoby/redact.

Coordinate-Free Automatic Differentiation

Alex Guerra, Joshua Aduol, Yaqian Tang, Daniel Williams, Ryan P. Adams, Peter Orbanz

cs.LG, cs.MS, math.DG

Automatic differentiation (AD) is a cornerstone of modern machine learning. Since all AD frameworks implicitly fix a basis of Euclidean space to define gradients and Jacobians, they natively process mathematical objects that are arrays of a fixed set of coordinates. This makes it awkward to express objects such as rotations, volume forms, and curvatures that arise, for example, in applications of AI to science. Bespoke workarounds lack reusability. We show that (i) a single, basis-independent derivative operator can represent many geometric differential operations, and (ii) this operator can be expressed entirely in terms of existing AD primitives. This reusable transformation can be composed with JAX batching, compilation, and hardware acceleration. Geometry enters as algebraic data. We experimentally demonstrate its reuse across different manifold operators, its composition through a spacetime inverse-problem pipeline, and its use in optimization over different rotor subgroups.

Meta-Reflective Evolution Effectively Improves Production AI Systems

Seung Won Wilson Yoo, Chang Zeng, Aditi Somayajula, Sahithi Ankireddy, Rohan Kosalge, Avishree Khare, John Mitchell, Aditya G. Parameswaran, Rajhans Samdani, Anupam Datta

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.SE

Large Language Model (LLM)-guided evolutionary optimization has successfully improved prompts, programs, and even entire AI systems. Most optimization methods select a candidate from the search frontier, then mutate it in one LLM pass based only on its failures. This focus on the frontier can stall on production AI systems, where isolated failures reveal little about complex, hidden interactions. We introduce CoCoEvolve (Cortex Code Evolve), an evolutionary optimizer built around what we call meta-reflection, wherein an agentic mutator (Cortex Code) selectively retrieves evidence from the entire evolutionary history and probes the live system before verifying and proposing mutations. We apply CoCoEvolve to optimize three classes of production AI systems: data agents, LLM-powered data pipelines, and data engineering workflows. On DABStep, CoCoEvolve tops the public leaderboard, reaching 92.8% held-out hard accuracy compared to 78.4% for Meta-Harness and 30.4% for GEPA under matched conditions. Across six LLM data pipeline workloads, it outperforms every evaluated baseline. On data-eng-bench, CoCoEvolve lifts test-set solve rate from 59.1% to 77.4%, past the best baseline of 73.6%. It also has 11 percentage points higher accuracy than GEPA on HotpotQA while remaining competitive with specialized optimizers on established mathematical and systems benchmarks. Matched ablations confirm that each component of CoCoEvolve contributes to these gains, demonstrating the value of meta-reflection for optimizing production AI systems.

The Expressive Power of Random-Feature MPNNs Across Graph Sizes

Robert R. Nerem, Mitchell Black, Antonis Vasileiou, Christopher Morris, Yusu Wang

cs.LG

Continuous random node features increase the expressivity of message-passing neural networks (MPNNs), but existing theoretical analyses are not fine-grained enough to capture their limitations, particularly in approximating functions across graph sizes or under a fixed number of random bits. We develop a population-level framework for size-uniform approximation. For MPNNs with a graph-size-independent Lipschitz bound in their initial features, including standard mean, max, and order-normalized sum architectures, we show that continuous random features can be quantized to finitely many colors with arbitrarily small uniform error in expected output. This reduction lets us transfer limitations on finite-color distinguishability to limitations on size-uniform approximation with continuous random features. We use this framework to prove that these MPNNs are not size-uniformly universal, analyze the expressivity gains from colors and independent trials, and establish incomparability between random-color MPNNs and higher-order Weisfeiler-Leman methods. We also give finite-color MPNN constructions for tree subgraph isomorphism via color coding and a local MST task.

Deploying RDMA for Large-Scale Inference and Training Workloads

Yibo Huang, Yi Cui, Yukai Huang, Huaping Zhou, Jiancong Tong, Zhaoyu Wu, Gen Miao, Haoran Wei, Boyan Ding, Zhuo Jiang, Jianxi Ye, Yibo Zhu, Chuanxiong Guo, Yang Xu, Jie Wu

cs.LG, cs.DC, cs.NI, cs.SY, eess.SY

RDMA can reduce communication overhead in distributed machine learning systems, but realizing these benefits in production requires more than replacing TCP. Variable-size messages, concurrent connections, bursty incasts, and service failures introduce challenges that hardware offload alone cannot address. This paper reports our experience deploying ByteExpress, a software communication layer over hardware RDMA, in ByteDance's parameter-server-based inference and training systems. We present three lessons from the deployment: efficient RDMA requires buffer management across application and transport layers; scalable communication requires coordinated management of connections, receive capacity, and outstanding data; and service availability requires recovery above the transport layer. ByteExpress combines an RDMA-aware allocator, zero-copy transfers, QP multiplexing, receive-credit flow control, a software send window, and RPC-assisted recovery. In production inference, ByteExpress reduces mean and P99 model parameter fetch latency by 35.7% and 43.7%, respectively, and total CPU usage by 10.69% compared with kernel TCP. In training, ByteExpress reduces P99 model parameter push latency by up to 74.0% compared with TCP, with no PFC pauses observed at runtime.

Inverse Synthesis of Shock Time Series from Shock Response Spectra Using Ensembles of Conditional Variational Autoencoders

Adam Watts, Charles Young, Ryan Bowering, Andrew Jeon, Destry Newton

cs.LG, eess.SP

The shock response spectrum (SRS) characterizes the peak responses of single-degree-of-freedom systems to transient acceleration. Because the mapping from an acceleration time series (waveform) to its SRS is many-to-one, inverse synthesis seeks candidate waveforms consistent with a prescribed spectrum. We extend an earlier conditional variational autoencoder (CVAE) framework with a recurrent decoder that predicts multiscale wavelet coefficients and reconstructs waveforms through an inverse discrete wavelet transform. Individual models exhibit limited variability under latent sampling, motivating an ensemble of independently trained models spanning different mother wavelets and decomposition levels. Each member produces a separate candidate waveform for the same target SRS. Evaluation on four held-out benchmark datasets shows lower mean and median spectral errors for the illustrated wavelet model than for the earlier direct time-domain CVAE and a sum-of-decayed-sinusoids (SDS) baseline. A conditioning study examines frequency-grid and encoding choices. Comparisons with published inverse-SRS methods demonstrate superior performance across four holdout benchmark datasets (two operational shock datasets, one earthquake dataset, and one synthetic shock dataset) and competitive performance on synthetic targets reported by Brake. Single-model inference requires approximately 0.1-0.6 ms per waveform during batched inference compared with 5-8 s for SDS, excluding offline training. This low inference cost enables rapid generation of distinct SRS-compatible candidate waveforms across the ensemble, although their physical suitability requires assessment beyond spectral agreement.

TreeHCA: Hindsight-Guided Exploration and Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn LLM Agents

Debajoy Mukherjee, Jeremy Carleton, Sushil Vemuri, Srinivas Shakkottai, Dileep Kalathil

cs.LG

Outcome-supervised group-relative policy optimization (GRPO) assigns the same advantage to every token in an LLM agent trajectory, leaving useful and unhelpful decisions undifferentiated. Hindsight credit assignment (HCA) can refine this feedback, but its estimates rely on trajectories containing informative alternatives at consequential histories. We introduce TreeHCA, which couples hindsight-guided exploration with hindsight credit assignment. TreeHCA uses changes in reference-answer likelihood to revisit intermediate histories and sample alternative continuations, forming a tree of reasoning and tool interactions. Because this exploration changes the distribution of sampled continuations, we derive the corresponding inverse-hindsight importance correction, including an exact finite-pool identity. We use this structure in a normalized tree backup that propagates terminal outcomes into turn-level advantages. This shared hindsight structure is TreeHCA's central design principle, linking informative exploration with hindsight-guided credit assignment. Across seven question-answering benchmarks and three Qwen backbones, TreeHCA outperforms all baselines in aggregate exact match, validating the effectiveness of paired exploration and credit assignment for multi-turn LLM agent training. Our code is available at github.com/TheBlackCat22/TreeHCA.

Reasoning post-training draws on offline demonstrations and policy-generated rollouts, yet conventional supervised fine-tuning (SFT) followed by reinforcement learning (RL) separates their objectives and limits continued reuse of collected responses. As new responses enter the buffer, optimization retains the same regression objective without coordinating separate SFT and RL objectives or requiring stored behavior-policy probabilities, importance weighting, or a learned critic. Restricting the available sources gives offline, on-policy, and off-policy variants, while CBPO-Unified combines them.
