When Generative Models Act as Simulators

Sevan Brodjian, Michael Hobley, Pietro Perona

cs.LG, cs.AI

A simulator summarizes an environment in a state that users can read and change. Generative models learn representations of the world without explicit specification, but offer no built-in way to edit that world's state. We investigate the conditions under which a generative model can act as a simulator through $\textit{editability}$: whether changing a model's latent representation produces the next-step outputs we get by editing a simulator's state. Plainly stated, our goal is to find a mapping between a generative model's latent state and the simulator state, where the model is trained on observations alone and the mapping is learned afterward. We test two kinds of mappings, both learned from observations paired with simulator states. In one, we train probes to decode the simulator state from the model, then steer along them. In the other, we learn the mapping directly with an inverse map, which can succeed even where steering fails. We study both in two contrasting environments: the discrete board game Othello, and Rayworld, our own continuous world of moving discs seen through partial renderings, closer to physical scenes and video. We find that which mapping succeeds depends strongly on the environment, even though the state is decodable in every variant. In Othello, editability falls smoothly as the game is simplified, and even small rule changes have large consequences. In Rayworld, probes fail to steer along the simulator's continuous state, yet an inverse map sets the model from that state anyway. A model can therefore be driven by a simulator it may have never learned.

Reinforcement Learning via Fixed-point Flow Matching

Denis Blessing, Onur Celik, Gerhard Neumann

cs.LG, cs.RO

In maximum entropy and behavior-constrained reinforcement learning (RL), the optimal policy is given by a Boltzmann distribution. Generating samples from this distribution can be challenging, as it is often complex and multimodal. While flow policies trained with flow matching provide a powerful framework for representing such distributions, they traditionally rely on access to training data, which is generally unavailable in online RL. To address this limitation, we propose fixed-point flow matching (FFM), a data-free procedure for training and fine-tuning flow policies in RL. FFM leverages the target flow identity to formulate the actor update as a fixed-point iteration, utilizing a flow-matching-style regression objective where the critic's action gradients serve as regression targets. We show that the optimal policy is a fixed point of this iteration. Through extensive experiments, we demonstrate that FFM effectively fine-tunes pretrained flow policies in the offline-to-online setting and enables efficient and accurate training of flow policies from scratch in online RL. These results establish fixed-point flow matching as a principled approach for learning expressive flow-based policies without requiring training data.

Robust Langevin Sampling under State-Dependent Heavy-Tailed Noise with Applications to LLM Fine-Tuning

M. Berk Sahin, Ege C. Kaya, Arda Fazla, Behzad Sharif, Abolfazl Hashemi

cs.LG

Existing convergence guarantees for non-log-concave sampling with stochastic gradient Langevin Monte Carlo (SG-LMC) typically assume globally Lipschitz gradients and uniformly bounded noise variance. Both assumptions can fail even in elementary sampling problems, and relaxing them better reflects practical applications such as Bayesian fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs). We relax both assumptions through generalized smoothness and our proposed state-dependent heavy-tailed oracle with finite $p$-th moments, $1<p\leq 2$. The oracle permits infinite variance when $p<2$ and includes the Blum-Gladyshev (BG-0) condition. We study these relaxations separately and jointly, proposing three methods. Under globally Lipschitz gradients, robust LMC (R-LMC) handles the proposed oracle through geometric-median centering and residual clipping. With exact gradients, adaptive LMC (A-LMC) controls discretization error under generalized smoothness. Robust adaptive LMC (RA-LMC) addresses both relaxations through robust estimation and a noise-aware step size. For all three methods, we establish non-asymptotic convergence guarantees in Fisher information (FI) without assuming dissipativity or a functional inequality. Synthetic experiments demonstrate FI convergence and the benefit of each mechanism. Bayesian fine-tuning experiments with LLMs and vision-language models support our oracle model and show that RA-LMC matches or outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in accuracy and uncertainty quantification.

HART: Hybrid Attention-RWKV Time series Foundation Model

Qingwen Lin, Boyan Xu, Shifeng Xie, Zhifeng Hao, Keli Zhang, Ruichu Cai

cs.LG

Non-autoregressive multi-step forecasting (NAF) enables time series foundation models to predict an entire future horizon in a single forward pass. Despite its strong sequence modeling capabilities, RWKV-7 exhibits long-horizon degradation in our NAF experiments. In this setup, future patches are represented as missing inputs, requiring the recurrent state to evolve across the prediction horizon without new observations. Our analysis of RWKV-7's generalized delta rule suggests a mechanism we term Placeholder Idling Decay (PID): repeated state transitions can attenuate historical information when placeholder-driven writes provide insufficient compensation. Layer-wise analysis reveals reduced state-injection norms in most layers, while transition-matrix norms remain similar between historical and prediction regions, consistent with PID. Motivated by this analysis, we propose HART, a Hybrid Attention-RWKV Time-series Foundation Model that alternates attention and RWKV blocks, combining direct access to historical tokens with recurrent temporal modeling. Controlled experiments on GIFT-Eval and TIME-Benchmark show that HART improves over pure RWKV at medium and long horizons while maintaining comparable short-horizon performance, and achieves better aggregate performance than a comparably sized pure Transformer. With 36M parameters, HART achieves long-horizon MASE comparable to Toto-2.0-1B on GIFT-Eval (0.752 vs. 0.754), using approximately 1/28 of its parameters.

CoGameReg: Cooperative-Game-Based Collaborative Semi-Supervised Regression for Seismic Reservoir Property Prediction

Zhiyu Guo, Jiwei Jia, Lei Li

cs.LG

Accurate estimation of reservoir properties from seismic data remains challenging when well-log labels are sparse but three-dimensional seismic observations are abundant. We present CoGameReg, a cooperative-game-based semi-supervised regression framework that uses contribution-weighted predictions of heterogeneous regressors to construct shared pseudo-labels. This design makes each regressor's influence explicit in the learning targets generated for unlabeled seismic samples under sparse well control. The Tri-Game Shapley mechanism combines a rank-MSE performance game with a pairwise consistency game to estimate nonnegative regressor weights from labeled training data. These weights remain fixed during collaborative training and are reused for final prediction fusion. Candidate pseudo-labels are screened using a common local k-nearest-neighbor error-reduction criterion before retraining. We evaluate CoGameReg on 10 public regression datasets, a synthetic three-dimensional P-wave velocity prediction task, and a field water-saturation application. The benchmark advantage is most pronounced at the 2.5% labeled-data ratio and decreases as supervision increases. CoGameReg obtains the best blind-well metrics in the synthetic experiment. In the field application, predictions clipped to 0-100% yield a pooled coefficient of determination of 0.5942, a root mean square error of 5.7441 percentage points, and a Pearson correlation of 0.8265 across three blind wells. CoGameReg has the highest Pearson correlation among the four field methods. The results support the use of CoGameReg to integrate sparse well labels with dense seismic attributes, with performance varying across metrics and well locations.

Personalized Federated Direct Preference Optimization via LoRA Adapters

Zhanhong Jiang, Soumik Sarkar

cs.LG

Federated Direct Preference Optimization (FedDPO) enables privacy-preserving alignment of language models across distributed agents without centralizing sensitive preference data. However, existing FedDPO analysis assumes all clients converge to a single shared policy, which is fundamentally at odds with the heterogeneous preference distributions present in practice. We propose Personalized FedDPO (pFedDPO), in which each client maintains a small private LoRA adapter atop a shared global base model. In pFedDPO, the base model is synchronized via FedAvg each round, while LoRA adapters remain local and capture client-specific preference patterns. We prove that this decomposition replaces the gradient diversity $\kappa^2$ in the FedDPO bound with a residual heterogeneity $\kappa^2_{\mathrm{eff}}$, which is no larger than $\kappa^2$ when adapters absorb client-specific directions, at the cost of an additive adapter-bias term that vanishes as adapters converge. Experiments on preference datasets show that pFedDPO consistently attains a lower gradient norm than vanilla FedDPO while remaining competitive in training loss and per-client reward accuracy.

GIST: Generator-state-Informed Supervision for Tabular Foundation Models

Zhangyang Peng, Jingze Shi, Tongyi Zhang, Yuyu Luo

cs.LG

Tabular foundation models (TFMs) based on prior-data fitted networks typically use sampled hard query labels, leaving avoidable sampling variability in pretraining. A key observation is that intermediate generator states can reveal label uncertainty beyond a sampled outcome. We propose Generator-state-Informed Supervision for TFMs (GIST), which uses these states to average query losses over conditional label distributions. GIST jointly designs task generation and query supervision, using entropy-calibrated encodings to obtain tractable, input-consistent targets on supported label-dependent branches. We show that this averaging preserves the population objective and yields no larger gradient covariance at fixed parameters within each generating distribution. Under matched pretraining schedules, GIST improves mean rank across three architectures on both TabArena and TALENT without changing architecture or inference. Across these benchmarks and architectures, GIST wins 62.1% of paired dataset-level comparisons against the corresponding hard-label controls.

Controlled-Minimax: Stabilizing Adversarial Optimization via Control Theory

Tianjiao Luo, Jianfei Chen, Jun Zhu

cs.LG

Minimax optimization is fundamental to adversarial learning yet suffers from severe stability challenges, often manifesting as persistent limit cycles or divergence. This paper investigates these instabilities through the lens of continuous-time dynamical systems. By modeling training dynamics as a second-order Ordinary Differential Equation (ODE), we rigorously identify the root cause of non-convergence: the problematic eigenstructure of the loss function's Hessian near equilibrium points. To address this, we introduce Controlled-Minimax, a novel stabilization framework that reformulates the optimization trajectory as a controllable physical system. Unlike heuristic regularization techniques, our approach derives a specific feedback controller that provides theoretical guarantees of local exponential stability, regardless of the underlying Hessian spectrum. Consequently, our method stabilizes general minimax problems where traditional gradient descent-ascent methods fail. Extensive experiments on pathological benchmarks including the Dirac-GAN, 2D Gaussian mixtures, and Swiss Roll datasets, as well as high-resolution image synthesis with StyleGAN2-ada, demonstrate that Controlled-Minimax effectively suppresses oscillatory behavior while ensuring fast and robust convergence.

DataCupid: Stable Matching Repair for Training Data Acquisition with Delayed Feedback

He Sun, Junyuan Mao, Wenhao Jiang, Mingjun Xiao, Shinan Liu

cs.LG, cs.IT, math.IT

Matching repair after utility changes can reuse valid observations, but new evaluations must respect matching capacity and may return out of order. We study announced invalidations with bounded, possibly reward-dependent reporting delays and pending evaluations that do not occupy future matching capacity. Residualload repair (RLR) combines sound outcome certificates with batched matching schedules for unequal sample deficits. Its complete dyadic quota schedule admits a sharp issue-round bound with coefficient cb < 1.607 relative to the terminal residual endpoint load, together with separate delay and evidence-lifetime bounds. RLR-P monitors complete issue-order prefixes without changing the sampling plan before acceptance. With a deterministic pure oracle, a single-repair coupling from the same drained initial state guarantees no increase in certification time, issued observations, or time to a reusable checkpoint. We also derive an information-load lower bound. On fixed priority-star markets with unknown Bernoulli gaps, PrioritySearch attains the history-adjusted first-order constant for expected certification time as {\delta} {\sim} 0, under hypothesis-independent linear history scaling and sublogarithmic reporting delays. Paired simulations and measured-feedback replay quantify the trade-offs: in the replay, RLR-P has lower mean capped update-certification time than a monitored width-based baseline, at higher oracle CPU cost.

Diffusion Precipitation Generation with Medium-range Weather Forecasting

Guowen Li, Yang Liu, Mengxuan Chen, Jiashun Cheng, Haoyuan Liang, Shilei Cao, Yujie Wang, Juepeng Zheng, Fang Wang, Yerong Feng, Meng Jin, Hong Cheng, Yutong Lu, Haohuan Fu

cs.LG

Accurate daily precipitation forecasts are important for weather-sensitive decisions. Although recent Weather Foundation Models (WFMs) have achieved significant progress, they are trained on reanalysis data, which exhibit regional and intensity-dependent biases. Since retraining WFMs on observation-based targets is costly, we study Dynamics-Precipitation Decoupling, a plug-and-play framework that uses a separately trained generator to map non-precipitation forecasts from a frozen weather model to observation-aligned daily precipitation. Its key challenges are to capture heterogeneous rainfall distributions and align daily accumulations with atmospheric signals that evolve across space and time. To address them, we train a diffusion generator conditional on atmospheric states and satellite-gauge estimates of precipitation. A multi-scale recurrent encoder summarizes atmospheric evolution, while cross-attention aligns atmospheric and precipitation features during denoising. Extensive evaluation of various backbones, including Pangu-Weather, GraphCast, and HRES, on global and regional forecasting shows improved daily precipitation accuracy and event detection, with better representation of localized accumulation patterns. Global ensemble evaluation shows lower Brier scores than ENS and training-from-scratch GenCast at all reported lead times, with lower CRPS in most comparisons. Remarkably, a heavy-rainfall case study using 2,288 stations in China further supports improved daily accumulation patterns and event detection against ground observations.

Gaussian-Width Bounds for Offline-to-Online Linear Bandits

Kushagra Chandak, Volodymyr Tkachuk, Xiaoqi Tan, Csaba Szepesv\'ari

cs.LG

We study how offline data can help online learning in stochastic linear bandits. We focus on partially covered offline data, where only $k$ of the $d$ feature directions are poorly covered. Existing analyses of algorithms based on phased elimination and optimism give regret bounds of order $\widetilde O(\sqrt{dkT})$ in this regime. We show that this dependence arises from loose control of the reward prediction error, and obtain tighter bounds using concentration of Lipschitz functions of Gaussian vectors. Based on this analysis, we develop a phased elimination algorithm with regret $\widetilde O(k\sqrt T)$. For the unit Euclidean ball, we prove a matching regret lower bound under partial coverage, as well as matching upper and lower bounds under uniform offline coverage. We also study pure exploration with offline data, providing algorithms and matching upper and lower bounds on simple regret for fixed-design algorithms and also for adaptive algorithms under partial and uniform coverage of offline data.

Evidential Multimodal Transformers with Dempster-Shafer Fusion for Emotion Recognition

Muhammad Usama, Yeoreum Jung

cs.LG, figures,, plus, 5, pages, of, supplementary, material

Softmax-based multimodal emotion recognition methods often assign high confidence even when sensor data are corrupted or contradictory, for example when facial expressions suggest positive valence while vocal prosody indicates negative arousal. We present an Evidential Multimodal Transformer with Dempster-Shafer Fusion (EMT-DS) that addresses this problem through formal uncertainty quantification. EMT-DS models predictions as Subjective Opinions (Dirichlet distributions), separating epistemic uncertainty (lack of evidence) from aleatoric uncertainty (sensor noise). Modality-specific transformer encoders produce belief mass distributions that are fused with Dempster's Rule of Combination, which normalizes the joint belief mass by a conflict coefficient 1/(1-c), so that contradictory evidence is explicitly quantified as conflict and the fused prediction becomes diffuse rather than being forced into an unjustified consensus. Under subject-independent and speaker-independent evaluation on four benchmarks, EMT-DS achieves 84.3% accuracy on IEMOCAP versus 81.7% for the best baseline (p < 0.01), 82.4% on DEAP under leave-one-subject-out evaluation versus 78.1%, and 96.8% on the highly imbalanced KEMDy20, and it improves accuracy on CMU-MOSEI by 2.4 points over the strongest evidential baseline. In controlled ablation, it reduces Expected Calibration Error from 0.153 (softmax transformer) to 0.042. It maintains 71.2% accuracy under severe Gaussian noise on audio features (sigma = 1.0), where attention baselines drop to 45.3%, and degrades gracefully when entire modalities are absent. The fusion step has O(MK^2) complexity, compared with O(M^2 d^2) for cross-modal attention. The learned conflict metric also flags likely errors: reviewing 20% of samples captures 80% of misclassifications, which supports selective prediction in clinical and safety-critical systems.

Generalized Proximal Policy Optimization: Unifying LLM RL Algorithms from the Perspective of Divergence

Zhicheng Cai, Xinyuan Guo, Hanlin Wu, Weinan Dai, Yuyan Zhou, Wei-Ying Ma, Ya-Qin Zhang, Hao Zhou

cs.LG, cs.AI

Reinforcement learning has become the foundational paradigm for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. Yet the dominant approach, Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), relies on a geometrically degenerate clipping proxy that cannot adapt its resistance to policy shifts, leading to premature clipping and degraded exploration. Subsequent variants alleviate these issues through fragmented and heuristic modifications. We propose Generalized Proximal Policy Optimization (GPPO), a principled and unified framework for understanding and designing LLM RL algorithms through the lens of divergence. We establish a generalized $f$-divergence trust-region theory with a monotonic performance improvement guarantee, and show that different $f$-divergences induce distinct geometries and policy update dynamics. We further introduce novel estimator topologies that provide more faithful divergence estimates than the conventional single-sample Monte Carlo estimation. Under this framework, PPO and recent variants such as DAPO, DPPO, and RIPO emerge as special cases, establishing a principled two-dimensional design space of {Divergence $\times$ Estimator}. Extensive experiments on eight competition-level math and coding benchmarks show that both dimensions are critical. The best-performing GPPO instantiation surpasses standard GRPO by up to 50% and 14% on math and coding, delivering SOTA performance.

Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization: Closing the State Distribution Gap in Long-Horizon LLM RL

Zhicheng Cai, Weinan Dai, Yuyan Zhou, Wei-Ying Ma, Ya-Qin Zhang, Hao Zhou

cs.LG

Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a dominant paradigm for enhancing LLMs' reasoning capabilities. Yet long-horizon autoregressive rollouts introduce a fundamental limitation: local policy mismatch can compound into severe state distribution shift over trajectories. Existing LLM RL methods, such as GRPO and GSPO, control policy mismatch at the token or sequence level, but overlook this trajectory-induced shift, causing training instability and eventual collapse as the horizon grows. To close the state distribution gap, we propose Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization (TAPO), a principled framework that theoretically derives the state-distribution correction from the off-policy policy gradient and incorporates it into the RL objective. TAPO further employs a geometric-mean estimator for variance reduction, together with a length-aware dynamic clipping that prevents overly deviated updates across the trajectory. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TAPO significantly surpasses existing LLM RL algorithms across eight competition-level benchmarks, achieving up to 48% and 12% improvement over GRPO on mathematical reasoning and algorithmic coding, with substantially enhanced training stability.

Low-Rank Kolmogorov-Arnold Generator Flows for Interpretable Operator Learning

Minjong Cheon

cs.LG, physics.comp-ph

Neural operators that learn physical laws from data should generalize beyond their training distribution and expose the learned law in closed form. The Kolmogorov-Arnold neural operator achieves both for position-dependent dynamics by learning a dense pseudo-differential symbol over all positions and frequencies, at a cost that grows with the square of the resolution and with closed forms that must be read out by hand. This dense symbol is unnecessary: a finite-order variable-coefficient differential operator has a symbol whose separation rank is bounded by the number of its coefficient functions, independently of the resolution, and the symbols that this network learns on its own benchmarks have rank two. We introduce the Kolmogorov-Arnold flow (KAFlow), which learns the generator of a physical operator as a low-rank symbol built from univariate Kolmogorov-Arnold functions, fixes the gauge of these functions so that every term has a unique place, integrates the dynamics with exact exponential integrators, and distills its closed form automatically. On the synthetic benchmarks of the Kolmogorov-Arnold neural operator, KAFlow lowers the out-of-distribution loss by 3.4 to 11 times and recovers every coefficient to within 10^-4 without symbolic templates, reaching the precision floor of the data. On long-horizon quantum dynamics, it lowers the state infidelity by up to 6 orders of magnitude, and by 30 times when trained only on position and momentum measurements. KAFlow also learns a variable-order fractional operator 15 times more accurately than existing operator networks and discovers nonlocal, fractional and non-polynomial dispersion relations on which sparse regression fails.

Target-Constrained Pooling for Trajectory Extrapolation

Ning Wang, Claire Cardie, Sainyam Galhotra

cs.LG

Trajectory extrapolation from a partial target trajectory is underdetermined, but completed related trajectories provide information about the unresolved continuation. We propose target-constrained pooling (TCP), which separates the finite-horizon endpoint from continuation shape and estimates that endpoint from two complementary source signals. Uniform pooling captures final response levels, while matched-increment pooling captures the remaining change after aligning source and target states. TCP selects representative sources, projects both endpoint estimates onto a target-consistent region, converts them into corrections of the free forecast, and controls their influence through strength calibration and degradation-aware weighting. Across 25,245 targets from five domains and both forecasting directions, TCP reduces mean RMSE by 35.8% to 42.1% for all ten target-only forecasters and improves all 100 forecaster, dataset, and direction comparisons. The two endpoint signals consistently outperform either alone, while representative selection remains within 0.64% to 1.02% of the full donor pool using 12.7x fewer donor inputs and 138.8x fewer matching distance computations.

Towards Easier-to-Tune Training Algorithms: New Results from AlgoPerf

Priya Kasimbeg, Ahmed Khaled, Aahlad Chandrabhatta, Yifan Wang, Andy Liu, Niccol\`o Ajroldi, David Tweedle, Xingyu (Alice) Yang, Isaac Patole, Frank Schneider, and George E. Dahl

cs.LG

Evaluating deep learning optimizer is challenging because confounding factors such as hyperparameter tuning and optimizer-model co-design often obscure true algorithmic performance. We present results of the updated AlgoPerf V1 benchmark, in which we evaluate a new set of training algorithms on wall-clock time-to-target across nine workloads on fixed hardware. This report focuses on the results of the benchmark's self-tuning ruleset, which requires submissions to use a single configuration across all workloads. Out of the 13 submissions in PyTorch and JAX, Schedule-Free AdamW ranked first. In addition to the wall-clock scores, we also analyze the trade-offs between step efficiency and wall-clock execution, revealing that matrix-preconditioned and two-loop methods achieve good sample efficiency but require more aggressive computational optimizations to be competitive in runtime. CB-Orchestrator: Adaptive Workflow Optimization for LLM Agents via Contextual Bandits

Jiahang Sun, Zhiwei Shang, Zhipiao Liu, Hongwei Yang, Guoqing Xie, Shuang Qiu, Zhongxiang Dai

cs.LG, Interpretability,, and, Agents

Large Language Model (LLM) agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in tackling complex tasks through agentic workflows. However, manually designing these workflows is labor-intensive and lacks flexibility to adapt to diverse queries. While automated workflow optimization is a promising direction, existing methods often incur expensive API costs during training-phase feedback collection. Furthermore, they fail to adaptively reuse high-quality workflows, which creates a bottleneck for both performance and cost-effectiveness. To address these limitations, we propose CB-Orchestrator, an adaptive framework that decouples workflow generation from selection. In the first stage, we construct a diverse pool of candidate workflows via evolutionary search. In the second stage, we formulate workflow selection as a Contextual Bandit problem, which enables sample-efficient learning by balancing exploration and exploitation, thereby significantly reducing the feedback required for training. During inference, the model adaptively selects the optimal workflow tailored to each query. Evaluations across five diverse benchmarks demonstrate that CB-Orchestrator consistently outperforms all baselines. Notably, on the MATH dataset, CB-Orchestrator achieves better performance than strong recent baselines while reducing training-phase API token overhead by no less than 40.55% and total end-to-end training (including workflow pool generation) token overhead by no less than 29.73%.

Double-Take: Improving Capacity, Binding, and Selectivity In Long-Context Within Bidirectional Memory

Vakul Raghavan, Utkarsh Grover, Mingyang Mao, Chengwen Wang, Xiaomin Lin

cs.LG, q-bio.GN, q-bio.QM

Processing a long sequence does not guarantee that distant information remains usable. As context grows, a fixed-size memory must retain many associations, connect information across positions, and preserve relevant structure without being overwhelmed by everything it observes. These are distinct requirements: a memory may store more information without representing the relationships needed for retrieval, and it may transport those relationships without preserving the ones that matter. Thus, the challenge is not simply extending context length, but constructing a memory that remains usable as context grows. We study this problem in large-chunk test-time training (TTT) and identify two basic failures: single-step writes accumulate interference, limiting capacity, while self-associative writes cannot form cross-position bindings. We introduce Double-Take (DT), which repairs these failures with multi-step unpinned updates and cross-position mixing, and adds bidirectional change-sensitive writes for selective retention. Capacity alone leaves end-to-end recall at the $0.10$ chance floor; adding binding raises it to $0.92$. Trained only on $32\,\mathrm{kb}$ genomic contexts, DT reaches $0.927$ AUROC at $131\,\mathrm{kb}$ and $0.856$ near $1\,\mathrm{Mb}$, while transferring zero-shot from human to mouse. Together, these results identify capacity, binding, and selectivity as three requirements for usable long-range memory.

Human and LLM Facilitation in Collaborative Design Meetings: A Computational Behavioral Comparison

Mohammed Azeez Khan, Aaron Dsouza, Ashutosh Mishra, Ummul Faiz Z. Bibi, Neha K. Nair, and Amar Kumar Behera

cs.LG

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being explored as facilitators of group discussion and collaborative work, but the behavioral differences between LLM-generated and human facilitation remain insufficiently characterized. We compare human Project Manager facilitation turns from scenario-based collaborative design meetings with Llama 3.1 8B Instruct responses across 199 paired contexts from the AMI Meeting Corpus. The analysis covers five confirmatory dimensions: contextual semantic distance, a lexical elaboration proxy, empathyand-hedge lexical density, topic divergence, and phase-vocabulary alignment. An exploratory directiveness measure failed domain validation and is not interpreted. The sampled human Project Manager turns were higher on contextual semantic distance (r = 0.644) and on empathyand-hedge lexical density (r = 0.203). The second contrast is carried mainly by hedge terms, so we read it as lexical hedging rather than psychological empathy. Llama-generated facilitation was higher on topic divergence (r = 0.703), the lexical elaboration proxy (r = 0.288; lengthconfounded), and instruction-conditioned phase-vocabulary alignment (r = 0.382). A secondary no-phase check left the Llama-higher phase contrast in place. The system prompt still requested a facilitation move, so that check is not evidence of spontaneous phase awareness. These differences should not be interpreted as a ranking of facilitation quality. The resulting behavioral profile provides a reference point for later comparisons of LLM facilitators, human facilitators, prompts, models, and collaborative settings.

Can Failed Trajectories Reveal Where Agents Go Wrong? Agentic Failure Attribution Without Step-Level Labels

Do Nguyen-Thanh, Junlin Fang, Bing Li, Zhen Fang, Sean Du

cs.LG

Failure attribution for LLM agents aims to identify decisive steps responsible for task failure, which is critical for debugging and improving agentic systems. Existing methods rely on costly LLM prompting, step-level error annotations, or verified successful trajectories that lack failure signals for assistance. In this paper, we study failure attribution from step-unlabeled failure trajectories, where each trajectory is known to have failed but its decisive step is unknown. We propose SOAP (Spectral scOring with Attention-guided Propagation), an effective framework requiring neither step-level training labels nor a separate success-only reference set. SOAP first constructs a spectral reference space from LLM representations and assigns each step a base error score according to its projection behavior. Importantly, later steps may exhibit stronger failure signals because they inherit an earlier mistake, causing independent scorers to favor downstream consequences over the decisive error. To address this downstream contamination, SOAP derives soft step dependencies from attention patterns and propagates downstream error evidence toward its likely upstream sources. Extensive experiments show that SOAP consistently improves attribution performance over competitive baselines.

Scaling RNA Foundation Models through Iterative Refinement

Pengan Chen, Liang Hong, Xinyuan Liu, Han Huang, Ziqian Lin, Jiayang Chen, Yimin Fan, Jiyue Jiang, Yu Li

cs.LG

Biological function in RNA is encoded across scales, from local sequence motifs to long-range structural dependencies. Conventional RNA foundation models couple parameter capacity and inference depth within a fixed architecture, prescribing a fixed computation graph for each inference. Here we present Eco-RNA, an 80M-parameter RNA foundation model that makes inference depth adjustable through learned iterative refinement. Its shared, input-conditioned Transformer core refines sequence representations through repeated application. The iteration count is selected after training and controls effective depth at fixed model parameters. We train across recurrent depths with masked reconstruction, state regularization, and a one-sided loss that anchors deeper reconstruction to a shallow reference. On the 70-assay RNAGym fitness benchmark, increasing refinement from one to four iterations raises aggregate absolute Spearman correlation from 0.242 to 0.286, with the gain retained through eight iterations. At four iterations, Eco-RNA surpasses the current SOTA Evo 2 40B on all three aggregate fitness metrics despite 500x fewer parameters. Its iteration configurations advance the compute-performance Pareto frontier among the evaluated models. It also outperforms the evaluated baselines in non-coding RNA family classification, RNA secondary-structure prediction, and chemical probing profile prediction. These results establish internal inference computation as an effective scaling dimension for RNA foundation models.

Continually Post-training Small Models via Synthetic Self-distillation

Woosung Koh, Hyunsoo Lee, Kyungjae Lee, Haeju Park, Dahyun Lee, Dasol Hwang, Moontae Lee

cs.LG, cs.AI

Continual post-training faces a dual challenge of acquisition and retention: models must learn new capabilities while preserving prior knowledge. While Self-Distillation Fine-Tuning (SDFT) mitigates this using self-supervision with in-context demonstrations, it relies on strong in-context learning and instruction-following abilities. Consequently, SDFT fails on smaller models critical for low-latency, on-device use. To enable continual learning at smaller scales, we first identify the root cause of failure. We show that poor acquisition and forgetting correlate linearly with the post-trained model's distribution shift, revealing the necessity of in-distribution learning. We thus introduce Synthetic Self-Distillation (SynthSelf), imitating SDFT's in-distribution rewrite by outsourcing the task to a minimally larger, same-family aligner. Coupled with off-policy supervision and rejection sampling, this provides the high-quality supervision small models require without breaking their original distribution. Evaluations spanning 4 acquisition datasets (including a mixture) and 14 retention benchmarks show SynthSelf consistently outperforms 6 methods (including off- and on-policy distillation) across 3 small model scales. Extensive further analyses confirm SynthSelf paves the way toward effective small-model continual learning.

WaSA: Wavelet Scattering Autoencoders for Unsupervised Offline Detection of Malicious Clients in Federated Learning

Alessandro Licciardi

cs.LG, Systems, (COINS), 2026,, Bologna,, Italy;, September, 7-9,, 2026

Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative model training without exposing raw data, yet remains vulnerable to malicious clients injecting poisoned datasets. Existing defenses are typically reactive (operating during training) or supervised (requiring prior knowledge of attack types) - assumptions that rarely hold in open-ended IoT deployments. We propose WaSA, a fully unsupervised, offline framework for malicious client detection. Before training, each client transmits a single, privacy-preserving spectral summary of its data to the server. A denoising autoencoder, trained solely on clean auxiliary data, scores clients based on their deviation from the learned benign manifold, identifying corruptions regardless of the attack type. WaSA requires no attack labels, generalizes to unseen anomalies, and introduces a Boltzmann sampling strategy for client selection that smoothly interpolates between hard exclusion and standard aggregation. Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100 under diverse attack scenarios (up to 90 % malicious clients) demonstrate consistent improvements over supervised baselines and robust aggregation methods, with negligible communication overhead. Code is available at https://github.com/alelicciardi99/wavelet_autoencoders_malicious.

Does Learning Recurrent Networks Matter in Partially Observable RL?

Zekang Wang, Zhe He, Gaojie Jin

cs.LG, cs.AI

Reinforcement learning (RL) under partial observability typically re- lies on recurrent networks to encode temporal information, with the recurrent module trained jointly with the policy or value function. This raises a natural question: is learning the recurrent network actually necessary? In this paper, we investigate whether a frozen recurrent network can provide sufficient memory for solving par- tially observable Markov decision processes (POMDPs). Motivated by the theoretical foundations of Reservoir Computing (RC) and Generalized Reservoir Computing (GRC), we study a simple archi- tecture that combines a fixed recurrent network with a trainable nonlinear output module. We evaluate this approach across tasks with varying memory demands, observation modalities, and action spaces. Surprisingly, frozen recurrent networks support recall over hundreds of steps and achieve competitive control performance across diverse POMDPs, while using less than half the number of trainable parameters required by their fully trainable counterparts. These results suggest that learning the recurrent memory module is not always necessary for solving POMDPs. Frozen recurrent networks therefore provide a simple, parameter-efficient, and sur- prisingly strong baseline for partially observable RL. Our source code is available at https://github.com/Aphlios/FRN-POMDP

S$^3$R: Fast and Faithful RL Rollouts via Self-Sparse Speculative Decoding

Yuhang He, Hongyu Ge, Zihan Lin, Siyi Liu, Yongqi Zhang

cs.LG, cs.CL

Fast rollout generation for long-context reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) requires reducing KV-cache traffic without changing the response groups used for policy optimization. In our profiled RLVR workloads, attention accounts for 72--76\% of autoregressive decoding kernel time. Sparse attention reduces these reads, but sampling directly from a sparse policy changes the response groups used for learning. For group-relative optimization, we show that even exact response-wise reweighting does not generally recover the expected dense-policy update because the advantage of each response depends on the rewards of other group members. We propose Self-Sparse Speculative Rollouts (S$^3$R). It uses sparse attention to propose tokens, then generates responses through full-attention verification and exact speculative sampling. Drafting and verification share the same policy parameter, eliminating the need to train or update a separate drafter. The resulting response groups follow the dense-policy distribution. Under S$^3$R, we characterize speedup conditions and the costs of drafting and verification. We found that dense verification still requires the complete historical KV cache. A KV-aware scheduler therefore limits KV-cache repeated offloading and restoration while maintaining rollout parallelism. % OR thrashing In a matched ablation with an aggressive sparse drafting budget, S$^3$R maintains stable training while direct sparse sampling with training-side correction becomes unstable. On long-context mathematical reasoning, S$^3$R achieves $1.39\times~1.35\times$ the end-to-end training throughput of autoregressive rollouts on Qwen3 model, with comparable task performance.

Rethinking Relative Policy Differences Transfer of On-Policy Distillation

Rong Wu, Daocheng Fu, Yu Yang, Xuemeng Yang, Siqi Li, Shu Zou, Yao Zhu, Botian Shi, Bo Zhang, Cong Zhang, Pinlong Cai, Jianbiao Mei

cs.LG

On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a target policy on its own rollouts using token-level supervision from a stronger teacher, but typically relies on the teacher being substantially more capable than the target. Recent work relaxes this requirement by transferring the relative policy difference between an expert and a reference policy rather than the expert's absolute distribution. Yet it remains unclear which differences provide useful supervision and which targets can benefit from them. We develop a unified view in which the log-probability ratio between a proxy expert and a proxy reference defines an implicit reward for KL-regularized policy optimization. This formulation induces an on-policy update for the target, recovers conventional OPD as a special case, and enables any-to-any transfer across models that differ in scale, training stage or generation. Our systematic experiments yield three findings. First, a stronger expert is not necessarily a better source of supervision: differences induced by task-specific training, including reinforcement learning and supervised fine-tuning, transfer consistently, whereas those arising from model scale alone transfer weakly or not at all. Second, the expert and reference serve complementary roles: the expert provides the capability to be transferred, while the reference filters out shared behavior and isolates the relevant residual. Third, even a useful difference does not transfer to every target: success requires the induced update to move the target toward the expert and generalize beyond sampled rollouts. Raw policy distance does not predict transferability, whereas an appropriate reference can rescue otherwise unsuccessful transfer. These results characterize when relative policy differences provide effective supervision for OPD.

How Much Computation Does Every Forecast Need? Structured Block Skipping in Two Time-Series Foundation Models

Max Bourgeat

cs.LG

Time-series foundation models use the same network depth for every input, although forecasting windows differ in structure and difficulty. We test whether whole Transformer blocks can be removed at inference time in two pretrained models: Chronos-Bolt and TimesFM 2.5. Our bypasses avoid the original block computation and preserve the dense model exactly when all blocks are enabled. Masks are selected only on calibration windows and evaluated on 1,088 held-out windows from ETTh1, Traffic, Weather, and Exchange. At moderate sparsity, Chronos-Bolt with 7/11 candidate blocks and TimesFM with 16/20 blocks show little change in mean relative forecasting quality across datasets while reducing median A100 batch-1 latency by 11.2% and 13.4%. More aggressive TimesFM skipping accelerates inference further but substantially harms Traffic and Weather. Across both models, different equal-budget masks win on different windows, and a retrospective oracle significantly improves over the best static mask on every dataset. However, dataset-specific masks transfer inconsistently, and Chronos representations do not support reliable unseen-domain routing. These results establish cross-architecture structured redundancy and input-dependent opportunity, but not a deployable dynamic inference method.

Monarch-MoE: Structured Experts for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning

Xi Zhang, Boyu Diao, Zesen Chen, Ruiqi Liu, Hangda Liu, Yongjun Xu

cs.LG

Learning new tasks in multimodal continual instruction tuning can cause catastrophic forgetting of previously acquired knowledge. Although multi-expert LoRA distributes learning across experts, updates to a dense low-rank expert can still affect many output features. We introduce Monarch-MoE, which replaces the dense factors in LoRA experts with block-diagonal factors connected by a fixed permutation. This structure provides blockwise gradient isolation: individual block-pair updates affect only one output block, while connections between all input and output blocks are maintained. To make these compact experts practical on memory-limited hardware, we combine batched computation, fused forward operators, and an optimized backward pass. Across three continual-learning methods evaluated on MLLM-DCL and UCIT with LLaVA-1.5-7B, Monarch-MoE improves first-task retention while reducing adapter parameters by approximately 75% and 50%, respectively. In the main comparison, first-task forgetting is as low as 0.52 percentage points on MLLM-DCL and 0.10 on UCIT, with method-dependent trade-offs in final performance. Our implementation supports fine-tuning and inference on a single 24 GB RTX 4090, with lower measured memory use and competitive runtime in the evaluated settings. Together, these algorithmic and systems improvements make structured experts a practical option for continual adaptation on memory-limited hardware.

Tensor-based Fusion of Multi-modal Data for Predicting Cancer Risk Levels

Ali Anaissi, Yuanzhe Jia, Qishun Peng, Weidong Huang, Ali Braytee, Junaid Akram

cs.LG, q-bio.QM

Cancer diagnosis and treatment increasingly rely on multi-modal diagnostic technologies that generate high-dimensional datasets. Although these datasets are rich in pathological information, they pose considerable challenges in interpretation, dimensionality reduction, and clinical applicability. Conventional machine learning approaches often fail to extract meaningful cross-modal features, thereby limiting predictive accuracy in patient risk stratification. To address these limitations, this paper proposes an unsupervised machine learning framework that integrates feature extraction, tensor decomposition, and clustering to improve cancer risk prediction. The framework employs tensor decomposition to fuse heterogeneous genomic and clinical data, enabling the extraction of latent, biologically meaningful features that outperform unimodal feature sets. Multiple feature extraction strategies are investigated, and the resulting features are subsequently clustered using a range of clustering algorithms. Comparative evaluations based on internal and external metrics are then used to identify the most effective configurations. The framework is validated on LinkedOmics data comprising 11,158 patients across 32 cancer types. For patient risk stratification, clinically relevant attributes such as tumor purity, pathological stage, and survival outcomes serve as ground-truth indicators. Experimental results demonstrate the superiority of tSVD+GMM, which consistently outperforms other configurations across clinical benchmarks.

ReSFlow: Rectifying Categorical Flows on Statistical Manifolds for One-Step Generation

Xiaohuan Jia, Renzhe Xu, Xiao Wang, Jiayun Wu, Shaohua Fan

cs.LG, cs.AI

Generative modeling for discrete data has been significantly advanced by learning continuous vector fields on the statistical manifolds to transport simple priors to target discrete distributions. However, because these continuous-time flows are not constrained to follow geodesics on the statistical manifold, thereby requiring multi-step numerical integration for simulation and leading to substantial computational costs during inference. In this work, drawing inspiration from the "straight and fast" properties of Rectified Flow in continuous Euclidean space, we propose the Rectified Statistical Flow (ReSFlow) model, which enables one-step categorical generation on statistical manifolds by explicitly learning geodesic trajectories that adhere to the intrinsic Riemannian geometry. By rectifying the flow on the statistical manifold, we theoretically guarantee that trajectories between prior and target distributions approximate geodesics. This rectification significantly reduces the transport cost by aligning the learned trajectories directly along geodesics on the statistical manifold, allowing the model to map pure noise to target discrete data in a single step. Through extensive experiments on diverse real-world generative tasks, ranging from image and text to biological domains, we demonstrate ReSFlow's superior one-step generation quality, which truly embodies geodesically optimal and rapid categorical generation. Our code is available at https://github.com/xiaohuanjia/ReSFlow.

Learning Schwarzschild Geodesic Motion with Lagrangian Neural Networks

Sukru Caglar, Sena Kalabalik, Abdullah Umut Hamzaogullari, Arkadas Ozakin

cs.LG, gr-qc

Lagrangian Neural Networks (LNNs) learn dynamics from trajectory data by parameterizing a scalar Lagrangian and deriving accelerations through the Euler--Lagrange equation, providing a variational inductive bias that improves physical consistency over purely data-driven models. LNNs have recently been extended to general relativistic settings, leaving Schwarzschild geodesics as an open challenge. Training on complex relativistic systems is hindered by the diversity of the dynamics, as well as numerical instabilities and the difficulty of adequately sampling physically relevant, diverse orbital regimes. We introduce two coordinated improvements: a decoupled position--velocity normalization scheme that independently rescales each coordinate and its time derivatives, reweighting acceleration targets to balance contributions across all channels; and an energy-constrained log-uniformly sampled dataset generation strategy informed by the innermost stable circular orbit (ISCO) structure. With these improvements, our model accurately reproduces perihelion precession and relativistic time dilation, conserves energy and angular momentum to within a few percent even after completing multiple orbits around the central mass and predicts orbital trajectories across eight orbital scenarios in close, medium, and far-field regimes. These results establish that LNNs, augmented with physically motivated normalization and sampling, can reliably learn geodesic dynamics in curved spacetime. Our work also represents the first successful training of LNNs on Schwarzschild geodesics and offers a promising direction for data-driven modeling of complex relativistic systems.

State-OPD: State-Grounded On-Policy Distillation for Multi-Turn Agents

Zhentao Zhang, Yanzhao Zheng, Yanlin Liu, Yuanqiang Yu, Tianze Xu, Chao Ma, Jihuai Zhu, Lelun Wu, Baohua Dong, Hangcheng Zhu, Ruohui Huang, Gang Yu

cs.LG

On-Policy Distillation (OPD) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for transferring reasoning capabilities from frontier models to smaller models, while extending its success to multi-turn agentic environments remains challenging. We study agentic OPD through interaction states, distinguishing the objective task and executed action-observation record from the internal semantic trajectory of student reasoning. Through this lens, we identify two roles of student reasoning in multi-turn OPD: prior reasoning distorts teacher supervision, while current reasoning shapes the action alternatives sampled at the same state. Specifically, at matched interaction states, removing prior student reasoning improves teacher continuation success and changes the teacher's token-level targets, with larger continuation gains at later turns. Meanwhile, resampling complete reasoning-action responses yields greater action diversity at the same state than resampling actions under a fixed reasoning trace. We introduce State-Grounded On-Policy Distillation, a framework that grounds teacher supervision in interaction states and reallocates the sampling budget to generate multiple complete reasoning-action responses at states with high response entropy, while retaining full-history student generation. Comprehensive evaluations across ALFWorld, ScienceWorld and WebShop demonstrate that State-OPD achieves the highest macro-average success rate among the evaluated methods for both Qwen3-1.7B and Qwen3-4B students. Notably, for Qwen3-4B, State-OPD raises macro-average success from 26.07\% with the strongest baseline to 31.74\% and surpasses its Qwen3-32B teacher on ALFWorld Seen. Further analyses reveal that State-OPD achieves higher task success with fewer supervised turn responses and substantially reduces persistent failed-action repetition; qualitative traces illustrate decision revision based on interaction feedback.

Tabular Foundation Models Are Effectively Shallow

Irene Cannistraci, Julia E. Vogt

cs.LG, cs.AI

In-context-learning Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs) are uniquely suited to scenarios with limited resources or privacy constraints, as they solve new tasks without additional training. However, their architecture consists of multiple transformer blocks, resulting in high computational and memory footprints. Meanwhile, traditional alternatives like Gradient-Boosted Decision Trees (GBDTs) remain highly competitive and run efficiently on CPUs, suggesting that tabular tasks may not inherently require deep architectures. This contrast raises a fundamental question: can TFMs be simplified by removing architectural redundancies without sacrificing performance? To answer this, we introduce a closed-form, label-free affine translator that replaces a contiguous window of up to 96% of the blocks, leaving the rest unchanged. Evaluating eight frontier TFMs on 171 classification datasets, we reveal that tabular transformers are vastly overparameterized: replacing half of a model's blocks costs at most $0.002$ median AUROC across all architectures, with the half-depth TabFM still outperforming every other full-depth TFMs and tuned GBDTs. Furthermore, different model families are overparameterized in different ways: TabPFN-3 relies on its encoders, maintaining performance regardless of which single block is retained. Other single-axis models spread computation thinly, needing only their first block, while dual-axis models concentrate it in one critical block. Ultimately, removing depth saves up to 1.5B parameters, speeds up GPU inference by up to $6\times$ at a median cost of $0.03$ AUROC, while a single-block TabPFN-3 serves cached queries $2.4\times$ faster using $6\times$ less memory at negligible cost.

CAST: Credit Alignment for Cross-Tokenizer On-Policy Distillation

Jingru Yang, Jinhang Wu, Minjing Shi, Shengfeng He, Xuyan Li, Man Tan, Biye Li

cs.LG, cs.CL

Cross-tokenizer on-policy distillation (OPD) requires aligning teacher and student sequences before their scores can be compared. Decoded-text alignment provides such correspondence at the chunk level, but leaves a distinct problem unresolved: credit alignment. A chunk-level signal specifies how much a span should change, but not how that signal should be distributed across student tokens, while tokens without teacher correspondence remain unconstrained. We propose CAST (Chunk-aware credit Assignment with Selective Token Anchoring), a shift-or-stay framework that addresses both issues. For aligned chunks, CAST redistributes each chunk's advantage across student tokens using predictive uncertainty and local teacher-signal smoothness, emphasizing learning opportunities supported by locally consistent teacher feedback while preserving the chunk's total advantage. The resulting weights exactly solve a KL-regularized credit-allocation problem. For unaligned positions, CAST selectively anchors the student to its initial policy through a KL penalty, limiting drift where teacher supervision is unavailable. Because credit assignment is separated from chunk construction, CAST also applies to punctuation-defined or fixed-length chunks under shared tokenization. Across six mathematics, science, and code benchmarks, CAST improves the average over the strongest evaluated baseline by $2.45$ and $1.32$ points for cross-tokenizer transfer from Qwen3-8B to Mistral-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, respectively, and by $2.10$ points for shared-tokenizer transfer to Qwen3-1.7B.

Neural Surrogate Modeling of Ultrasonic Wave Propagation in Heterogeneous Media

Burla Nur Korkmaz, Petro Moilanen, Ari Salmi, Harsha Vardhan Tetali, Arto Klami

cs.LG

Neural surrogates provide computationally efficient auto-regressive modeling of wave propagation, enabling e.g. fast inverse solutions. Despite recent progress, the current surrogates are not yet sufficient for the needs of ultrasonic sensing, characterized by high frequency and heterogeneous propagation media. We show how methods building on Fourier Neural Operators (FNO) fail to capture essential signal features, such as reflections, whereas U-Net -based methods learn the wave fields well but lack the ability to transfer information across different spatial scales. We then propose a new surrogate that captures the best of the two worlds, by integrating FNOs and local-integral resampling into a U-Net architecture. We show it can be trained on limited data, and that the predictions are robust to input noise and work across spatial scales.

Data-Adaptive Mahalanobis Metric Learning for Cross-Head Attention in Transformers

Zhenke Duan, Xin Li, Jiqun Pan, Dong Xiaofei, Hanwen Ning, Xinyuan Song

cs.LG

In this paper, we propose Data-Adaptive Mahalanobis Cross-Head Attention (DAMCHA), a simple and effective attention mechanism that generalizes Multi-Head Attention (MHA) through the lens of metric learning. Standard MHA implicitly learns a static, head-wise Euclidean similarity corresponding to the diagonal blocks of a unified cross-head metric matrix, discarding all off-diagonal interactions across heads. DAMCHA addresses this limitation via two key innovations. First, it learns the full cross-head metric matrix, inducing a Mahalanobis-like similarity that explicitly captures inter-head interactions beyond the restricted block-diagonal structure of MHA. Second, it parameterizes the metric matrix as an input-dependent function, enabling the attention geometry to dynamically adapt to the intrinsic manifold structure of the data rather than remaining fixed after training. We further introduce principled regularization strategies, most notably stack-wise parameter sharing, to ensure computational efficiency and stable optimization. DAMCHA serves as a drop-in replacement for MHA and its variants in various Transformer architectures, delivering superior expressive power and learning performance with comparable or reduced computational overhead. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DAMCHA-based Transformers consistently outperform competing baselines across a range of benchmark tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/erwinmsmith/DAMCHA.

CopRoute: Dependence-Aware Conformal Routing for Language Models

Siva Rajesh Kasa, Santhosh Kumar Kasa, Karan Gupta

cs.LG, the, NeurIPS, 2026, Workshop, on, On-Device, Intelligence:, Foundation, Models, under, Real-World, Constraints, (ODI, 2026)

Set-based language model routing aims to increase the coverage of the event where at least one model answers a query correctly while controlling the number of inference calls made. Existing risk-controlled routing policies bound the all-miss risk (the probability that every model in the selected set fails) by calibrating a single conformal threshold and applying it to a nested family of model sets. Because each model is scored in isolation, these policies ignore the dependence structure of model failures. Models that share training data or architecture tend to fail on the same queries, so two individually strong models can be jointly redundant while a weaker but complementary model covers the queries that the others miss. To account for this dependence, which existing risk-controlled policies ignore, we propose CopRoute, which orders models by how many new queries each one answers correctly beyond those already selected, decides per query how far down that order to go, and then picks the smallest cutoff that still meets the target risk. We show that CopRoute's ordering attains, on the design data, the best guarantee any efficient fixed-ordering algorithm can give for the average number of calls to the first correct model, and that a separate calibration step certifies the all-miss risk of the deployed sets. On RouterBench and RouterEval, CopRoute attains the fewest certified calls on nine of ten datasets at a target risk of 0.05 and on eight of ten at 0.10, making up to 79 percent fewer calls (median 24 percent) than the state-of-the-art risk-controlled policy at the same certified all-miss risk.

MHR-Router: Multimodal Heterogeneous Retrieval-Enhanced Adaptive Routing for VLM Inference

Wenjuan Zhu, Zheming Yang, Yunqing Hu, Qi Guo

cs.LG

Vision-language models (VLMs) exhibit complementary capabilities, yet selecting the most suitable expert for a given image-question query remains challenging due to heterogeneous multimodal inputs and varying expert strengths. Existing routing approaches rely on text-based cues or static model profiles, failing to capture fine-grained visual evidence and expert-specific failure patterns. To address this, we propose MHR-Router, a multimodal heterogeneous retrieval-enhanced adaptive routing framework for VLM inference. It accurately dispatches each multimodal query to the most suitable VLM expert via local retrieval evidence. MHR-Router consists of two stages: (1) Multimodal Routing Representation, which fuses frozen multimodal features into a comprehensive query representation; and (2) Heterogeneous Retrieval-Enhanced Adaptive Routing, which retrieves expert-specific success and failure memories as local evidence and employs a shared scorer to adaptively select the optimal expert. Experiments show that MHR-Router effectively achieves heterogeneous modality routing and consistently improves accuracy over traditional baselines.

Recovering Irreducible Three-Body Interactions with Lagrangian Neural Networks

Sena Kalabalik, Sukru Caglar, Abdullah Umut Hamzaogullari, Arkadas Ozakin

cs.LG, physics.atom-ph, physics.comp-ph

Lagrangian Neural Networks (LNNs) learn mechanical systems from trajectory data by parameterizing a scalar Lagrangian and deriving the dynamics through the Euler-Lagrange equations, biasing the model toward motion that respects the variational structure of mechanics. The interactions studied so far have been pairwise-additive, leaving open whether LNNs can recover an irreducible many-body interaction from motion alone. We address this using the Axilrod-Teller triple-dipole interaction, the leading three-body dispersion term, whose dependence on the collective geometry of three particles cannot be represented by any sum of pairwise terms. We prescribe the kinetic energy exactly and learn only the potential, as a function of interparticle distances and angles. Combined with a signal-aware sampling strategy that concentrates training data where the three-body signal is strongest while preserving broad coverage of configuration space, this recovers the interaction from accelerations alone. The learned potential reproduces the radial scaling, angular dependence, and characteristic sign reversal of the Axilrod-Teller interaction, and generates stable trajectories. Embedding it within a pairwise Lennard-Jones potential lets us examine its observability: the three-body term is recovered once its dynamical signature exceeds the residual modeling error, which it does cleanly at amplified coupling but not at the coupling of a real noble gas, bracketing the transition between the two. Finally, symbolic regression on the trained potential, with no assumed functional form, returns the Axilrod-Teller expression with angular numerator, radial law, and coupling constant intact. An LNN with domain-specific structure can thus recover an irreducible three-body interaction from trajectories as a closed-form law, pointing toward learning interaction laws whose structure is not known a priori.

DAG Structure Learning with Node-Varying Heavy-Tailed Disturbances

Robin Francis, Sundeep Prabhakar Chepuri

cs.LG

DAG structures can be learned from observations by incorporating acyclicity via smooth constraints, such as DAGMA's log-determinant function. Typically, observations and the underlying DAG are related through linear structural equation models, with Gaussianity assumption on the disturbances at each node. Extending DAG learning methods that account for scale and tail behavior of disturbances that vary across nodes remains a challenge. We propose a node-varying Student-$t$ DAGMA that jointly estimates the graph structure, node-specific disturbance scales, and the degrees-of-freedom parameter. A Gaussian scale-mixture representation yields an iteratively reweighted formulation that down-weights large standardized residuals. Building on existing results, we also establish identifiability of the graph and disturbance parameters. Synthetic experiments demonstrate improved structural recovery under heterogeneous heavy-tailed disturbances, and experiments on real finance data show that the proposed method yields lower test negative log-likelihood than the evaluated baselines.

MORAD: Reinforcement Learning with Multi-Objective Rewards for RNA Inverse Design

Jixin Tang, Ji Guo, Jun Zhao

cs.LG, q-bio.BM, Project, page:, https://gabrile166.github.io/MORAD/

We introduce MORAD, an online reinforcement learning framework for backbone-conditioned RNA diffusion models that trains a single shared policy across diverse target backbones. Structure-conditioned RNA inverse design seeks nucleotide sequences compatible with a target three-dimensional backbone, yet existing reinforcement learning approaches either optimize a separate policy for each target backbone or rely on offline preference optimization, leaving online post-training of a reusable backbone-conditioned model largely unexplored. MORAD combines backbone-conditioned group sampling, group-relative reward normalization, and a forward-process diffusion objective. Beyond existing RL rewards that primarily target tertiary structural quality, MORAD jointly optimizes six measurements spanning tertiary geometry, base pairing, ensemble behavior, and sequence composition. To aggregate these heterogeneous objectives despite differences in scale and optimization direction, MORAD maps each measurement to a bounded desirability score and combines them with a weighted geometric mean. Compared with a tertiary-structure-only reward, this multi-objective formulation improves 10 of the 11 evaluation metrics that have a preferred direction. Applied to pretrained RIDE, MORAD improves GDT-TS by 16.0% (from 0.3300 to 0.3827) and pairing MCC by 19.4% (from 0.6110 to 0.7295), while reducing normalized ensemble defect by 27.5% (from 0.3906 to 0.2830). On the test set, MORAD outperforms RiboDiffusion, gRNAde, and RDesign across tertiary structural accuracy, secondary-structure pairing, and thermodynamic quality, establishing a new state of the art in structure-conditioned RNA inverse design.

Structure-Aware Numerical Stabilization for Half-Precision Proximal Policy Optimization

Linxuan Wu, Liang Chen

cs.LG

Local online training on resource-constrained platforms requires low computational and training-state overhead. We study the numerical stability and computational efficiency of half-precision proximal policy optimization (PPO) on commodity GPUs. Directly converting PPO training to FP16 is unreliable because numerical errors feed back through policy updates and subsequent data collection, while PPO repeatedly compares a current policy with a fixed behavior policy. We propose PPO-FP16 with three structure-aware modules. Global scalar protection stabilizes statistics shared across a minibatch or parameter update. Policy-change residual protection extracts and preserves small differences between the current and behavior policies. Efficient persistent-update protection stabilizes optimizer state and parameter writeback while reducing state storage. These changes preserve the PPO objective, hyperparameters, and training budget. Across six continuous-control tasks and five seeds, PPO-FP16 achieves the lowest mean return-AUC rank (1.90) among the four methods and ranks first in 17 of 30 environment-seed comparisons. Eight of nine performance configurations yield PPO update speedups ranging from 1.16 to 2.03 times. Relative to Full FP32 Adam, optimizer-state memory is reduced by 71.7 to 74.9 percent, and peak allocated GPU memory is reduced by up to 44.5 percent. The results show that protecting a small set of structurally important quantities can deliver stable, efficient FP16 PPO without retuning.

Sustainable Cloud-Fog-Edge Systems for Smart Buildings: Algorithmic Adaptation under Memory Constraints and Dynamic Orchestration Strategies

Mamadou Sow, Christophe Cerin

cs.LG, cs.DC, cs.NA, math.NA

The escalating environmental footprint of the Information and Communication Technol- ogy (ICT) sectorwith global Data Center electric- ity usage potentially reaching 3% by 2030 and the imperative for real-time responsiveness in smart buildings demand a foundational shift towards Edge Computing. This article proposes a holis- tic architectural and algorithmic approach for cre- ating autonomous, resilient, and privacy-preserving learning systems within smart buildings, advancing the core concept of the building itself as the data center. Our contributions, demonstrating originality compared to conventional centralized systems, are structured around three main pillars: 1) Validation of resilient data acquisition chains using the af- fordable Arduino ecosystem (Uno R4 WiFi/BME280) and secure persistence solutions including centralized archiving (Node-RED/MySQL) and secure remote transfer (Python/rsync/SSH). 2). The proposal of a lightweight, KOptim orchestration framework, inter- operable with the FIWARE standard, for the dynamic management of Service Level Agreements (SLA). 3) The adaptation and experimental valida- tion of the Generalized Hebbian Algorithm (GHA) for Principal Component Analysis (PCA) on low- power microcontrollers,We introduce and compare an iterative learning strategy using data buffers, featuring sorting and explicit preservation of extreme samples to maintain diversity. Experimental results on the high-dimensional Tour Perret sensor data confirm that this novel method, despite introducing a necessary temporal complexity of O(W log W ) due to sorting, achieves convergence and final result quality that is visually equivalent to processing the entire dataset in full memory, reliably identifying two distinct operational states.

SBNO : Schr\"odinger Bridge Neural Operator for the Forward and Inverse Problems under Missing Information

Jaehyeon Park, Inhyeok Jeong, Noseong Park

cs.LG

Partial differential equation (PDE) surrogate modeling often relies on either dense observations or access to governing equations/physics constraints. In practice, observations can be sparse and partial, and the underlying physics may be partially specified or unavailable. We introduce SBNO, an equation-free paired-data diffusion bridge matching framework that learns conditional operators for both forward and inverse PDE problems under partial observations. SBNO uses diffusion Schr\"odinger bridge matching to learn a pair of time-dependent drifts from a mask-aware endpoint coupling that preserves empirical input-solution pairing during training and supports both PDE-task directions within a single framework. At inference time, SBNO produces a deterministic point reconstruction by integrating the probability-flow ODE constructed from the learned drift pair, without inference-time observation or PDE guidance-weight tuning. On five PDE benchmarks with 5% observations, SBNO achieves superior or comparable accuracy to baselines while demonstrating lower test-instance error dispersion. Furthermore, SBNO is at least $9.7 \times$ faster and reduces GPU memory by at least $4.3 \times$ compared to guided-diffusion baselines.

DuoRate: Two-Rate Per-Expert Precision Allocation for Mixture-of-Experts Serving

Mengqi Guo, Zhe Wei, Yuan Yuan, Jiunn Bin Lim, Boyi Pan, Michael Bi Mi

cs.LG

Serving a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model pays for expert precision twice: the resident footprint pays for every expert the router can reach, while each batch-one decode step pays only for the experts it selects. These are two different rate functionals over the same per-expert widths, yet published per-expert MoE allocators constrain only the first. We call the difference the store-read gap; in the released BitsMoE checkpoint, 2.54 bits per weight (bpw) of storage become 7.50 bpw of decode traffic. We propose DuoRate, which stores once and reads per workload. Offline, it stores one uniform tier of a prefix-readable weight stream and bakes a distortion tensor. Online, it profiles a workload's own prefill without labels and solves a Lagrangian rate-distortion allocation under the decode functional; routing frequency provably cancels from every per-expert decision, and the solve costs one dot product per expert and a bisection. A deployment therefore keeps a single artifact that prefill reads at the stored rate and decode reads at the workload's own rate. On Qwen3-30B-A3B, DuoRate reading 2.3 bpw exceeds every 2-bit-class baseline by 6.3 to 47.0 points of average accuracy, and at 2.9 bpw it matches 4-bit formats that read 1.4-1.6 bpw more. The advantage is largest under long decoding: on GSM8K, DuoRate at 2.9 bpw matches or exceeds every 4-bit format and trails BF16 by only 0.25 points, and at 2.6 bpw it still matches or exceeds the 4.5-bit NF4 and FP4. Against controls in its own format, the allocation alone is worth 0.07-0.11 bpw of read rate. Code will be released upon publication.

PDE-PFN: Prior-Data Fitted Neural PDE Solver

Jaehyeon Park, Mingu Kang, Dongseok Lee, Woojin Cho, Kookjin Lee, Anthony Gruber, Youngjoon Hong, Noseong Park

cs.LG

Motivated by the success of large language models (LLMs) with broad generalizability and robustness to noisy or unreliable pre-training data, we explore in-context prediction of PDE solution fields using approximate pre-training data. In addition, inspired by the posterior predictive mean-based inference mechanism of the in-context learning in prior-data fitted networks (PFNs), we propose PDE-PFN, a prior-data fitted neural solver that approximates the posterior predictive mean of PDE solutions via in-context learning. PDE-PFN builds on a PFN architecture with self- and cross-attention mechanisms of Transformer and is pre-trained on noisy approximate solutions generated by physics-informed neural networks, serving as diverse but not necessarily exact priors. Through experiments on a range of two-dimensional PDEs, we demonstrate that PDE-PFN achieves empirical generalization across heterogeneous equations, robustness under noisy priors, and zero-gradient-update in-context inference capability. PDE-PFN achieves competitive or improved predictive accuracy relative to the evaluated baselines while supporting flexible context and query representations.

AgentTune: A Unified Training Lifecycle for Tool-Using LLM Agents

R E Zera Marveen Lyngkhoi, Abhivansh Gupta, Vidushee Vats, Ram Mohan Rao Kadiyala, Vinay Kumar Sankarapu, Pratinav Seth

cs.LG

Tool-using agents increasingly require a common substrate for building, training, evaluating, distilling, and maintaining agentic systems, yet existing tooling typically addresses these stages in isolation. We present AgentTune, a source-available Python library that provides a unified framework for developing and optimizing tool-using agents. At its core, AgentTune introduces a two-tier EventLog representation that preserves agent trajectories while supporting consistent projections for training, evaluation, and auditing. Building on this substrate, the library provides a shared agent runtime, tool-calling reinforcement learning, agentic distillation, retrieval-augmented generation, decision workflows, evaluation utilities, and a designed path toward self-healing agents. These components are exposed through composable interfaces so that agent strategies, training methods, evaluation procedures, and recovery mechanisms can be combined without requiring separate representations or pipelines. We demonstrate the framework across agentic tasks including legal contract search and domain-specific applications, illustrating its use for trajectory-based optimization, tool-use evaluation, and cross-task agent development. AgentTune is released as a source-available library at https://github.com/Lexsi-Labs/AgentTune

Understanding Mamba-3 Through Oscillatory Dynamics

Shutian Liang, Yue Song

cs.LG

Mamba-3 introduces input-dependent rotations into recurrent state updates, but their contribution beyond state retention remains unclear. We interpret these ro- tations as underdamped second-order dynamics, providing a framework for in- vestigating the role of oscillation in sequence modeling. Controlled comparisons show that underdamped dynamics outperform non-oscillatory damping regimes on state-tracking tasks, while language-model pretraining demonstrates benefits for long-context prediction and length generalization. Interventions in pretrained models further establish the importance of learned phase, and state analysis sug- gests that rotation regulates how retained information combines with incoming up- dates. Taken together, these findings identify underdamped dynamics as a useful mechanism for recurrent computation and suggest that oscillation supports long- context processing by regulating how information accumulates.

Hidden Data Challenges in Clinical Machine Learning

Carolin Ciss\'ee, Dennis Fast, Raneen Younis, Alexander L\"oser, Wolfgang Nejdl, Zahra Ahmadi

cs.LG

Widely reused preprocessing pipelines for electronic health records can encode assumptions about when information became available, what clinical episode a label represents, and what missingness means. These assumptions are rarely audited systematically and may propagate across subsequent studies when preprocessing pipelines are reused or adapted. Using MIMIC-IV as a case study, we identify and quantify violations of three complementary forms of preprocessing alignment, i.e., temporal, semantic, and population alignment, which manifest as temporal leakage, feature-task misalignment, and biased imputation. We show how ambiguities in timestamp semantics, episode definitions, endpoint construction, modality selection, and missingness handling can alter the information available to a model and affect the interpretation of its predictions. Through analyses of MIMIC-IV data and empirical case studies, we demonstrate that such choices may remain largely invisible in aggregate predictive performance while changing the signals on which models rely. We derive practical recommendations for temporally valid, clinically aligned, and reproducible preprocessing of MIMIC-IV and related electronic health record datasets.
