In this work, we address this gap by performing a systematic re-evaluation of regression objectives across a broad collection of established tabular datasets and multiple neural backbones. Our comparison reveals that many proposed objectives provide limited or inconsistent gains over a properly tuned MSE baseline, while quantile loss yields consistent aggregate improvements over MSE across the tested backbones. We then identify a limitation that remains even with this strong objective: regions with different levels of target noise can reach their best predictive performance at different training epochs. Consequently, a single global early-stopping checkpoint can impose a compromise between uncertainty regions, even when the objective can represent heteroskedastic target distributions. Motivated by this limitation, we introduce a heteroskedasticity-aware extension of quantile loss that uses input-dependent label smoothing as a potential remedy and improves over quantile loss on several backbones. Overall, our results provide a modern empirical picture of regression objectives for tabular deep learning and highlight how differences in optimal stopping times across uncertainty regions can limit the effectiveness of global early stopping. The code is available at https://github.com/yandex-research/tabular-regression.

Designing Protein Structures from Natural Language

Joseph Lee, Yanjing Li, Bo Qiang, Gregory Lau, Allyson Ettinger, Noah A. Smith, Rory Donovan-Maiye, David Baker, Hanna Hajishirzi, Pang Wei Koh

cs.LG, q-bio.BM, https://github.com/jleechung/prompteina

A central challenge in de novo protein design is generating proteins with desired structural and functional properties. Existing design models typically require a dedicated input representation for each property, limiting the design objectives they support. We develop Prompteina, the first all-atom structure generation model that supports new and broader design objectives specified in natural language, analogous to text-to-image generation. To train Prompteina, we construct Prompteina-Data, a paired text--protein structure dataset of 1.8M structures and 50M+ captions describing global and residue-specific structural and functional properties. We adapt Proteina-Complexa, a pretrained structure generation model, through text-conditioned pretraining and finetuning that aligns residues with their corresponding text. Prompteina outperforms baselines on 15 of 18 structural properties and generates proteins that more closely match descriptions of their intended functions. In binder design, it leads baselines on at least 13 of 20 targets and improves interface confidence by 14\% over the strongest baseline. With iterative in silico feedback, Prompteina's gain over non-iterative generation is 57\% larger than the next-best baseline on the hardest targets. Finally, we experimentally validate Prompteina designs against GM2A, a target with no reported de novo binders. We identify nine binders, with the strongest at an apparent $K_{\mathrm{D}}$ of 107 nM. These results establish natural language as an effective and powerful conditioning interface for protein structure generation.

KL Before and After Mixing: A Round-Complexity Separation in Parallel Sampling

Tianyi Wu

cs.LG, cs.IT, math.IT, appendices;, 7, figures., Formal, proof, materials, included, as, ancillary, files

A parallel sampler can choose at random which coordinates to generate together. For a target $p$ and the output law $q_U$ of a schedule $U$ drawn afresh for each output, we compare the schedule-averaged divergence $\mathbb{E}_U D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p\|q_U)$ with the output-law divergence $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p\|\mathbb{E}_U q_U)$, in nats. Rounds commit independent draws from exact conditional marginals frozen at the round's start, each output's schedule is drawn before sampling from a finite mixture of schedules, and every scheduled round is charged. For the uniform distribution on binary strings of even length $n$ with $n/2$ ones, the least expected number of rounds at a fixed tolerance $\varepsilon>0$ is $\Theta_\varepsilon((\log n)^2)$ under the first criterion but $(e^{-\varepsilon}+o(1))\log_2\ln n$ under the second, and the double-logarithmic order is attainable with the same round count on every run. For this target, a count-state recursion gives the exact least output divergence at every round cap over all causal randomized policies. The separation extends to weighted fixed-count targets with bounded weight ratios and quota fractions bounded away from zero and one, without permutation symmetry, and to the full-support Beta$(1,1)$--Bernoulli target, where the sharp expected cost under the output-law criterion is $(e^{-\varepsilon}/2+o(1))\log_2\log_2n$, attained with rare $n$-round runs. Prior work implies the order of the schedule-averaged cost on the balanced target and its $O_\varepsilon((\log n)^2)$ upper bounds on both extensions.

AI Models Can Provably Hide Arbitrary Capabilities

Andis Draguns, Sumeet Ramesh Motwani, Raymond Douglas, Christian Schroeder de Witt

cs.LG, cs.CR

Capability evaluations aim to surface dangerous model behaviors before deployment, but their reliability depends on the assumption that hidden capabilities can be elicited. We show this assumption does not hold under adversarial conditions by constructing backdoor attacks that embed encrypted neural networks within host model weights, decrypting and executing them only upon receiving a secret trigger. Using digital lockers, these hidden circuits remain provably hard to elicit and interpret under cryptographic assumptions, even given full access to model weights, extending prior work from hiding fixed strings to arbitrary computations. We implement our proof of concept constructions in PyTorch and evaluate elicitation through fine-tuning, weight noising, gradient-based trigger search, and latent adversarial training on a small chemical reaction prediction task - a proxy for CBRN-relevant capabilities. Our results demonstrate resistance to these methods and, together with our cryptographic guarantees, reveal a limit of elicitation-based capability audits. We release our models to support the improvement of defenses.

SoftwareGym: Scaling Computer-Use Agent Training with Diverse Synthesized Software Environments

Tian Jin, Wenhao Wang, Jing Kang, Haoting Shi, Rui Ye, Yijun Lu, Siheng Chen

cs.MA, cs.SE

Computer-use agents (CUAs) are rapidly improving, yet synthesizing diverse and reliable software environments at scale remains challenging. Native application environments require substantial construction and integration effort, which limits application coverage and increases the risk of application-specific overfitting. Browser-hosted environments provide limited interaction depth and workflow complexity. These limitations motivate SoftwareGym, an automated pipeline for synthesizing GUI software environments with diverse interfaces, executable functionality, and runtime-verified behavior. It generates structurally diverse designs, realizes them as runnable applications, and uses GUI evaluation with iterative repair to ensure functional reliability. Using SoftwareGym, we synthesize 182 applications across twelve categories and achieve a mean GUI task pass rate of 91.5%. We construct SoftwareGym-Bench, a 120-task evaluation suite spanning the same twelve categories, and use trajectories collected from these environments for CUA training. The resulting model improves Pass@3 from 41.39% to 56.97% on the strictly out-of-domain OSWorld benchmark and achieves 40.0% on SoftwareGym-Bench. These results demonstrate the potential of synthesized software environments as scalable and transferable supervision for CUA training.

How Do Hardware Faults Manifest Differently in LLM Agents?

Aiden Rubey, Zachary Coalson, Cong Chen, Jean-Philippe Monteuuis, Jonathan Petit, Sanghyun Hong

cs.MA, first, two, authors, contributed, equally

We present the first systematic study of the resilience of large language model (LLM) agents to random hardware-induced faults. Prior work on hardware fault resilience in LLMs focuses on model-level outcomes, such as incorrect outputs or generation failures. Yet LLM agents repeatedly interact with tools and environments, creating loops that can change how hardware faults manifest. We conduct an extensive evaluation across three agent benchmarks, comprising 6,814 agent executions: 2,400+ clean runs and 4,300+ fault-injected runs with independently sampled random two-bit weight faults. We trace how these faults propagate through agent execution. Unlike prior studies, we find that agentic execution transforms model-level corruption into distinct system-level outcomes: (1) agents can recover from faulty model outputs and still complete tasks correctly; (2) this recovery increases computational and execution costs; (3) a few faults cause subtle but semantically meaningful errors while the agent otherwise behaves normally; and (4) for some models and scenarios, faults can alter security and alignment behavior.

SwingGuard: Risk-Conditioned Multi-Agent Swing Trading with Dual-Timescale Governance

Junzhou Su, Yichen Luo, Yebo Feng, Teng Li, Jiahua Xu

cs.MA

Cryptocurrency swing trading spans mismatched timescales: positions remain active while market and event risk can change within minutes. However, many predictive and large language model (LLM)-based trading systems assess risk after strategy generation or run both functions at the same cadence, leaving proposals unconditioned by current risk and exposure weakly governed between revisions. We propose SwingGuard, a dual-timescale, long-only multi-agent framework for risk-conditioned strategy generation. At the strategy timescale, specialized analysts synthesize point-in-time technical, sentiment, on-chain, and macroeconomic evidence through bullish-bearish deliberation. The resulting evidence and current risk context are then combined by a Portfolio Architect to form a target position. At the governance timescale, a five-minute Risk Controller performs conditional diagnosis, maintains persistent risk states with staged recovery, audits the proposal, and constrains execution until the next strategy revision. This design allows risk to shape the feasible strategy before proposal finalization while continuing to govern the active position afterward. Evaluations on Bitcoin (BTC) and Ether (ETH) show that SwingGuard preserves positive returns after transaction costs while maintaining shallow drawdowns and strong risk-adjusted performance on both assets. The exposure-capped joint portfolio achieves a Sharpe ratio of 2.98, compared with 2.79 for an otherwise identical Post-hoc Risk variant. Structured traces further link risk evidence and state transitions to strategy constraints and executed position changes, providing an auditable framework for asynchronous cryptocurrency swing trading. Overall, the results indicate that conditioning strategy formation on current risk, together with persistent high-frequency governance, improves risk-adjusted performance without sacrificing auditability.

The Limits of Manipulation within Personalized Incentive Policies

Andrea Araldo, Alessa Mayer, Ravi Seshadri, Amir Brudner

cs.MA

Demand management policies aim to shift individual behavior toward sustainable choices (e.g., goods, transport modes). Personalized incentive policies tailor subsidies to agents' needs and constraints, but personalization also exposes the regulator to agents' strategic manipulative behavior. The extent to which agents can profitably manipulate such policies remains poorly understood, which limits their applicability. This paper establishes analytically the conditions under which manipulation benefits the agent. We formalize a Stackelberg game in which an agent may simulate a different profile to extract higher incentives from a regulator. Previous work typically assumes that the regulator would instantaneously classify agents into the simulated user profile. In contrast, we explicitly model the regulator's process of learning the agent profile, based on observed past behavior. This makes manipulation harder, since it forces the agent to consistently behave according to a simulated profile, thus renouncing following their true preferences. Manipulative agents thus incur a Cost of Manipulation, which has typically been neglected. By accounting for it, we get a counterintuitive finding: although classification uncertainty reduces personalization accuracy, it may benefit the regulator by deterring manipulation. We establish an upper bound on the agent's gain from manipulation, which becomes stricter as profiles become harder to distinguish for the regulator. Simulations on the London Passenger Mode Choice dataset illustrate this deterrence effect. The framework can therefore be used to stress-test the robustness of personalized incentive policies before deployment.

When AI Advisors Agree: Distinguishing Forecast Risk from Forecast Reliability

Russlan Ramdowar

cs.MA, https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23262216., Code, and, documentation:, https://github.com/TheFutureEdge/research-when-ai-advisors-agree

If seven AI advisors give similar forecasts, should we trust them more? Agreement can indicate useful shared evidence, but it can also reflect an asset that was easier to forecast in the first place. We examine this distinction using 928 completed first-quarter forecasts for 232 assets across four production batches of iPulse AI. Ten historical Gemini configurations are grouped into seven investment frameworks; their archived forecasts are compared with later market outcomes, without generating new reports. Mean absolute return error rises from 10.31 percentage points in the lowest-disagreement quartile to 21.05 in the highest. Yet a forecast of no price change shows almost the same increase, from 9.89 to 21.15. The relationship between disagreement and additional forecast error is not resolved after accounting for visible asset risk. Retaining the quarter of forecasts with lowest historical volatility produces lower error than retaining those with least disagreement: 7.97 versus 10.31 percentage points. Agreement about direction behaves differently. When at least six of seven frameworks share a direction, directional accuracy is higher, but return-magnitude error is also larger. These results distinguish three questions often collapsed into one confidence score: how risky the asset is, whether advisors agree on its direction, and how precisely they forecast its return. For this historical council, agreement is informative in specific ways; it is not a general certificate of forecast reliability.

Collective Autonomous Vehicle Routing Needs Social Awareness for Coexistence with Human Drivers

Anastasia Psarou, Mikolaj Rams, Blazej Torbus, Rafal Kucharski

cs.MA, cs.AI

Collective Autonomous Vehicle (AV) route choices in real-world traffic networks can create externalities for Human-Driven Vehicles (HDVs), as routes that benefit AVs may increase travel times for other road users. To address this, we introduce a social component, the marginal cost, to the selfish travel time AV reward. Marginal cost captures an AV's impact on other road users by comparing their travel times with those in a counterfactual simulation in which the AV is removed. We study AV route choice in mixed-autonomy traffic settings using Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL), where AVs learn routing policies through repeated interactions with the traffic environment. Experiments on the real-world networks of Ingolstadt and Provins show that incorporating marginal cost into the AV reward not only limits the increase in HDV travel times and shortens AV travel times relative to selfish AV routing, but also improves overall system performance. In Ingolstadt, selfish routing of 40\% AVs increases the travel times of other vehicles by 1.7\%, and this drops to 1.1\% when the social reward is included. Interestingly, not only does overall system performance improve, but the AVs are also better off when acting socially.This effect is consistent across tested cities, penetration rates, and replications, yet when the weight of the social component in the reward increases, the system fails to reach the desired performance. For a more scalable approach to social AV routing in mixed-autonomy traffic, we compute the marginal cost only for the AVs whose route choices most affect the traffic system. This reduces the number of counterfactual simulations and achieves similar performance at a significantly lower computational cost. Our results suggest that AV routing should go beyond selfish rewards and, for the benefit of all traffic participants, include a social component in its objective.

Optimizers that reduce these calls face a dilemma: cascades that trust a cheap proxy miss their precision and recall targets on many queries, methods with finite-sample guarantees save little, and all of them discard the oracle's labels when the query ends. We present SemViews, a certified semantic filter that orders tuples by signals that cost no oracle calls (the labels that earlier queries left behind as semantic views, a local embedding, and a helper model's score), refines that order with labels it must pay for anyway, and certifies its answer with fresh stratified oracle samples. Common generative methods, such as diffusion and flow, typically learn fine-grained local dynamics whose integration defines this global mapping, hence requiring many network evaluations at inference. Existing few-step sampling approaches can lower computational burden by approximating large transformations from local dynamics, albeit at the expense of reconstruction fidelity. FLEX-MRI instead partitions recovery into a small number of finite intervals between retained states defined by progressive changes in k-space support, and directly learns the reconstruction change across each interval. Because such finite transitions can admit multiple plausible outcomes, FLEX-MRI combines support-relaxed finite-transition learning with stochastic exploration. During training, randomized k-space sampling masks define finite-transition targets with varying support, while the prediction loss remains unrestricted by each individual mask. At inference, FLEX-MRI perturbs a random subset of currently estimated k-space coefficients, allowing the learned predictor to stochastically explore alternative transition paths. Each predicted state is then reconciled with the acquired measurements through data consistency. Their generation therefore involves two distinct problems. First, every output must remain on the correlation elliptope, irrespective of how its variables are ordered. Second, the generator must learn the domain-specific distribution occupied by empirical data inside that valid space. Existing financial image generators mix these problems: they learn ordered matrix entries and repair invalid draws afterward. We introduce CorrFlow, which separates them. A generalized Fisher transform maps full-rank correlation matrices bijectively to unconstrained coordinates, guaranteeing valid outputs without projection. A graph flow-matching network learns the empirical distribution in those coordinates and is permutation equivariant: reordering the variables only reorders the output. We compare a conditional CorrGAN reimplementation, image diffusion, and CorrFlow on $80\times80$ Taiwan equity matrices using five seeds per neural method. CorrFlow produces 100% valid draws without repair and obtains feature sliced-Wasserstein distance $.712\pm.141$, versus $4.339\pm.252$ for CorrGAN and $4.610\pm.191$ for image diffusion after their invalid outputs are projected back to the correlation space.
